# Full critic findings, section by section

Eleven adversarial critics read v0.11.0 against your April 2024 draft and were told to rip it apart. This is everything they returned, unedited except for formatting. Quotes are verbatim from v0.11.0 and name their speaker. The synthesis is in CRITIQUE.md.

## Introduction: The Uncompromising Truth

**Verdict.** This is not an introduction. It is three documents stapled together: a confessional AGI forecast, a food-security policy brief stacked with Oxfam, CFPB, USDA and court citations, and a 600-word manual for the book's own apparatus. The best lines in the front matter are here and they are buried: the thesis question lands at word 1,117 of the book, the title line at 1,441, and "That's the whole book" arrives with about 1,700 words of Introduction still to go. It never says what the Singularity is, it contradicts itself on historical comparisons, and its AI-slop paragraph sneers at the same artists Chapter 0 grieves for. By block tally, about 1,800 of its 3,757 words are restatement, hedging, signposting, images and captions, or evidence that never touches AI.

**Publishable as is?** No. As the first section a reader meets, it would lose the newcomer before Chapter 0 and hand a hostile reviewer three quotable self-contradictions: b0087 against b0124 and b0151; b0124 against Chapter 0 (sts.chapter0.b0018) and Chapter 10 (sts.chapter10.b0128); b0132 against b0143 and b0162. The blocks that must survive (b0050, b0096, b0089, b0097, b0098, b0100, b0101, b0127, b0140, b0141, b0162, b0148, b0149, b0150, b0082, b0086, b0088, b0151) come to under 900 words.

### Failure modes

- **critical**: The Introduction never says what the Singularity is and never spells out or defines AGI, yet the reader is asked to accept a dated AGI forecast within the first 300 words. The word Singularity appears in the whole 9,320-word front matter only in the title phrase in b0163 and in the Part I heading. CT's 2024 draft defined it in two sentences ("You’ve heard of the Singularity, the point at which technology changes faster than we can keep up with. It’s where our tools leap from reacting to our input to proactively acting on their own."). The current book dropped the definition and kept the forecast. This breaks the brief to spin a newcomer up from zero. (sts.introduction.b0083, sts.introduction.b0088, sts.introduction.b0153, sts.introduction.b0163) Quote (CT, author voice): "Not because I was unsure of my own math, but because as long as the industry dismissed AGI as fringe, sci-fi nonsense, nobody would listen to a guy like me saying it out loud."
- **critical**: Self-contradiction on historical comparison. The Introduction says that pointing a scared worker at the printing press is a failure to listen. It then spends 600 words selling a 24-entry Precedent Ledger built from exactly those comparisons, uses the printing press itself against "AI slop" commenters, and advertises the abbot and the printed book as a coming attraction. A hostile podcast host will read b0087 and b0151 back to back on air. (sts.introduction.b0087, sts.introduction.b0124, sts.introduction.b0151, sts.introduction.b0152) Quote (CT, author voice): "Telling a frightened worker to be excited about productivity is a failure to listen. So is telling them that people once feared the printing press. A comparison with a dead person doesn't pay a living person's electric bill."
- **critical**: The AI-slop paragraph is contempt aimed at a large slice of the likely audience, and the book contradicts it twice. Chapter 0 shows the real grievance with sympathy: Claypot's models drew "in any style you asked for, including styles he recognized, styles with names attached, people attached, rent payments attached" and "The quiet part was in the training data" (sts.chapter0.b0018). Chapter 10 treats slop as a real quality problem with "a real feedback loop with real math" (sts.chapter10.b0128). The Introduction says the complaint is mostly wounded status. The one-line concession about "reliability, privacy, consent, and power" is a fig leaf. The same section also runs an image captioned as an "AI-adapted cutout" (b0161), so artists will say the book calls their objection vanity while it illustrates itself with AI. (sts.introduction.b0124, sts.introduction.b0125, sts.introduction.b0161) Quote (CT, author voice): "That's a person looking at a tool that could feed someone, teach someone, get a scared kid out of a job that treats a bathroom break as a privilege, and deciding the thing worth posting about is their own wounded status."
- **major**: Identity crisis: the Introduction pitches three different books. It opens as an AGI forecast book (b0088), turns into an anti-capitalist food-security brief with policy diction (b0103, b0114, b0142, b0143), and then promises a DIY builder's book (b0109). These pull against each other: b0109 says survival will be made "by machines a determined person could learn to build and repair", and b0114 says it "takes workers, growers, neighbors, public institutions, and businesses". CT's 2024 framing, "a primer on understanding and navigating the double exponential shift in the way we get our needs met", was personal and unified. Someone who searched for the singularity gets eviction law and a 2008 USDA chart by page three. (sts.introduction.b0088, sts.introduction.b0103, sts.introduction.b0109, sts.introduction.b0114, sts.introduction.b0142, sts.introduction.b0143, sts.introduction.b0144) Quote (CT, author voice): "That takes workers, growers, neighbors, public institutions, and businesses willing to supply a service on terms people can rely on."
- **major**: Buried lede and two openings. CT's real thesis question (b0050) comes at word 1,117 of the book and the title line (b0127) at 1,441. CT's own 2024 opening line, "So the robots have taken over. Congratulations! You've lost.", now opens the Preface at word 3,803, after this 3,757-word Introduction. The story the VOICE-GUIDE calls the strongest writing in the book starts at word 5,487. The Introduction also signs off twice before ending: b0084 says "That's where I want to begin." at word ~1,110, and b0078 says "before we begin" at word ~2,900. (sts.introduction.b0081, sts.introduction.b0084, sts.introduction.b0050, sts.introduction.b0127, sts.introduction.b0078) Quote (CT, author voice): "One more thing before we begin, because you're going to meet it at the end of every chapter."
- **major**: The forecast is hedged into a liability. AGI by Thanksgiving 2027 is stated twice (b0153, b0088), and the Introduction says four times that the forecast might be wrong or doesn't matter (b0153, b0088, b0163, b0132). Chapter 1 then does it all again. A skeptic's obvious reply: if being wrong changes nothing, why lead with the date? The section title promises "The Uncompromising Truth" (b0001), and the body compromises on nearly every claim it makes. (sts.introduction.b0001, sts.introduction.b0153, sts.introduction.b0088, sts.introduction.b0163, sts.introduction.b0132) Quote (CT, author voice): "We can begin now, with farmers, cooks, neighbors, useful machines, and public institutions. My forecast may be wrong. A dependable dinner still matters. The work is making survival less precarious for everyone."
- **major**: Hammer lines swung twice, and "the whole book" claimed three different ways. "Not abolished. Optional." appears in b0094 and again in b0147. "law of physics" appears in b0088 and b0149. "invent new tools" appears in b0083 and b0138. "enforced" carries b0142, b0146 and b0149. The book is "the whole book" in b0150, "the question this whole book turns on" in b0109, and "The two machines this whole book is about" in b0120. The VOICE-GUIDE says not to explain the hammer after you swing it; here the hammer gets swung again instead. (sts.introduction.b0094, sts.introduction.b0147, sts.introduction.b0088, sts.introduction.b0149, sts.introduction.b0083, sts.introduction.b0138, sts.introduction.b0109, sts.introduction.b0120, sts.introduction.b0150) Quote (CT, author voice): "Money should be optional. Not abolished. Optional."
- **major**: Statistics a hostile host will attack. Oxfam's 2017 eight-men figure rests on a net-wealth method that critics have contested for years. The 2026 Oxfam claim about political office will be read aloud as if it were absurd. Zuboff is cited to a Wikipedia page. The food-insecurity chart covers 2001 to 2024, centers on the 2008 recession, has nothing to do with AI, and its own caption disclaims causation. The argument's credibility ends up resting on its weakest sources. The figure alt text and caption (b0144, b0145) also set year ranges with en dashes, which breaks the book's no-dash rule. (sts.introduction.b0142, sts.introduction.b0143, sts.introduction.b0144, sts.introduction.b0145) Quote (caption, book apparatus (author voice)): "These household survey estimates show a historical pattern, not a causal test of job loss."
- **major**: The Introduction spoils Chapter 0 and defuses its last line. It summarizes Elijah's arc before the reader meets him, including the turn the Bear Flag scene is built to deliver, and it labels the whole frame fiction. Chapter 0 then ends by claiming Elijah's manuscript is the book in your hands (sts.chapter0.b0077). The Introduction's "I" (b0082: the crackpot whose loved ones gave him the look) shares Elijah's exact beat (sts.chapter0.b0031: the "good for you, buddy" look), so a first-timer cannot tell whether the narrator is CT or Elijah. (sts.introduction.b0130, sts.introduction.b0082) Quote (CT, author voice): "Their story follows a man who wants machines to make his friends more capable, helps set off something with very different consequences, and has to learn what responsibility looks like after the demonstration."
- **major**: Mixed and contradictory animal metaphors. The Introduction calls the machine an ox we domesticate (b0091). Two thousand words later the Preface's best story says "nobody owns a Mustang" (sts.preface.b0072). b0126 then stacks weather, receipts, stampede, choreography and herd in one paragraph. "The second ox" is a section heading, yet a search of every annotated section finds it only in the Introduction, so the metaphor carries nothing beyond this section. (sts.introduction.b0091, sts.introduction.b0099, sts.introduction.b0126) Quote (CT, author voice): "Not a god arriving to end us. An animal we're learning to domesticate, one that happens to think."
- **major**: Headings don't match their contents. "The Precedent Ledger" (b0123) heads three paragraphs about goalposts and AI slop before the Ledger is explained in b0151. "Give the machines a purpose worth having" (b0105) ends with a funding argument and then a non sequitur, "This is the roadmap. These are the nine stages.", attached to a figure the text never explains. "Roadmap" is also Chapter 18's title word, so the term names two different things. (sts.introduction.b0123, sts.introduction.b0105, sts.introduction.b0077, sts.introduction.b0121) Quote (CT, author voice): "This is the roadmap. These are the nine stages. And we're already inside the first one."
- **minor**: A dangling setup and a muddled target. "It's time. It's finally time." and "For years I wanted someone with credentials to say it first" imply that someone credentialed finally said it, and the reader is never told who. Two paragraphs later the reader is told to stop listening to "the guys in lab coats", so the Introduction both wants credentialed validation and tells the reader to reject credentials. (sts.introduction.b0081, sts.introduction.b0083, sts.introduction.b0086) Quote (CT, author voice): "For years I wanted someone with credentials to say it first."
- **minor**: A factual-argument slip that a skeptic will catch. The printing press is listed as a technology that changed what "hands and backs" could do. The press was an information technology, and the Preface's P-01 exists to argue exactly that (cheap novels changing minds). (sts.introduction.b0079) Quote (CT, author voice): "Every revolution before this one changed what our hands and backs could do: the plow, the press, the engine, the airplane."
- **minor**: The profanity budget is spent up front. Four of the book's strong words (asshole, goddamn, fucking, fuck) sit in this one section. The VOICE-GUIDE budget is "a handful of times in 85,000 words". This is CT's register (the 2024 draft opens Chapter 1 with "What the fuck is going on?"), so the problem is concentration, not use. Parents, librarians and the book clubs Appendix H targets meet all four on the first pages. (sts.introduction.b0082, sts.introduction.b0086, sts.introduction.b0050, sts.introduction.b0148)
- **minor**: Over-apparatus at the close: five signposts in the last 400 words. They explain the Ledger's four beats and its numbering, tell the reader to read every entry twice, map the three Parts, point to a one-page guide to reading, and point to the Preface's Ledger entry. (sts.introduction.b0152, sts.introduction.b0129, sts.introduction.b0154, sts.introduction.b0157, sts.introduction.b0134) Quote (CT, author voice): "There's a one-page guide to reading it, by the time you have and by who you are, right after the Preface."
- **minor**: Image plan works against the thesis. The section opens on a DARPA humanoid, shows the same Atlas robot again 600 words later, and captions the first with a line about data where no data appears. A book about dinner opens on a military robot photographed twice. (sts.introduction.b0002, sts.introduction.b0048, sts.introduction.b0160, sts.introduction.b0161) Quote (caption, book apparatus (author voice)): "The data does not care how you feel about it."

### What readers would complain about

- *newcomer*: "Bought a book about the Singularity. Four thousand words in, I still don't know what the Singularity is, but I know California's eviction timeline and Oxfam's billionaire count."
- *singularity regular*: "AGI by Thanksgiving 2027, with no definition of AGI and four disclaimers in one chapter. Either stake the claim or don't."
- *illustrator / working artist*: "He says my objection to AI slop is wounded ego, then illustrates his own book with an 'AI-adapted cutout.' The wise ones are learning to solder? The wise ones got scraped."
- *conservative skeptic*: "Tells me I don't need 'a particular politics' after three pages on billionaires, surveillance capitalism and sheriffs with guns. It's a pamphlet with a robot on the cover."
- *hostile podcast host*: "Page two: comparing AI to the printing press doesn't pay anyone's electric bill. Page five: here are twenty-four history lessons, starting with the printing press."
- *data-literate skeptic*: "The first chart in the book comes with a caption saying it proves nothing, and it's about 2008, not AI."
- *bored reader*: "It says 'That's the whole book' and then keeps going for another 1,700 words, ending with directions to a page about how to read the book."

### Weak writing

- `sts.introduction.b0079` "That's a price comparison at one measured performance level, not proof that every task got equally cheap." : A fact-checker's footnote pasted into the prose. It also uses the "not X" construction, and it lands right after the section's boldest claim ("We have automated thought itself") and deflates it.
- `sts.introduction.b0083` "Not a smarter search box. A tool that can invent new tools." : "Not X. Y." fragment pair, and the same idea is repeated in b0138 ("it can invent new tools").
- `sts.introduction.b0136` "I'm worried about two very specific things: missing our window to get this right, and what bad actors and sociopaths will do with it." : Promises specifics and delivers two vague worries. Neither is developed anywhere in the Introduction; the section turns to food instead.
- `sts.introduction.b0090` "You still have to train it, feed it, guide it, and care for it." : A list of four for rhythm, in a paragraph that cites the UN FAO to prove an ox can pull a plow. That is over-citation a reviewer will mock.
- `sts.introduction.b0094` "It's easier to imagine the end of the world than the end of capitalism, the line goes, and you can watch the reflex fire in real time." : A famous line (Jameson, popularized by Mark Fisher) used without credit; "the line goes" dodges the attribution. The 137-word paragraph also tries to carry four ideas at once.
- `sts.introduction.b0095` "What if we get capable of doing much more of the work that keeps us alive, and decide that being alive should get less frightening?" : Clumsy ("get capable", "should get less frightening"). It comes right after a sentence of pre-emptive hedging: "Not flawlessly. Not without arguments..."
- `sts.introduction.b0101` "When I criticize capitalism in this book, that's what I mean: productive capacity and human security can move in opposite directions." : Stacked abstract nouns (productive capacity, human security) and seminar diction where the argument most needs a body or a bill.
- `sts.introduction.b0102` "The leap from "a machine can do my work" to "my family might go hungry" is an indictment of that failure." : Lecture tone. "an indictment of that failure" restates b0101 in heavier words.
- `sts.introduction.b0137` "Here's what I actually believe, as plainly as I can say it." : Throat-clearing 1,400 words in. It implies the previous 1,400 words were neither plain nor actual belief.
- `sts.introduction.b0128` "AI helps cure our diseases, helps us learn and grow, helps us explore the universe, and helps us discover new ways of building new kinds of technology, better societies, and a better life for every individual person." : Padding: "helps" four times, "new ways of building new kinds of", and a list closing on "every individual person". The voice is CT's; the sentence needs half its words.
- `sts.introduction.b0110` "That question isn't a complaint. It's a design brief. The answer isn't to flee the world or bunker against it. It's to build the thing that makes the question obsolete, close to home, with your neighbors, starting now." : Two "isn't X. It's Y." constructions back to back. To be fair, CT's 2024 draft has the same tic ("This isn’t about tech support; it’s about revolutionizing curiosity."), so this is his habit amplified by revision, not purely an editing artifact.
- `sts.introduction.b0113` "The distance between a promising task and a finished benefit is where the work is, and we can be ambitious enough to cross it without pretending it isn't there." : Abstract and evenly hedged. "Promising task" and "finished benefit" are noun stacks with no picture behind them.
- `sts.introduction.b0114` "The question is how to arrange dependable provision, not how to make an invoice vanish." : Policy diction ("dependable provision") inside a "not X" construction. The paragraph also introduces the word "free" only so it can defend it, a word the Introduction never used before.
- `sts.introduction.b0138` "Let's get one thing straight: nothing like this has ever happened before. That's just true." : Cliché opener plus bare assertion. Ninety words later b0126 says "The moment isn't" unprecedented, which muddies the claim.
- `sts.introduction.b0080` "It's been said that the last thing humanity will ever do is move the goalposts." : Weasel attribution ("It's been said") wrapped around a cliché.
- `sts.introduction.b0126` "humanity has stood inside that weather before, over and over, and left receipts. The technology is new every time. The stampede never is. And a person who knows the stampede's choreography doesn't have to run with the herd." : Five metaphors in four sentences (weather, receipts, stampede, choreography, herd). The same paragraph also stacks a list of three ("The size, the shape, and the scale") and a list of four ("the panic, the denial, the goalpost-moving, the moral outcry").
- `sts.introduction.b0152` "because these are exhibits, not anecdotes" : "Not X" construction inside a paragraph that explains the book's own formatting to the reader.
- `sts.introduction.b0129` "History doesn't repeat, but it rhymes hard enough to set a plan to the meter." : A reworked cliché (the misattributed Twain line), next to an instruction nobody will follow: "Read each one twice."
- `sts.introduction.b0118` "If a robot can grow my food and build my house, why am I still renting my survival?" : A pull quote that repeats the question asked two sentences earlier in b0109, so the skimmer reads the same line twice. b0125 does the same thing to the end of b0124.

### Not in your voice

- `sts.introduction.b0163` "It means having a say in who owns the tools and who gets their benefit." : Mission-statement or grant-proposal register, added at v0.11.0 to define the title. CT's own 2024 definition was gut-level and personal: "a primer on understanding and navigating the double exponential shift in the way we get our needs met." This paragraph reads as written by committee.
- `sts.introduction.b0103` "I want adequate food available to everyone, regardless of income or employment. Enough of it, suitable for the person who needs it, in a form they can actually use." : "Adequate food" is UN right-to-food policy language. CT's 2024 voice never sounds like an NGO brief; it says things like "If humanity could get its collective head out of its collective ass, we could be living in the Star Trek future or the Jetsons."
- `sts.introduction.b0114` "Free means the person receiving the baseline doesn't have to produce money before they can eat." : Bureaucratic nouns ("the person receiving the baseline") pre-empting an objection nobody has raised yet. CT's original never argues defensively; it dares the reader.
- `sts.introduction.b0101` "When I criticize capitalism in this book, that's what I mean: productive capacity and human security can move in opposite directions." : In the 2024 draft CT's anti-capitalism was a joke with teeth ("there is always a period where Capitalism fights to the last breath and takes a lot of people with it"). Here it is a seminar definition.
- `sts.introduction.b0130` "The story also names real research and technologies; its imagined events aren't evidence for the arguments that follow each scene. Those arguments make their own claims, with sources where evidence is needed." : Legal or fact-check boilerplate. Nothing in CT's original reads like a disclaimer; this sounds like the factcheck harness speaking.
- `sts.introduction.b0079` "That's a price comparison at one measured performance level, not proof that every task got equally cheap." : CT's 2024 draft makes bold claims and moves on ("hint: we’re a LOT closer than you think"). Inline self-qualification like this appears all through the revised text and never in the original.
- `sts.introduction.b0113` "The distance between a promising task and a finished benefit is where the work is, and we can be ambitious enough to cross it without pretending it isn't there." : A balanced, hedged abstraction. CT's original shows the idea with a picture instead ("Your mom doesn't call you for tech help anymore; she's got Einstein on speed dial.").
- `sts.introduction.b0095` "Not flawlessly. Not without arguments, broken machines, bad decisions, or people who try to keep the best parts for themselves." : Pre-emptive apology before the vision has even been stated. The 2024 draft's vision paragraph ends "this is the single most exhilarating time to be alive in the history of humanity" and does not apologize first.

### Where a newcomer gets lost

- `sts.introduction.b0083`: AGI is never spelled out. The closest gloss is "a system that can understand its own architecture and help engineer the next version of itself", which is a description of recursive self-improvement, not of AGI.
- `sts.introduction.b0163`: The title term "the Singularity" is defined only as a mission ("keeping people fed, housed, and cared for"), never as a technological event. The reader must wait for Part I.
- `sts.introduction.b0079`: GPT-3.5 is named with no explanation. MMLU gets a three-word gloss. "Consumer hardware" and "server farm" assume background knowledge.
- `sts.introduction.b0094`: "it's all over" and "we're so back" are internet meme phrases a newcomer won't recognize as a pair.
- `sts.introduction.b0077`: "These are the nine stages" is the first time the stages appear. They are never listed or explained in text; the figure (b0121) is the only explanation, and its alt text introduces ASI without a definition.
- `sts.introduction.b0109`: "If a robot can fold laundry and walk a dog" points at recent humanoid demos the reader may never have seen. No product or source is named.
- `sts.introduction.b0081`: "It's time. It's finally time." has no antecedent. Time for what, and why now?

### Skim problems

- 87 blocks under 7 headings, and at least three headings mislabel their contents: "The second ox" ends on a capitalism argument (b0094), "Give the machines a purpose worth having" ends on the nine-stages roadmap (b0077), and "The Precedent Ledger" opens on goalposts and AI slop (b0138, b0080, b0124).
- Both pull quotes (b0118, b0125) repeat a sentence the skimmer's eye just passed.
- The longest blocks are the ones carrying claims: b0124 at 167 words, b0094 at 137, b0162 at 108, b0143 at 100. Skimmers skip exactly the paragraphs that need defending.
- The 'In this chapter' bullets (b0153) lead with the forecast and the Ledger's formatting. The thesis question (b0050) appears in no bullet, heading or pull quote.
- Someone reading only headings and bold text never learns what the Singularity is or what the nine stages are.
- Three images and their captions sit in the first 900 words, two of them the same robot, and the section carries six figures in all.

### Best blocks

- `sts.introduction.b0050`: "If these things are going to take all our jobs, why not make them grow our fucking food first?" The book's real thesis in CT's own voice, and the closest thing in v0.11.0 to the 2024 draft's "What the fuck is going on?" energy. It should be near the top, not at word 1,117.
- `sts.introduction.b0096`: Fired on a Tuesday, dinner already handled. The single most persuasive case for a food baseline, made with one household and no statistics. "It hasn't revoked your permission to have one" is a line people will repeat.
- `sts.introduction.b0089`: "the plow doesn't pull itself". The VOICE-GUIDE's own model of a tattooable hammer. It does more than the 143 ox words that follow it.
- `sts.introduction.b0097`: "listen to what follows" turns AI anxiety into rent, kids and sickness in 47 words. Pure empathy, no lecture.
- `sts.introduction.b0098`: Food still growing, houses still standing, the person locked out. This is the contradiction made concrete; keep it with b0100 ("There's the contradiction.").
- `sts.introduction.b0127`: The title line, set alone. Keep it with b0140 (the UN quote) and b0141 ("So why should food be denied to hungry people because it doesn't sell?").
- `sts.introduction.b0162`: The most concrete, verifiable demonstration of what it means when survival costs money: a court-system quote and a sheriff. It does the work of b0142 and b0143 without their weak sources.
- `sts.introduction.b0148`: CT's voice at full strength, and it earns its profanity. Pair it with b0149 ("Greed isn't a law of physics") and b0150 ("That's the whole book. Everything else is how.").
- `sts.introduction.b0086`: "Get your goddamn hands on it. Use it yourself. Then you'll know." A real instruction with real heat, and the only Monday-morning action in the Introduction.
- `sts.introduction.b0082`: Confessional, unvarnished, and unmistakably CT. It sets the stakes of the author's credibility in one paragraph.
- `sts.introduction.b0151`: If the Ledger is explained anywhere in the core cut, this is the block to keep: concrete hooks (the internet-fad pundit, the digital camera company, the abbot) and a strong end on "The one-way door."


## Preface: Welcome to the Weirdness

**Verdict.** The Preface holds CT's actual first line from April 2024 and his best parable, and wastes both. The line arrives as a second opening after a 3,757-word Introduction, and the very next block explains the joke. The rest is a second table of contents, a defensive all-caps disclaimer, and a 595-word P-01 (44 percent of the section) whose rule contradicts the Preface's own account of who gains power from AI. Its 762-word body repeats the Introduction's food-first ladder and wealth-concentration claim almost line for line.

**Publishable as is?** No. In this position it is a restart, not a preface. The opening and the horse-ranch story (b0039, b0071, b0072, b0073) belong at the front of the book. The disclaimer (b0058, b0059), the contents list (b0051) and the explained joke (b0069) are cut candidates. P-01 survives only in a much shorter form.

### Failure modes

- **critical**: A second book opening placed after the first. b0039 is verbatim CT's 2024 opening line, the best hook in the front matter, and the book now uses it at word 3,803. A reader who just finished the Introduction is restarted with a cold-open joke, then given another tour of what's ahead. (sts.preface.b0039, sts.preface.b0040, sts.preface.b0051) Quote (CT, author voice (verbatim from the 2024 first draft)): "So the robots have taken over. Congratulations! You've lost."
- **major**: The joke is explained immediately. CT's 2024 draft escalated it (nanobots in the water, a brain in a jar, "a sim in an alien game") and trusted the reader. v0.11.0 defuses it in one block and turns earnest. The VOICE-GUIDE's own humor rule says the joke should be the argument, not be followed by one. (sts.preface.b0069) Quote (CT, author voice): "What you've lost is the old deal: sell your days or don't eat. Losing it is the opening. Now we get to decide what we mean by progress."
- **major**: P-01's rule contradicts the Preface. b0044 says AI concentrates wealth and power in "the few people who own the servers, the models, and the land." The P-01 rule tells the reader to hear every "rotting minds" complaint as proof that power moved toward ordinary people. Both cannot be true of AI. The practice then trains readers to treat "AI is rotting people's brains" as a location signal rather than a claim to check. That is a gift to any critic who can cite research on AI and cognition, even though b0066 concedes "Some of the concerns were real." (sts.preface.b0044, sts.preface.b0067, sts.preface.b0068) Quote (CT, author voice): "something just shifted power toward people who don't need permission anymore."
- **major**: Near-duplicate of Introduction content. The food-first ladder, "less precarious", wealth concentration and the bill-paying line all appeared one section earlier (sts.introduction.b0108, sts.introduction.b0163, sts.introduction.b0143, sts.introduction.b0087). (sts.preface.b0056, sts.preface.b0044, sts.preface.b0047) Quote (CT, author voice): "Food is the first assignment. Land, cleanup, shelter, and health belong to the same larger project: use growing capability to make survival less precarious."
- **major**: A defensive disclaimer causes tonal whiplash. After an Introduction about sheriffs, billionaires and enforced rules, an all-caps NOTE says the book isn't about overthrowing the system. The skeptic reads it as either legal cover or a wink, and either way it weakens the Introduction's heat. b0059 then explains the narrative scenes, which the Introduction already did (sts.introduction.b0130). (sts.preface.b0058, sts.preface.b0059) Quote (CT, author voice): "NOTE: this book isn't a guide to overthrowing the system."
- **minor**: The horse-ranch parable cuts against the Introduction's ox. The line "nobody owns a Mustang" says the thinking machine won't stay owned; the Introduction calls it an animal we domesticate (sts.introduction.b0091). The Mustang's meaning is never stated plainly, so a newcomer may miss the warning entirely. (sts.preface.b0072, sts.preface.b0073) Quote (CT, author voice): "The ones in your stable were trained to do what you say. But nobody owns a Mustang."
- **minor**: The 150-word bolded contents list does not match the Part structure the Introduction just gave (sts.introduction.b0154) or the How to Use routes. It introduces mesh networks and open hardware cold, and it leans on a Kipling cliché. (sts.preface.b0051) Quote (CT, author voice): "how to keep your head while everyone else is losing theirs"
- **minor**: In P-01 the caption and the next paragraph make the same point back to back (b0063, then b0064). Fact-check scaffolding also sits inside the story (b0050). (sts.preface.b0063, sts.preface.b0064, sts.preface.b0050) Quote (caption, book apparatus (author voice)): "Two hundred years later we hand this exact picture to children as the cure for their screens."
- **minor**: A practice step assumes an older reader. A 22-year-old cannot do step two. (sts.preface.b0068) Quote (CT, author voice): "Find one person at least fifteen years younger than you who uses AI daily, and ask for twenty minutes of watching them work."
- **minor**: The epigraph has no source, unlike every other epigraph in the front matter, and the VOICE-GUIDE requires a sourced quotation. It is also one of the most overused lines in counterculture writing. (sts.preface.b0052) Quote (Hunter S. Thompson, epigraph): "Buy the ticket, take the ride."

### What readers would complain about

- *newcomer*: "I finished a long introduction and the Preface starts the book over with a joke, then gives me another table of contents."
- *skeptic*: "One chapter rages about sheriffs and billionaires; the next says in capital letters it's not about overthrowing the system. Which book am I reading?"
- *younger reader*: "Step two says to find someone fifteen years younger than me who uses AI. I'm twenty-two. Who is this written for?"
- *academic critic*: "The Preface says AI concentrates power in a few server owners, then teaches me that every moral panic means power moved to the people. Pick one."
- *singularity regular*: "'But nobody owns a Mustang' is the best line in the front matter, and he never tells you what it means."

### Weak writing

- `sts.preface.b0038` "The weirdness arrived on schedule." : An empty caption quip. It means nothing and delays the first sentence.
- `sts.preface.b0044` "Wait, that's not quite right." : A staged self-correction device that reads as a tic, followed by a list of three ("the servers, the models, and the land").
- `sts.preface.b0054` "And that's why you're reading this." : Presumptuous filler set as its own paragraph.
- `sts.preface.b0047` "Here's the neat trick of this moment: how you frame it changes what you can see to do. A reframe won't pay a bill." : Self-help "reframe" language, the clumsy phrase "what you can see to do", and the second bill-paying line in two sections.
- `sts.preface.b0055` "Optimism here means wanting a better outcome enough to investigate how it might actually work. It doesn't mean calling every concern cowardice or every product announcement a breakthrough." : A definitional lecture with a "doesn't mean X" construction. It also contradicts the Introduction's slop paragraph, which does treat a concern as cowardice of a kind (sts.introduction.b0124).
- `sts.preface.b0059` "You'll meet people in these pages who are wrong about important things. Some of them notice. Some need help noticing. That's the point of the scenes." : Explains the fiction before the reader has seen it. The VOICE-GUIDE says scenes never announce their lesson, and here the front matter announces it for them.
- `sts.preface.b0050` "The objections weren't one medical verdict or a view held by everyone. They were arguments about a changing practice, its audience, and who got to supervise it." : An academic hedge with a list of three, sitting in the middle of a story that was working.
- `sts.preface.b0064` "Sit with that. The activity we now prescribe to children as the antidote to their screens, the one on posters in every school library, was condemned in almost exactly the vocabulary now aimed at the tools in your pocket." : "Sit with that" is a stock instruction, and the sentence restates the caption printed directly above it.
- `sts.preface.b0065` "Defend the access; examine the belief. An optimist can inherit that discipline." : The first sentence lands. The second explains it in lecture register.

### Not in your voice

- `sts.preface.b0040` "Popular media tells us we'll be fending off hordes of Terminator bots or fighting over food in the ruins of a climate famine. That's one way it can go. It isn't the only way." : The 2024 draft committed to the bit: "Surviving the Singularity isn't just about fending off hordes of Terminator bots or finding food in the era of economic collapse, and climate famines. It's also about staying hidden from autonomous kill drones and filtering out nanobots from the water!" The revision blames "popular media" and goes sober, keeping the setup and dropping the laugh.
- `sts.preface.b0043` "Look around today and... well, it's pretty much the same, isn't it? The main thing that seems to have changed is the pace." : The original paragraph's punchline was cut: "The only thing that's really changed is the pace at which nightmarish horrors beyond our comprehension are being cooked up in some secret underground lab somewhere halfway across the world, (or on another planet, if you believe that sort of thing)." Its "weapons that threaten to eradicate all life on earth" also became "weapons that could devastate civilization." Every edit hedged, and "seems" was added.
- `sts.preface.b0069` "Now we get to decide what we mean by progress." : Abstract and earnest where CT's original stayed absurd and specific. CT does not explain his own jokes in the 2024 draft.
- `sts.preface.b0055` "Optimism here means wanting a better outcome enough to investigate how it might actually work." : Defining a common word for the reader is a seminar move. CT's original simply shows the optimism ("this is the single most exhilarating time to be alive in the history of humanity").
- `sts.preface.b0058` "NOTE: this book isn't a guide to overthrowing the system." : A legal-disclaimer register with no counterpart in CT's original, which said flatly that capitalism "fights to the last breath and takes a lot of people with it."

### Where a newcomer gets lost

- `sts.preface.b0072`: The line "nobody owns a Mustang" depends on knowing Mustangs are wild horses and on Chapter 3's machine exodus. Neither is set up here.
- `sts.preface.b0051`: "the nine stages, from the first cash grab to the edge of the map" and "The thermodynamics of survival" are still undefined. This is the third mention of the nine stages with no explanation.

### Skim problems

- An image, a caption and an epigraph come before the first sentence.
- The most skimmable element, the bolded list (b0051), is a table of contents, so a skimmer takes away topics, not claims.
- P-01 runs 595 words against a 762-word body. The appendix-style apparatus is 44 percent of the section.
- Runs of one-line paragraphs (b0041, b0053, b0054, b0046, b0070, b0057) make it read like slides.

### Best blocks

- `sts.preface.b0039`: CT's own 2024 opening line, verbatim. It is the best first line the book has, and it belongs on page one.
- `sts.preface.b0071`: The horse-ranch setup: concrete, absurd and funny in exactly the 2024 register (horses with solar panels and flying cars).
- `sts.preface.b0072`: "What do horses want? They just want hay." and "But nobody owns a Mustang." The most memorable two sentences in the front matter.
- `sts.preface.b0073`: The flip ("We're the ones who just built the flying car") turns a doom parable into the book's thesis without a statistic. Keep it with b0071 and b0072 as one unit.
- `sts.preface.b0046`: "Redundancy doesn't have to mean uselessness. And it doesn't have to mean hunger." Short, earned, and on-thesis.
- `sts.preface.b0066`: If P-01 survives in any form, its mechanism is the part to keep: "Some of the concerns were real. The panic wasn't how anyone found out which ones."


## How to Use This Book

**Verdict.** This is the fourth map of the book in 5,200 words, and it sits after most of the material it tells you to skip. Its short route skips Chapter 0, which the book's own VOICE-GUIDE calls its strongest writing. At least one routing row points parents to chapters that aren't about parenting. The same three routes appear three times on one page.

**Publishable as is?** No, not in this position. Cut it from the core cut entirely; a routing page can only be written once the v0.12 structure exists. If anything survives, it is the reader-type table (b0006), and only with its rows re-checked.

### Failure modes

- **major**: The placement defeats its purpose. A reader reading in order reaches this page at word ~5,185, after the Introduction and Preface, so the short route is advice they have already half-followed or ignored. (sts.how-to-use.b0004) Quote (CT, author voice): "The Introduction, Chapter 19, and Appendix C. You'll have the argument, the plan, and the condensed version to hand to someone else."
- **major**: Two of three routes skip Chapter 0, the book's emotional engine. Only "the whole thing" includes Elijah, which tells the reader the story is optional. (sts.how-to-use.b0004, sts.how-to-use.b0009) Quote (caption, book apparatus (author voice)): "Three ways in. The short route gives you the argument and plan; the full route follows Elijah through the chapters."
- **major**: A routing claim the chapters don't support. Parents are sent to Chapter 8 (The Psychology of the Collapse) and Chapter 14 (The Collapse of the Long Tail). A search finds children in those chapters only incidentally (the Companion 2.0 scene, Marta's story about Sal and Gary, Devendra's kids). A skeptic is sent to Chapter 2, the most speculative stretch of the book. (sts.how-to-use.b0006) Quote (table, book apparatus (author voice)): "Chapters 8, 14, and 19, then Appendix G"
- **minor**: The same three routes are shown three times on one page: as a list (b0004), as a diagram (b0008), and again in the caption (b0009). (sts.how-to-use.b0004, sts.how-to-use.b0008, sts.how-to-use.b0009)
- **minor**: Signpost redundancy. The Introduction points to this page (sts.introduction.b0157), the Preface has already listed what's ahead (sts.preface.b0051), and the Part I divider maps it again (sts.part-1.b0004). (sts.how-to-use.b0002, sts.how-to-use.b0004)

### What readers would complain about

- *newcomer*: "Why is the 'short route' page after I've already read the long introduction and the preface?"
- *parent*: "It sent me to Chapters 8 and 14 for raising kids. One is a guy's panic attack, the other is a hardware store closing."
- *bored reader*: "Three ways to read it, shown as a list, then a diagram, then a caption saying the same thing."

### Weak writing

- `sts.how-to-use.b0002` "Three ways in, and a place to start for whoever you are." : A subtitle that restates the page's two headings word for word.

### Not in your voice

- `sts.how-to-use.b0004` "In order. Elijah's story runs through every chapter, and it pays off at the end." : Publisher's-blurb register. CT's 2024 draft guides the reader with a dare ("So let's dig in, let's get uncomfortable, let’s get excited"), not a routing menu. Fine for apparatus, but it is not his voice and doesn't need to be in the core cut.

### Where a newcomer gets lost

- `sts.how-to-use.b0006`: Chapters are named by number only, with no titles, so the table is useless without flipping to the contents.
- `sts.how-to-use.b0004`: "Appendix C" is described only as "the condensed version". A reader can't tell it is an executive reference guide.

### Skim problems

- The page skims well; its problem is position and accuracy, not layout.
- The diagram (b0008) adds nothing the list doesn't already say, and it costs a page turn in print.

### Best blocks

- `sts.how-to-use.b0006`: The reader-type table is the only element here that does something the Introduction doesn't. It is worth rebuilding for v0.12 with the rows checked against the final chapters.


## Chapter 0: The Demonstration

**Verdict.** This is the strongest section in the front matter and the one most worth protecting, but it is not publishable as is. The Bear Flag toast works. Then 318 words after "Were" explain it, which breaks the book's own rule that a scene never announces its lesson. A 129-word Transformer lecture stops the story cold. One key sentence is grammatically broken. And the premise, a weeks-old text chatbot replacing a listing-video producer within seven days, will not survive a singularity regular. P-02's best line is undercut by the hedge after it.

**Publishable as is?** Only with fixes, and all of them can be made by dropping whole blocks: the moralizing after the toast (b0061, b0063, b0091, b0065), the Transformer lecture (b0089), the narrator intrusion (b0012), the spoiler caption (b0098) and the explaining caption (b0110). The broken sentence in b0039 and the realism of the seven-day firing are for CT to fix in v0.12. The 3,236-word scene could run near 2,500 words and lose nothing that pays off later.

### Failure modes

- **major**: The scene announces its lesson after it lands, which violates the VOICE-GUIDE rule "If a narrative passage ends by explaining what it meant, cut the explanation." After the toast (b0058) and the strong beats b0090 and b0060, the narrator spends 318 words (b0061, b0063, b0091, b0065) telling the reader what it meant, in Argument-register sloganeering. The figure caption (b0110) then explains it a second time. (sts.chapter0.b0061, sts.chapter0.b0063, sts.chapter0.b0091, sts.chapter0.b0065, sts.chapter0.b0110) Quote (Chapter 0 narrator, third person, close on Elijah): "They weren't villains. That was the terrifying part. They were ordinary rational actors doing the ordinary rational thing, one week after first contact."
- **major**: The Exposition Machine runs mid-scene. The ELIJAH-PROTOCOL says characters never deliver clean historical lectures and the scene never stops for one. b0089 is a 129-word Transformer explainer: too technical for a newcomer (recurrent chain, parallel training), too basic for a regular, and capped with a fact-checker's correction. (sts.chapter0.b0089) Quote (Chapter 0 narrator, third person): "Many of the leading systems had processed a sentence through a chain, one step depending on the last. The Transformer connected words across a passage through attention, without that recurrent chain, and made training far easier to run in parallel. It still generated an answer piece by piece."
- **major**: A plausibility hole in the founding premise. The ELIJAH-PROTOCOL timeline sets the opening when "Chat Horizon is weeks old", a plain text box modeled on ChatGPT's launch. A week later the realtors have fired "the kid who did our listing videos" and their transaction coordinator. Anyone who used ChatGPT in December 2022 knows a text chatbot did not replace a video producer that week. The book stakes its emotional premise on that seven-day curve, so a regular who catches it will distrust everything after. (sts.chapter0.b0057, sts.chapter0.b0061, sts.chapter0.b0025) Quote (the silver-haired realtor, fictional character): "Yeah, we let three folks go this week. Our transaction coordinator, our marketing gal, and the kid who did our listing videos."
- **major**: Front-loaded backstory delays the payoff of a great hook. From the opener (b0005) to "The realtors were watching." (b0033) is 1,542 words, 843 of them biography (b0100 through b0021, including the 177-word b0015). The opening promises a bar and three careers, then cuts to the grandmother's hills, the farm, three gigs, COVID, a recruiter and Claypot before returning to the bar. (sts.chapter0.b0100, sts.chapter0.b0012, sts.chapter0.b0013, sts.chapter0.b0014, sts.chapter0.b0015) Quote (Chapter 0 narrator, intruding in second person): "You should know how he got there, because how he got there is how most of us got where we are: sideways, on the way to something else."
- **minor**: A broken sentence at the scene's ignition point. The resumptive "Elijah went off" is fused onto a full independent clause. Chapter 6 repeats this refrain in a grammatical form (sts.chapter6.b0087: "And Elijah, God help him, alone with the arithmetic for eleven months and now facing a stranger standing inside the exact answer to it, Elijah went off."), which shows the Chapter 0 version is the defective one. (sts.chapter0.b0039) Quote (Chapter 0 narrator, third person): "And Elijah, God help him, he'd been waiting a month for a single human being to ask, Elijah went off."
- **minor**: The header caption spoils the grandmother's line before she says it. A search of the other annotated sections for "from the root" and "madrone tree" found no echo, so the setup appears to have no later payoff. (sts.chapter0.b0098, sts.chapter0.b0100) Quote (caption, book apparatus (author voice)): "It can burn to the ground and come back from the root."
- **minor**: An entry-level character sees senior material. The ELIJAH-PROTOCOL makes Elijah a junior data hire two years in, yet b0091 has him knowing the company roadmaps. Regulars and industry readers will notice. (sts.chapter0.b0091) Quote (Chapter 0 narrator, close on Elijah): "and he knew, he had *seen the roadmaps*, how much better, how much faster, this was going to get."
- **minor**: Political-baggage risk. The manuscript's origin title rests on Strauss and Howe's generational-cycle theory. The Fourth Turning was popularized in the 2010s by Steve Bannon, and a hostile host can use that against a book that tells readers they don't need "a particular politics" (sts.introduction.b0132). This is a framing risk, not a factual error. (sts.chapter0.b0070, sts.chapter0.b0075) Quote (Chapter 0 narrator, third person): "Years before, a bass player had lent him a paperback about generational cycles"
- **minor**: P-02 hedges its own best line. "The telephone got the century" is immediately walked back. b0102 is source scaffolding (who heard what secondhand) that only a fact-checker needs. (sts.chapter0.b0105, sts.chapter0.b0102) Quote (CT, author voice): "The steam engine got the applause. The telephone got the century. But neither the applause nor the century was decided in that room."
- **minor**: The epigraph is the most-quoted line in tech writing, and its attribution is hedged. Regulars will roll their eyes before the first sentence. (sts.chapter0.b0099) Quote (William Gibson, epigraph): "The future is already here. It's just not very evenly distributed."

### What readers would complain about

- *singularity regular*: "A weeks-old chatbot got a guy who made listing videos fired in seven days? I was using it in December 2022. It couldn't."
- *newcomer*: "The story had me until it stopped to explain how Transformers work. I don't know what a 'recurrent chain' is and I didn't need to."
- *literary critic*: "The toast lands. Then the narrator spends three paragraphs telling me what the toast meant, and the picture caption tells me again."
- *realtor / small-business reader*: "'the blazers realtors wear so you know they're realtors,' the 'clear alpha,' 'commission-brained.' The book says they aren't villains and then dresses them as cartoons."
- *hostile podcast host*: "The book's origin story is named after The Fourth Turning. Interesting choice for a book that says you don't need a particular politics."
- *impatient reader*: "First line: he ended three careers in a bar. Next nine hundred words: his grandmother, his farm, his band, COVID, his résumé."

### Weak writing

- `sts.chapter0.b0013` "That, it would turn out, was the most useful thing school ever taught him, and it wasn't school." : A twist that doesn't parse: school taught him a thing that wasn't school.
- `sts.chapter0.b0025` "which Elijah would appreciate later with something between a laugh and a chill: the machine named for the forest, meeting the man named for a tree." : The narrator explains the name symbolism the reader was about to enjoy finding.
- `sts.chapter0.b0044` "He'd replay it later, the way you replay the moment before a car crash" : A cliché simile at the moment that most needs a fresh image.
- `sts.chapter0.b0063` "People for whom the economy was already a rigged carnival game. People with no margin, no cushion, no lobbyist, the first people every wave hits and the last people anyone rebuilds for." : Slogan fragments and a list of three ("no margin, no cushion, no lobbyist"). This is the author's Argument voice leaking into the narrative.
- `sts.chapter0.b0065` "It was the most significant thing that had ever happened in his lifetime" : Overclaim, followed in the same block by italic emphasis used for volume ("*to* his own community, *through* his own hands"), which the VOICE-GUIDE rules out.
- `sts.chapter0.b0110` "Fictional scene: the Bear Flag toast. Elijah hears what his demonstration helped set in motion." : A caption that explains the scene the reader just finished, and labels it fiction a second time.
- `sts.chapter0.b0012` "You should know how he got there, because how he got there is how most of us got where we are: sideways, on the way to something else." : A narrator intrusion into close third person, and a generalization that tells instead of showing.

### Not in your voice

- `sts.chapter0.b0089` "It still generated an answer piece by piece." : This is the factcheck harness speaking, not a storyteller. The VOICE-GUIDE calls the Narrative register the book's best asset; CT's 2024 draft explained AI by picture ("Every person on this spinning rock gets their own Einstein"), never by architecture correction.
- `sts.chapter0.b0061` "They were ordinary rational actors doing the ordinary rational thing, one week after first contact." : Economics-seminar diction ("rational actors") in a third-person scene. Off-register for the Narrative voice and unlike CT's gut-level phrasing.
- `sts.chapter0.b0102` "The letter is careful about what Bell saw and what he was told: the listeners' reactions come to his parents secondhand, from his associate Willie Hubbard." : Historiographic hedging. CT's original never stops to grade its sources in the text; that work belongs in Appendix B.
- `sts.chapter0.b0105` "But neither the applause nor the century was decided in that room." : A balanced qualifier walking back a hammer. CT's habit, per the VOICE-GUIDE and the 2024 draft, is to swing and leave it.

### Where a newcomer gets lost

- `sts.chapter0.b0089`: "attention", "recurrent chain" and "run in parallel" are unexplained jargon, and none of them is needed for the scene.
- `sts.chapter0.b0027`: "no mechanical Turk": a newcomer won't know the eighteenth-century hoax automaton and may think of Amazon's service.
- `sts.chapter0.b0042`: "Can it do the MLS." The realtors' listing database is never named.
- `sts.chapter0.b0025`: Four invented names (Claypot, PorusAI, Silvana, Chat Horizon) arrive within a few hundred words. Their real-world rhymes (Adobe, OpenAI, ChatGPT) are never signaled, so regulars decode them and newcomers just juggle them.

### Skim problems

- Fiction shouldn't be skimmed, but the long blocks are where readers bail: b0015 (177 words), b0031 (139), b0089 (129), b0040 (118), b0100 (117).
- The only skimmable structure is P-02's bold labels at the end, so a skimmer takes away the Bell lesson and misses the Bear Flag.

### Best blocks

- `sts.chapter0.b0005`: "The night Elijah Madrone ended three careers, he was drinking a four-dollar beer and trying to teach himself CSS." The best first sentence in the book.
- `sts.chapter0.b0018`: The Claypot demos. "The quiet part was in the training data" is the artists' grievance stated with real sympathy. Keep it, and make the Introduction agree with it.
- `sts.chapter0.b0019`: "the vertigo of watching the impossible get a Jira ticket" is the most precise line in the front matter for what this moment feels like from inside.
- `sts.chapter0.b0029`: The insurance-portal chatbot. It is funny and true, and it shows why nobody believed him, all in dialogue.
- `sts.chapter0.b0032`: "Explain flexbox to me like I'm a drummer." The only place in v0.11.0 that recovers the 2024 draft's sense of wonder (Einstein as tech support), and it ends on the hook "who was watching."
- `sts.chapter0.b0033`: "The realtors were watching." A four-word turn.
- `sts.chapter0.b0047`: "He thought he was handing them a telescope." A planted gun that fires in the Conclusion; it must survive.
- `sts.chapter0.b0054`: "Were," Elijah said. The best single beat in the scene. Keep it with b0053, b0055, b0056 and b0057 as one unit.
- `sts.chapter0.b0090`: "The beer was cold and tasted like nothing." Pure Narrative register: the body tells the reader everything.
- `sts.chapter0.b0060`: The kid who did the listing videos: "it wasn't a client, it was the tide." This is the moral done right, through a person.
- `sts.chapter0.b0062`: "He'd gotten one thing right, at least. They saw." A two-sentence callback that makes b0061's explanation unnecessary.
- `sts.chapter0.b0077`: "You're holding it." A bookend the Conclusion echoes verbatim. It must survive, which means the Introduction's fiction disclaimer (sts.introduction.b0130) should not.
- `sts.chapter0.b0106`: P-02's mechanism: "The crowd asks whether it's impressive. The handful asks what dies when it becomes boring, and who will own the wires." The best rule-statement in either Ledger entry in scope.
- `sts.chapter0.b0096`: P-02's practice step one, the boring test, is the most useful Monday action in the front matter: "That list is your actual resume."


## Part I: What is the Singularity?

**Verdict.** Fifty-five words that do one useful thing and two harmful ones. "Keep dinner in view the whole way up" is a good thread. But this is the fifth what's-coming map, and it hangs under a part title, "What is the Singularity?", that more than nine thousand words of front matter have never answered.

**Publishable as is?** Only with fixes. The heading and "Keep dinner in view the whole way up" can stay; the jargon ("the 2017 architecture shift", "the physics that meters all of it") does no work for a newcomer. Because the cut keeps or drops whole blocks, b0004 survives or goes as a unit; it is cheap enough (55 words) to keep for CT to rewrite.

### Failure modes

- **major**: The part title asks the question the front matter dodged. A reader reaches "What is the Singularity?" at about word 9,236 without one sentence answering it. CT's 2024 draft answered it in its first page. (sts.part-1.b0001) Quote (heading, book apparatus): "Part I: What is the Singularity?"
- **minor**: Jargon in the one paragraph meant to orient. The phrase "the physics that meters all of it" means thermodynamics and energy (Chapter 5), which a newcomer cannot know. The phrase "the 2017 architecture shift" points back to the Chapter 0 lecture the newcomer probably skimmed. (sts.part-1.b0004) Quote (CT, author voice): "This part maps the horizon: what the 2017 architecture shift actually did, the nine stages, and the physics that meters all of it."
- **minor**: The fifth roadmap in 9,300 words, after sts.introduction.b0154, sts.preface.b0051, sts.how-to-use.b0004 and the Introduction's nine-stage figure (sts.introduction.b0121). (sts.part-1.b0004)

### What readers would complain about

- *newcomer*: "'Part I: What is the Singularity?' Great question. I've been wondering for forty pages."

### Weak writing

- `sts.part-1.b0004` "what the 2017 architecture shift actually did" : "actually" promises a debunk that never comes, and "architecture shift" is an abstract noun stack.

### Not in your voice

- `sts.part-1.b0004` "The last ones are speculation, and the book will tell you when it crosses that line." : The author refers to "the book" in the third person and offers a disclaimer. CT's 2024 version of the same promise was a dare: "We’ll explore how close we are to crossing that threshold and what signs to watch for (hint: we’re a LOT closer than you think)."

### Where a newcomer gets lost

- `sts.part-1.b0004`: "the physics that meters all of it" and "the 2017 architecture shift" both assume knowledge the front matter never delivered in plain terms.

### Skim problems

- The divider image (b0002, a pixel-art sun over a perspective grid) is generic synthwave and says nothing about food, land or work.

### Best blocks

- `sts.part-1.b0004`: Worth keeping for one line: "Keep dinner in view the whole way up." That is the thread that ties the speculative Part I to the food thesis, and it is in CT's register.


## Chapter 1: The Event Horizon

**Verdict.** Chapter 1 is a good 750-word scene buried under a 3,600-word seminar. It answers the question of what the singularity is with twelve names, a BLEU-score table, a duplicated survey chart and five separate hedges about one date, while the single passage that actually spins a newcomer up from zero (sts.chapter1.b0173, CT's own Einstein-as-tech-support image, lifted almost word for word from the April 2024 draft) sits two-thirds of the way down with no heading and no pull quote. It contradicts itself on its method (a date 'is permission to wait', then 572 words defending a date by curve extrapolation) and on its key term (AGI here is broad capability; in Introduction b0083 it is a system that engineers its own successor). About 40 percent of it can go without losing one idea the rest of the book needs, and close to half with only light loss.

**Publishable as is?** No. Blockers: the Transformer benchmark block (b0147 to b0150) is unreadable for the newcomer the book promises to serve; the survey data appears four times (b0156, b0157, b0175, b0176, restated in b0158); the scene explains itself and spends Chapter 5's payoff (b0017, b0099 to b0101, b0129, captions b0065 and b0134); the AGI definition disagrees with Introduction b0083; the contradiction between 'a date is permission to wait' and the date itself (b0123 against b0111 and b0155) is a v0.12 writing fix that no cut can solve; the heading 'Seventeen definitions' (b0110) contradicts 'sixteen' in b0104 and b0139; and 'The Foundations' (b0023) is an empty H2 stacked on another H2. Proposed v0.11.1 core cut, whole blocks only: DROP b0002, b0127, b0133, b0134, b0064, b0065, b0017, b0099, b0100, b0101, b0023, b0103, b0107, b0108, b0128, b0117, b0120, b0121, b0129, b0124, b0131, b0139, b0147, b0148, b0149, b0150, b0152, b0155, b0156, b0157, b0175, b0176, b0158, b0159, b0163, b0119, b0164, b0165. That removes about 1,850 of 4,640 words and leaves about 2,790. Optional further drops to approach half: the 'In this chapter' pair b0005 and b0097 (both points recur in the kept body), b0114 (the table b0135 carries Kurzweil), b0109 and b0162, which lands near 2,500. Going lower means cutting the scene, which is the wrong trade. Seams to respect: b0131 ('Three things follow.') must go if b0124 goes; keep b0104 while b0110 and b0130 stay, because 'Danaylov left one open' needs him; keep b0102 and b0140 to b0145 as one unit or AGI is never defined in the core; keep b0154 because b0174 says 'the two tests above'; keep b0146 with b0151 so 'That paper' has an antecedent; keep b0169 because b0170's 'The shop' and 'measuring the air' point back to the Dayton wind tunnel.

### Failure modes

- **critical**: The newcomer on-ramp is buried. The first answer the chapter gives to the question of what the singularity is, is a 670-word lineage of twelve names with seven inline citations, before the one concrete picture (b0173) that a first-time reader could actually hold onto. CT's 2024 draft opened its 'What is the Singularity?' chapter on that picture; v0.11.0 hides it behind a seminar. (sts.chapter1.b0105, sts.chapter1.b0106, sts.chapter1.b0107, sts.chapter1.b0108, sts.chapter1.b0109, sts.chapter1.b0112, sts.chapter1.b0113, sts.chapter1.b0114, sts.chapter1.b0128, sts.chapter1.b0173) Quote (CT author voice, paraphrasing real people (Kevin Kelly, John Smart)): "Kevin Kelly says all the change in the last million years will be superseded by the change in the next five minutes. John Smart calls it a permanent and irreversible developmental phase change."
- **critical**: The chapter's method contradicts itself. It declines Accelerating Change 'as a planning tool on purpose' because a date 'is permission to wait', then gives a date and defends it with the most Accelerating-Change evidence available: Aschenbrenner's stacked compute gains and a survey whose 50 percent date moved. A skeptic will quote both halves side by side. (sts.chapter1.b0122, sts.chapter1.b0123, sts.chapter1.b0111, sts.chapter1.b0155, sts.chapter1.b0158, sts.chapter1.b0159) Quote (CT author voice): "It sells you a date, and a date is permission to wait. Yes, I have a date too, and you'll get it in a minute."
- **major**: AGI is defined two different ways in the first thirty pages. Introduction b0083 says the thing he means is 'a system that can understand its own architecture and help engineer the next version of itself'. Chapter 1 defines AGI as broad, reliable capability and explicitly splits self-improvement off as a separate question. The skeptic the reading guide sends here first will catch the goalpost move the book itself mocks. (sts.introduction.b0083, sts.chapter1.b0141, sts.chapter1.b0142) Quote (CT author voice): "Can a system do a broad range of unfamiliar intellectual work with the reliability you'd expect from a capable person? That's what I mean by AGI."
- **major**: The scene explains itself, against the book's own Voice Guide rule ('the scene never announces its lesson'), and spends Chapter 5's payoff in advance. Chapter 5 (b0124, b0125) stages the power bill as a shock and the heat insight as something he only half understood; Chapter 1 has already told the reader both. The captions also pre-announce outcomes before the prose gets there. (sts.chapter1.b0017, sts.chapter1.b0099, sts.chapter1.b0100, sts.chapter1.b0101, sts.chapter1.b0129, sts.chapter1.b0065, sts.chapter1.b0134, sts.chapter5.b0124, sts.chapter5.b0125) Quote (narrator (third person, Elijah's point of view)): "What he could see was the power bill, and the people who could afford it."
- **major**: Fiction is offered as evidence. Introduction b0130 promises that the story's 'imagined events aren't evidence for the arguments that follow each scene', then the Foundations use the invented Bear Flag and the invented tower shutdown as proof of a real-world claim. (sts.chapter1.b0129, sts.chapter1.b0132, sts.introduction.b0130) Quote (CT author voice): "Nobody at the Bear Flag lost their livelihood to a superintelligence. They lost it to a competent autocomplete with a subscription fee."
- **major**: Jargon wall in the Transformer section: 176 words of BLEU, FLOPs, ensembles, WMT 2014, ConvS2S, GNMT and P100s, plus a correction aimed at an imaginary pedant, to deliver one idea that b0151 states in a sentence. A newcomer stops reading here; a regular already knows it. (sts.chapter1.b0147, sts.chapter1.b0148, sts.chapter1.b0149, sts.chapter1.b0150, sts.chapter1.b0151) Quote (CT author voice): "It didn't abolish sequence. Positions still mattered, and its decoder generated an answer one token at a time, using the output already produced."
- **major**: Redundancy inside the chapter and against the Introduction. The AGI date appears in Introduction b0153 and b0088, chapter bullet b0097, then b0111, with three separate 'what would prove me wrong' passages (b0154, b0159, b0174). The two survey numbers appear as a table, its caption, a chart, the chart's caption, and again in prose. 'Plan by doors' lands five times (b0123, b0136, b0171, b0172, b0126). 'losing your job doesn't cost you dinner' (b0125) repeats Introduction b0096 and is restated in b0163. (sts.chapter1.b0097, sts.chapter1.b0111, sts.chapter1.b0154, sts.chapter1.b0159, sts.chapter1.b0174, sts.chapter1.b0156, sts.chapter1.b0157, sts.chapter1.b0175, sts.chapter1.b0176, sts.chapter1.b0158, sts.chapter1.b0123, sts.chapter1.b0136, sts.chapter1.b0171, sts.chapter1.b0172, sts.chapter1.b0126, sts.chapter1.b0125, sts.chapter1.b0163) Quote (CT author voice): "Plan by doors, not dates."
- **major**: Framework overload. In one chapter the reader is handed three schools, one definition with 'Three things follow', four questions, Vinge's four doors, and three practices, before Chapter 2 adds nine stages. Each frame is defensible; stacked, they read as a taxonomy exam. (sts.chapter1.b0135, sts.chapter1.b0131, sts.chapter1.b0140, sts.chapter1.b0113, sts.chapter1.b0126) Quote (CT author voice): "When somebody tells you the Singularity is here, or impossible, ask which of these four they mean."
- **major**: The book's own reading guide (the How to Use table row for 'A skeptic' says 'Chapters 1 and 2, then Appendix D') sends skeptics here first, and the chapter opens on undefined fiction: the toast, Claypot, the Bear Flag, MADRONE ≠ DRONE. The short route and the wider-view route skip Chapters 1 and 2 entirely, which is itself a signal about how load-bearing this chapter is. (sts.chapter1.b0008, sts.chapter1.b0021, sts.chapter1.b0099, sts.chapter1.b0132) Quote (narrator (third person)): "The Monday after the toast, Elijah brought the printout to work."
- **minor**: Factual stretches an informed reviewer will catch. Vinge's fourth road, as b0113 itself states, is 'biological enhancement of human intellect', not neurons in a dish playing Pong, so b0152 misfiles Cortical Labs. The Grace et al. respondents are people who published at AI venues, not 'the people building the systems'. And b0151 credits a 2017 translation paper alone with making intelligence buyable 'by the rack', compressing several later years of scaling work. (sts.chapter1.b0152, sts.chapter1.b0113, sts.chapter1.b0158, sts.chapter1.b0151) Quote (CT author voice): "Silicon isn't the only road, either. Remember Vinge's fourth door, biology."
- **minor**: Human extinction is raised in a subordinate clause and abandoned. The next block pivots to Thanksgiving and the food-first proposal. A newcomer who just read that roughly half of surveyed researchers give a 10 percent chance to extinction-level outcomes gets no follow-up at all. (sts.chapter1.b0158, sts.chapter1.b0159) Quote (CT author voice, summarizing a real survey (Grace et al.)): "gave at least a 10 percent chance to outcomes as bad as human extinction"
- **minor**: Register break and double homework at the close. A Part I Argument chapter suddenly switches to Field Manual imperatives aimed at interviewing growers (many readers know none), then immediately runs the P-03 practice list. Two assignments back to back, the first one off-topic for a chapter about what the word means. (sts.chapter1.b0119, sts.chapter1.b0164, sts.chapter1.b0165, sts.chapter1.b0126) Quote (CT author voice): "Write their answer down before you propose a robot."
- **minor**: Continuity slip: Elijah tells Devendra only about 'a bar, a Tuesday, three jobs', yet Devendra answers about them by trade: 'The realtors'. (sts.chapter1.b0010, sts.chapter1.b0011) Quote (Devendra (fictional character)): "The realtors didn't fire humans because a chatbot is a person."
- **minor**: Counting is inconsistent. The heading says seventeen definitions, the text says sixteen, and the span is given as 'a hundred and eighty years', 'more than a century and a half' and 'Eighteen decades'. (sts.chapter1.b0110, sts.chapter1.b0104, sts.chapter1.b0139, sts.chapter1.b0117, sts.chapter1.b0097) Quote (heading): "## Seventeen definitions and no agreement"
- **minor**: The precedent is the most recycled anecdote in AI-optimist writing. The regular reader has seen the 1903 'one million to ten million years' editorial in countless posts. The Wright wind-tunnel material (b0169, b0170) is the fresh part; the newspaper is the part they will skim. (sts.chapter1.b0166, sts.chapter1.b0167) Quote (CT author voice): "Sixty-nine days later, the Wright Flyer lifted off the sand at Kitty Hawk."

### What readers would complain about

- *newcomer*: "I bought a survival book and Chapter 1 handed me a reading list: a preacher, a sheep farmer, Turing, Ulam, Good, Vinge, Kurzweil, Moravec, Bostrom, Kelly, Smart, Yudkowsky, then a table of BLEU scores and FLOPs. I quit at 'Parallel training and sequential generation are different jobs.' One star."
- *singularity regular*: "Vinge, Good's 'last invention', Kurzweil's curve, Yudkowsky's three schools, Aschenbrenner, the Grace survey and the 1903 flying-machine editorial. I've read this chapter on every AI Substack since 2023. The only new thing is an AGI date picked 'Because.'"
- *skeptic*: "He says a date 'is permission to wait' and declines the curve 'on purpose', then props up Thanksgiving 2027 with Aschenbrenner's compute curve. And his proof that cheap autocomplete kills jobs is a bar scene he made up."
- *skeptic following the How to Use route*: "The reading guide told skeptics to start at Chapter 1. Chapter 1 opens 'The Monday after the toast' and ends the scene on MADRONE ≠ DRONE. What toast? What Bear Flag? Who is Devendra?"
- *hostile podcast host*: "He gives his AGI date twice in the Introduction, again in the chapter bullets, again here, tells you what would prove him wrong three separate times, and then explains his own joke: 'That's the joke, and it's also the boundary around the claim.' Pick a lane."
- *literary critic*: "The tower scene is the best writing in the chapter, and the narrator can't stop walking into it to explain it, even promising 'a whole later chapter of this book' to say it properly."
- *informed AI reviewer*: "Neurons in a dish playing Pong are not Vinge's fourth road, which by the author's own summary was 'biological enhancement of human intellect'. And survey respondents who published a paper are not 'the people building the systems'. Sloppy where it most wants to sound rigorous."
- *farmer*: "Chapter 1 ends by sending strangers to ask me which of my tasks 'eats time without making the food any better' before they propose a robot. So I'm the homework now."

### Weak writing

- `sts.chapter1.b0103` "That isn't a technicality, and it isn't pedantry. It's the reason the argument never resolves, and it's the reason two intelligent people can look at the same week of news and walk away with opposite plans." : Throat-clearing 'isn't X, isn't Y, it's Z' construction, then a doubled 'it's the reason' anaphora. The payload is the last clause.
- `sts.chapter1.b0117` "Eighteen decades. No consensus. One word, doing all of that work." : Fragment tricolon written for rhythm. It restates the previous 700 words and adds nothing.
- `sts.chapter1.b0121` "Those are three genuinely different animals." : Filler opener; the paragraph then restates the table directly above it.
- `sts.chapter1.b0154` "That's the joke, and it's also the boundary around the claim." : Explaining the joke. 'Because.' in b0111 works only if nobody annotates it.
- `sts.chapter1.b0173` "It's a funny picture." : Tells the reader how to react to the image instead of trusting it. Same defect as b0154.
- `sts.chapter1.b0159` "Thanksgiving can come and go without anyone getting permission to pretend a missed forecast was secretly a different forecast all along." : Tangled syntax; the subject and the point have to be reconstructed on a second read.
- `sts.chapter1.b0155` "That's one insider's argument, not a measurement." : 'X, not Y' hedge; the same block adds 'not simply the same people revising their answers'. Methods-section caution in an Argument chapter.
- `sts.chapter1.b0151` "That paper is an architecture, not a prophecy." : 'X, not Y' rebutting a claim nobody in the chapter made.
- `sts.chapter1.b0158` "These aren't doomers on a forum. These are the people building the systems, surveyed by the thousands" : 'Not X, these are Y' construction carrying an overclaim about who the respondents are.
- `sts.chapter1.b0015` "not magically, not infinitely, but *steadily*" : 'Not X, not Y, but Z' cadence inside otherwise physical, concrete narrative.
- `sts.chapter1.b0099` "needed *watts*, needed *cooling*, needed the same brutal physics that decided whether his grandmother's crop lived or died." : Anaphoric tricolon in a paragraph whose whole job is to explain a scene that already worked. Sermon.
- `sts.chapter1.b0101` "The machine had shut itself down. The question that had kept him feeding it hadn't stopped." : Restates b0016 in flatter words. Padding between the shutdown and the real closing beat (b0021).
- `sts.chapter1.b0017` "understood something he would spend a whole later chapter of this book trying to say properly." : Authorial intrusion into a third-person scene, plus a 'trust me, later' deferral that drains the moment.
- `sts.chapter1.b0124` "A pay cut or hiring freeze alone doesn't identify the cause. Find out which tasks actually changed, what replaced them, and whether the work still gets done." : Consultant memo register and a list of three, appended to a bold claim ('It's testable this quarter') that it quietly walks back.
- `sts.chapter1.b0142` "It doesn't mean every proposed change works or that lab experiments become instantaneous." : Rebuts a strawman no reader raised. Defensive drafting for a reviewer, not writing for a reader.
- `sts.chapter1.b0163` "This book's proposal for the first term is small, concrete, and testable" : Adjective tricolon describing the proposal instead of showing it.
- `sts.chapter1.b0139` "This one looks down, at the floor going soft under people you know by name." : The author grading his own definition above Vinge's and Kurzweil's. Pretty, but self-congratulatory, and it follows a block that already made the point.
- `sts.chapter1.b0016` "The room ticked as it cooled, exactly like the engine of a car after a long haul." : Second car simile in two paragraphs (b0015 already used 'the cab of his delivery car'). One of them should go.
- `sts.chapter1.b0125` "Everything in Part II is about that gap. Everything in Part III is about what you build inside it while it's open." : Roadmap signposting that the Introduction (b0154) already did, inserted into the chapter's strongest paragraph.

### Not in your voice

- `sts.chapter1.b0154` "That's the joke, and it's also the boundary around the claim. This is my forecast, not a mathematical deadline." : CT in 2024 throws a joke and leaves it ('Kidding. Mostly.'). He also wrote 'we’re a LOT closer than you think' with no hedge at all. Here the joke is footnoted and the claim is lawyered.
- `sts.chapter1.b0147` "Parallel training and sequential generation are different jobs." : Errata voice answering an imaginary pedant. Nothing in the 2024 draft corrects anyone; it talks to a scared reader, not to a reviewer.
- `sts.chapter1.b0155` "The later survey was much larger and drew from more publication venues, so these are changing samples, not simply the same people revising their answers" : Methods-section register. The 2024 CT voice is second person and emotional ('You're afraid. I get it. Who wouldn't be?').
- `sts.chapter1.b0150` "Figures are for the 213-million-parameter "big" configuration; the 65-million-parameter base model reached 27.3 and 38.1 on the same two tasks after twelve hours on the same hardware." : Technical-report caption. CT's original references (Siri, ChatGPT, 'ChatEinstein') are the ones a general reader already knows.
- `sts.chapter1.b0124` "A pay cut or hiring freeze alone doesn't identify the cause." : HR consultant caution. CT's own move is the blunt claim, then the joke.
- `sts.chapter1.b0164` "Ask what would count as a better result, and what would make a trial a failure." : User-research protocol language. It sounds like a methods appendix, not a man who opens a chapter with 'What the fuck is going on?'
