- `sts.chapter1.b0142` "It doesn't mean every proposed change works or that lab experiments become instantaneous." : Defensive qualification. The 2024 draft states 'the singularity isn't just coming, it's here' and lets the reader push back.
- `sts.chapter1.b0176` "The two milestones are distinct, and neither date is a deadline." : A caption that hedges a chart. CT's voice carries conviction; this carries a disclaimer.

### Where a newcomer gets lost

- `sts.chapter1.b0008`: 'the toast' assumes Chapter 0. The How to Use guide sends skeptics straight to Chapter 1, where it is undefined.
- `sts.chapter1.b0009`: 'Attention Is All You Need' and 'gradient descent' are unexplained; the paper is not described until b0147, about 1,900 words later.
- `sts.chapter1.b0014`: 'open model', 'fine-tuning' and 'consumer GPUs' are undefined, and the scene's meaning rests on them.
- `sts.chapter1.b0015`: 'The loss curve kept sliding down' is the emotional hinge of the scene, and a newcomer has no idea what a loss curve is or why sliding is good.
- `sts.chapter1.b0021`: MADRONE ≠ DRONE means nothing without Chapter 0.
- `sts.chapter1.b0097`: 'AGI' is used in the bullets and the date section (b0111) before b0141 defines it, and Introduction b0083 defined it differently.
- `sts.chapter1.b0106`: 'That's recursive self-improvement' names a concept the chapter does not define until b0142.
- `sts.chapter1.b0118`: Yudkowsky appears with no handle at all; Danaylov at least gets 'who runs Singularity Weblog'.
- `sts.chapter1.b0123`: 'Plan by doors' and the pull quote b0136 use 'door' as a term of art that is not defined until practice item 2 in b0126, at the very end.
- `sts.chapter1.b0128`: Moravec, Bostrom, Kevin Kelly and John Smart arrive as bare names; a newcomer cannot weigh any of them.
- `sts.chapter1.b0147`: recurrent chain, attention, decoder, token: none explained.
- `sts.chapter1.b0149`: BLEU, WMT 2014, ConvS2S, GNMT, ensembles, FLOPs, P100 GPUs, scientific notation: a wall.
- `sts.chapter1.b0152`: 'reinforcement-learning programs' and 'faster per sample' assume ML literacy.
- `sts.chapter1.b0156`: HLMI, FAOL and the idea of an aggregate 50 percent forecast are undefined; the table assumes the reader knows how expert elicitation works.
- `sts.chapter1.b0172`: 'orders of magnitude' is the rule's key phrase and is never glossed for a general reader.

### Skim problems

- '## The Foundations' (b0023) is immediately followed by '## Seventeen definitions and no agreement' (b0110): an empty H2 that shows up in any table of contents as a section with nothing in it.
- The heading b0110 says seventeen, the text (b0104, b0139) says sixteen, and the time span is stated three different ways; a skimmer gets three numbers for one fact.
- The bold-year lineage (b0106 to b0114) looks skimmable but most entries end in a long inline citation link, so the eye lands on URLs instead of points.
- The chapter's most vivid image (b0173, Einstein as tech support) has no heading and no pull quote; a skimmer will miss the one thing that makes the definition click.
- The thesis line 'losing your job doesn't cost you dinner' is the last clause of a bold-lead paragraph (b0125), not a pull quote.
- Three data displays in an Argument chapter, two of them (b0156 table and b0175 chart) carrying identical numbers, and the third (b0149) unreadable without ML literacy.
- 'Who gets the savings?' (b0160 to b0163) and 'The first question to take outside' (b0119, b0164, b0165) are thin H2 sections of 197 and 82 words that read as stubs.
- Two homework lists close the chapter back to back (b0164 and the P-03 practice b0126), so the chapter's real ending is hard to find.

### Best blocks

- `sts.chapter1.b0173`: CT's own 2024 image, nearly verbatim from the first draft's Chapter 2, and the only passage that turns the definition into a picture a newcomer can hold. It ends on the real question: 'what happens to everyone who was paid to be the smart one in the room.' In v0.12 it should lead the definition section, not trail it.
- `sts.chapter1.b0138`: The book's working definition. Everything downstream depends on it, and it is quotable.
- `sts.chapter1.b0012`: 'Elijah wanted to be talked down. That was the humiliating part.' Emotional truth in two sentences, and it plants 'the arithmetic'.
- `sts.chapter1.b0011`: Devendra gives the opposing view its best voice ('Augmentation, not replacement', 'Everybody's first month feels like the apocalypse') and sets up his Chapter 14 payoff.
- `sts.chapter1.b0015`: Physical, specific, and the heat plant Chapter 5 depends on. The meter 'spinning somewhere out in the garage' is the right amount of setup.
- `sts.chapter1.b0016`: The shutdown at 1:20 a.m., told without commentary. This is where the scene should end before b0021.
- `sts.chapter1.b0013`: 'the only way a farm kid ever really trusts a thing: he'd try to break it.' Character in one line.
- `sts.chapter1.b0021`: Clean closing beat that hands the notebook forward.
- `sts.chapter1.b0123`: The chapter's planning argument in CT's register: 'Plan by doors. Doors don't give notice.'
- `sts.chapter1.b0136`: 'A curve gives you a date. A door gives you a Monday.' The tattoo line the Voice Guide asks for.
- `sts.chapter1.b0132`: 'a competent autocomplete with a subscription fee' is the sharpest line in the Foundations (it needs the fiction-as-evidence problem fixed, not the line cut).
- `sts.chapter1.b0125`: Names the emergency as the gap, and lands the thesis in plain words: 'losing your job doesn't cost you dinner.'
- `sts.chapter1.b0111`: The date with 'Because.' Funny, CT, and memorable, as long as nothing explains it.
- `sts.chapter1.b0106`: Thornton 1847 is the one lineage entry a singularity regular has probably not seen, and the punchline ('by a man annoyed about arithmetic homework') is the book's humor at its best.
- `sts.chapter1.b0112`: Good's condition and the instruction 'Don't.' It also earns the next chapter's epigraph (which should then go).
- `sts.chapter1.b0135`: The three-schools table is the most skimmable thing in the chapter and maps each school to a posture.
- `sts.chapter1.b0144`: 'Someone can live next door to a productive farm and still go hungry.' The Access question in one line, and the one the book lives in.
- `sts.chapter1.b0170`: 'The Times was measuring the most recent failure. The Wrights were measuring the air.' Best line in the precedent.
- `sts.chapter1.b0171`: The mechanism, with the honest clause 'including mine' that buys back credibility the date section spends.
- `sts.chapter1.b0126`: The practice list finally defines a 'door' (item 2) and ends on 'You don't have that luxury; you live here.'
- `sts.chapter1.b0161`: 'If thinking gets cheap, somebody saves a lot of money. The question is who.' Plain, sharp, CT.
- `sts.chapter1.b0174`: 'written to be true on both sides of that Thanksgiving' is the one date-hedge worth keeping.


## Chapter 2: The Era of AGI (Stages 1 to 5)

**Verdict.** The Claypot all-hands is the best fiction in Part I and should survive nearly whole: COMPANION lettered like a children's hospital, the sovereignty tier, the pride that turns in his stomach, and Elijah's 'That's the hedge,' then 'I helped plant it.' The Foundations under it are a scenario map that keeps claiming to be a prediction. The title says 'Era of AGI' while every receipt predates the author's own November 2027 AGI date, and the one modern receipt is announced as 'Stage 3 arrived on schedule' though it matches nothing in the Stage 3 definition. It also carries a hard-rule en dash in its title, two orphaned phrases from old edits, the same 'Read the rule' advice three times, and a liability paragraph about oxygen concentrators in the middle of an argument about capital.

**Publishable as is?** No. Hard blockers: the en dash in the title heading b0001 (the same defect is in the Chapter 3 and Chapter 4 titles); the orphaned 'The corners' in b0104 and 'by the year they were born' in b0133; and the Stage 3 label on the June 2026 receipt (b0141) that contradicts the Stage 3 definition (b0110) and the chapter's own 'may overlap or fail to occur' (b0096). Proposed v0.11.1 core cut, whole blocks only: DROP b0002, b0003, b0120, b0005, b0096, b0009, b0031, b0100, b0102, b0104, b0126, b0141, b0142, b0143, b0127, b0128, b0144, b0145, b0111, b0130, b0131, b0133, b0137, b0138. That removes about 1,330 of 3,445 words and leaves about 2,110, most of it the scene, which is the right shape for this chapter. To get closer to half, also drop b0125 (the Red Flag teaser that P-04 retells in full), which lands near 2,030. Seams to respect: b0028 needs b0122 and b0123; keep b0097 and b0098 together (the heading is empty without b0098); with b0009 gone, b0015 still establishes the notebook and the tower.

### Failure modes

- **critical**: The title contradicts the chapter's own evidence. Chapter 1 b0111 puts AGI at November 25, 2027 and the Introduction (b0077) says 'we're already inside the first one'. Yet this chapter, titled 'The Era of AGI', proves Stage 1 with the ChatGPT launch and Stages 2 and 3 with June 2026 events. Either the stages do not require AGI, or we are not in them yet. A skeptic will put the two dates side by side. (sts.chapter2.b0001, sts.chapter2.b0100, sts.chapter2.b0126, sts.chapter2.b0141, sts.chapter1.b0111, sts.introduction.b0077) Quote (CT author voice): "We've already seen the preview. Look at what happened when ChatGPT dropped."
- **critical**: The modern receipt is mislabeled and overclaimed. Stage 3 is defined (b0110) as grid operators, water-plant crews, SREs and volunteers keeping systems up. The event labeled Stage 3 is a government lifting export controls and a company announcing safeguards and 'closer government collaboration'. Calling it 'on schedule' also contradicts the chapter's own bullets and b0098, and the caption b0143 then retreats to 'the author’s interpretation'. Three positions on one event within 150 words. (sts.chapter2.b0141, sts.chapter2.b0110, sts.chapter2.b0096, sts.chapter2.b0098, sts.chapter2.b0143) Quote (CT author voice): "Then Stage 3 arrived on schedule."
- **major**: Hard-rule violation: the chapter title contains an en dash (U+2013) between 'Stages 1' and '5'. CT's rule is no em or en dashes anywhere. (sts.chapter2.b0001) Quote (heading): "# Chapter 2: The Era of AGI (Stages 1"
- **major**: Orphaned references left by earlier edits. 'The corners' has no antecedent anywhere in Stage 1 (the paragraphs above talk about a leash, a market and a door). 'by the year they were born' answers a generational argument that does not exist in this chapter. (sts.chapter2.b0104, sts.chapter2.b0133) Quote (CT author voice): "The corners don't hold for long, though."
- **major**: The chapter argues with itself. It titles the section 'The Cash Grab', scolds the reader that calling the players greedy 'explains less', then two paragraphs later says 'the greed of Stage 1'. The P-04 mechanism says rules aimed at a specific harm 'can do real good', yet the Fable 5 directive, triggered (per b0126) by a safeguard bypass that produced working exploit code, is framed as incumbent panic. (sts.chapter2.b0101, sts.chapter2.b0104, sts.chapter2.b0126, sts.chapter2.b0139) Quote (CT author voice): "Calling all of them greedy explains less than asking what each of them gets paid to do."
- **major**: Tendentious framing of a security rule. The US Commerce Department is called 'an incumbent', and Z.ai's open-weight release ('Within days') is placed to imply cause. The receipt is also date-stamped June 2026 and will read as stale news to anyone who picks the book up a year from now, and it is the most jargon-dense paragraph in the chapter. (sts.chapter2.b0126) Quote (CT author voice): "They were doing what an incumbent with a legal pad can do to a machine that's already loose: deciding where it lives, while telling themselves they were deciding whether."
- **major**: Repetition. 'Read the rule' three times (b0127, b0140, practice item 1 in b0117). The Red Flag story told twice (b0125 summary, then P-04 at 500 words). The Good epigraph (b0120) repeats the quote Chapter 1 b0112 just analyzed. The coin-slot image three times (b0015, b0100, b0124). 'We've already seen the preview' twice (b0100, b0116). The 'who slows down' tricolon twice (b0137, b0139). (sts.chapter2.b0127, sts.chapter2.b0140, sts.chapter2.b0117, sts.chapter2.b0125, sts.chapter2.b0136, sts.chapter2.b0120, sts.chapter1.b0112, sts.chapter2.b0015, sts.chapter2.b0100, sts.chapter2.b0124, sts.chapter2.b0116, sts.chapter2.b0137, sts.chapter2.b0139) Quote (CT author voice): "**The rule.** Read the rule before you turn it into a morality play, in either direction."
- **major**: Disclaimer and liability padding breaks the Argument register. The 'In this chapter' bullets are three hedges; b0098 repeats the hedge; b0128 detours into powered medical equipment and the FDA; two conceptual figures (b0142, b0144) restate adjacent prose; b0138 pauses to clarify which heavy road engines were exempt. (sts.chapter2.b0096, sts.chapter2.b0098, sts.chapter2.b0128, sts.chapter2.b0142, sts.chapter2.b0143, sts.chapter2.b0144, sts.chapter2.b0145, sts.chapter2.b0138) Quote (CT author voice): "Someone who relies on powered medical equipment needs an outage plan made with their healthcare provider and equipment supplier."
- **major**: Stage 4, which b0115 calls 'the hinge of the whole map', has no mechanism. The proposal is four vague imperatives with no who, no funding and no example, while Stage 2 gets 669 words and two figures. (sts.chapter2.b0130, sts.chapter2.b0131, sts.chapter2.b0115) Quote (CT author voice): "Pay for the remaining work through something dependable. Let farmers and kitchens take part on terms they can live with."
- **minor**: The food thesis is bolted onto stages whose evidence is about creative software and export controls, so it reads like an ad read inserted mid-segment. (sts.chapter2.b0102, sts.chapter2.b0128) Quote (CT author voice): "For food, the Stage 1 question is specific: does the money help produce and deliver something people can eat, and do people without money share in it?"
- **minor**: The label 'primate backlash' will read as contempt to the displaced workers the scene just asked the reader to care about. The hedge 'the primates are all of us' shows the author knows it lands badly. (sts.chapter2.b0094, sts.chapter2.b0132, sts.chapter2.b0021) Quote (CT author voice): "Call it the primate backlash, and understand that the primates are all of us."
- **minor**: Exposition in dialogue. ELIJAH-PROTOCOL allows enclosure as a weapon in a fight, but b0122 also carries a fact-checker's correction inside Elijah's angry speech. Nobody qualifies their own analogy mid-argument at a coffee machine. (sts.chapter2.b0122) Quote (Elijah (fictional character)): "The commons weren't free to everybody. There were rights and customs, who could graze what, who could cut what."
- **minor**: Unsourced claims in an otherwise heavily sourced chapter: robots kicked and cars burned, 'far more' people caring about sales funnels, and an oath every infrastructure worker supposedly took. (sts.chapter2.b0116, sts.chapter2.b0100, sts.chapter2.b0110) Quote (CT author voice): "Every one of them has taken something like an oath, formal or private, to keep the systems up."
- **minor**: Continuity. b0015 says he had been sketching the stages for weeks; b0124 has him write them down that night as if for the first time. b0124 calls it 'the dead tower', but Chapter 5 b0124 has a month of fine-tuning runs on that same tower. (sts.chapter2.b0015, sts.chapter2.b0124, sts.chapter5.b0124) Quote (narrator (third person, Elijah's point of view)): "He wrote the stages down as predictions, dated, initialed, so his future self couldn't pretend he hadn't seen it coming"
- **minor**: The header image is a museum exhibit of car-factory robot arms, unrelated to a chapter about software enclosure, export controls and a social contract. (sts.chapter2.b0002, sts.chapter2.b0003) Quote (caption (CT author voice)): "A demonstration of industrial automation."

### What readers would complain about

- *working illustrator*: "The book spends one scene making me care about artists whose portfolios got scraped, then five pages later anyone who breaks a robot is part of 'the primate backlash'. The only artist in the chapter never even gets a name."
- *policy skeptic*: "The government blocks a model after researchers get working exploit code out of it, and the author calls that 'an incumbent with a legal pad'. His own precedent two pages later says rules aimed at a specific harm 'can do real good'. Which is it?"
- *newcomer*: "Mythos 5, Fable 5, Z.ai, open-weight, MIT license, model card, mesh links, local compute, site-reliability engineers. Nobody stops to tell me what any of it is."
- *singularity regular*: "The chapter is called 'The Era of AGI' and every example in it happened before the author's own AGI date. These stages are the hype cycle with a food bank stapled on."
- *hostile podcast host*: "'Then Stage 3 arrived on schedule.' That's from the man whose own bullets promise the stages 'may overlap or fail to occur'. Then his caption calls it 'the author’s interpretation'. He wants credit for a prediction and an exit if it fails."
- *free-market reader*: "Stage 4 is supposedly 'the hinge of the whole map', and the plan is 'Pay for the remaining work through something dependable.' That is a shrug with a capital letter."
- *left-leaning reader*: "The section is literally titled 'The Cash Grab', and he still stops to scold me that calling them greedy 'explains less'. Two paragraphs later he writes 'the greed of Stage 1' himself."
- *bored general reader*: "In a chapter about AI and money I got told to make an outage plan with my oxygen supplier, with an FDA link. Is this a book or a terms-of-service page?"

### Weak writing

- `sts.chapter2.b0096` "Technical competence does not confer authority over a community." : Stacked abstract nouns, uncontracted, and a topic rather than a claim with teeth (the Voice Guide's own test for these bullets).
- `sts.chapter2.b0098` "The map is useful because it tells you what to watch for, not because history is obliged to follow it." : 'Because X, not because Y' disclaimer, the second hedge in a row after the bullets.
- `sts.chapter2.b0099` "When the machine gets good, there are no angels singing. The sky doesn't crack open to reveal a new dawn." : Two stacked cliches saying the same thing, followed by 'capitalization', a vague abstract noun where a concrete one belongs.
- `sts.chapter2.b0101` "Calling all of them greedy explains less than asking what each of them gets paid to do." : Academic hedge that takes the heat out of a section titled 'The Cash Grab', then gets contradicted in b0104.
- `sts.chapter2.b0104` "The corners don't hold for long, though." : Orphaned noun with no antecedent; the reader stops to look for corners.
- `sts.chapter2.b0125` "the oldest move in the incumbent's book" : Stock phrase.
- `sts.chapter2.b0141` "That's the whole arc of this chapter, run at speed, with receipts." : Overclaim: Stages 4 and 5 did not occur in those eighteen days.
- `sts.chapter2.b0110` "Every one of them has taken something like an oath, formal or private, to keep the systems up." : Unfalsifiable romance hedged with 'something like' and 'private'.
- `sts.chapter2.b0112` "The adults in the room earn trust by answering hard questions from people who didn't build the thing, not by getting to skip them." : Lecture tone and a 'not by' construction; the point is right, the delivery is civics class.
- `sts.chapter2.b0130` "Pay for the remaining work through something dependable." : Says nothing. The phrase 'something dependable' is a placeholder where a mechanism should be.
- `sts.chapter2.b0131` "We stop saying that one person's continued employment is the only acceptable bridge between a productive society and that person's next meal." : Abstract nouns stacked four deep (employment, bridge, society, meal). The block's last line does the job better.
- `sts.chapter2.b0133` "Nobody gets sorted into a morally fixed camp by the year they were born." : Orphaned line answering a generational argument that is not in the chapter.
- `sts.chapter2.b0137` "It wasn't a vote on progress. It was a fight over who had to slow down, who supplied the extra labor, and who got protected from a hazard." : 'Not X, it was Y' plus a tricolon, then repeated almost word for word in b0139.
- `sts.chapter2.b0121` "Then the pride curdled, all at once, into something that sat in his stomach like a swallowed stone." : Two stock images (curdled, swallowed stone) inside the chapter's best paragraph.
- `sts.chapter2.b0027` "For once, the first time in two years, Devendra didn't have a worn-smooth answer ready." : 'For once' and 'the first time in two years' say the same thing.
- `sts.chapter2.b0124` "did what he'd promised: the arithmetic, without flinching." : The arithmetic motif has come loose from meaning; he writes stage names, he does no arithmetic. Fourth use across two chapters.
- `sts.chapter2.b0115` "It's the hinge of the whole map." : Mixed metaphor (maps do not have hinges).
- `sts.chapter2.b0117` "If the pause holds longer than expected, you've lost nothing." : Overclaim; preparation costs time and money.
- `sts.chapter2.b0138` "It wasn't a repeal for every heavy road engine." : Pedantic aside that stalls the payoff of the precedent.

### Not in your voice

- `sts.chapter2.b0096` "Technical competence does not confer authority over a community." : Uncontracted policy-memo register. The 2024 CT says 'If humanity could get its collective head out of its collective ass'. The Voice Guide lists contractions as non-negotiable.
- `sts.chapter2.b0101` "Calling all of them greedy explains less than asking what each of them gets paid to do." : CT in 2024 wrote that 'Capitalism fights to the last breath and takes a lot of people with it.' This version apologizes for the word 'greedy' under his own heading 'The Cash Grab'.
- `sts.chapter2.b0128` "Someone who relies on powered medical equipment needs an outage plan made with their healthcare provider and equipment supplier." : Liability boilerplate. Nothing like it exists in the 2024 draft; it is a risk reviewer's sentence, not CT's.
- `sts.chapter2.b0130` "Let farmers and kitchens take part on terms they can live with. Build the recipient's access in from the start." : Committee voice. CT's original names the obstacle bluntly ('if we could get our shit together') instead of drafting terms of reference.
- `sts.chapter2.b0143` "Stage 2 and Stage 3 are the author’s interpretation." : Third-person 'the author' in a book written in first person. A legal caption, not CT.
- `sts.chapter2.b0138` "It wasn't a repeal for every heavy road engine." : Pedant's correction. The 2024 voice moves at speed and does not stop to qualify a historical aside.
- `sts.chapter2.b0122` "The commons weren't free to everybody. There were rights and customs, who could graze what, who could cut what." : Elijah's line (a character, not CT), but the hedge is an editor's insertion into dialogue. It flattens the Narrative register the Voice Guide calls the book's strongest writing.

### Where a newcomer gets lost

- `sts.chapter2.b0001`: 'AGI' in the title is never spelled out in this chapter, and 'Stages 1' to '5' assumes the reader remembers the nine-stage map from the Introduction (b0077).
- `sts.chapter2.b0009`: The Bear Flag, the tower and MADRONE ≠ DRONE are callbacks a skeptic routed here by How to Use has never seen.
- `sts.chapter2.b0017`: 'ingestion lattice', 'deduplication pipeline', 'commits' and 'trainable rows' are opaque; the reader gets the feeling but not the fact that his code fed the model.
- `sts.chapter2.b0104`: 'open models and foreign labs' assumes the reader knows what an open model is and why it escapes a walled garden.
- `sts.chapter2.b0126`: Anthropic, Mythos 5, Fable 5, export controls, 'foreign person', safeguards, exploit code, Z.ai, open-weight, MIT license and model card in one paragraph, none explained.
- `sts.chapter2.b0110`: 'site-reliability engineers' and 'people who carry pagers' assume tech-industry knowledge.
- `sts.chapter2.b0111`: 'local compute', 'mesh links' and 'the long-haul ones' are undefined.
- `sts.chapter2.b0122`: Enclosure of the commons and Parliament's enclosure acts are assumed British history; an American newcomer may not know what 'the commons' were.
- `sts.chapter2.b0136`: 'road locomotive' will make a newcomer picture a train; the chapter never says these were steam engines driven on ordinary roads.

### Skim problems

- '## The Foundations' (b0031) sits directly on top of '## Stages are scenarios' (b0097): two stacked H2s again, the second holding a single 54-word disclaimer.
- The 'In this chapter' bullets (b0096) are three disclaimers. A skimmer learns neither the five stage names nor any claim about them.
- The chapter has zero pull quotes, although 'grief with a brick in its hand' (b0132), 'The machine changed what's possible. The arrangement decides what happens next.' (b0129) and Elijah's 'That's the hedge,' / 'I helped plant it.' (b0028) are exactly what the Voice Guide's pull-quote line is for.
- Stage 2 runs 669 words with two figures; Stage 3 runs 197 and Stage 1 258. The stage the book calls its hinge (Stage 4) gets no visual anchor at all.
- Both figures (b0142 timeline, b0144 local-copy diagram) restate the adjacent prose, so a skimmer who looks only at images gets dates and a folder icon but no argument.
- The Red Flag story appears mid-chapter (b0125) and again as the closing precedent, so a skimmer reading section ends meets it twice.

### Best blocks

- `sts.chapter2.b0008`: 'They announced the future on a Thursday morning, with pastries.' Best opening line in Part I.
- `sts.chapter2.b0011`: COMPANION 'the way you'd letter a children's hospital'. Satire through one concrete detail.
- `sts.chapter2.b0012`: The CPO's speech skewers corporate language without the narrator commenting.
- `sts.chapter2.b0015`: Credits metered by the render, the sovereignty tier, 'a coin slot bolted to the side'. Keep this coin slot and drop the one in b0100.
- `sts.chapter2.b0017`: He recognizes his own whiteboard diagram on the slide. 'His name was in those commits.' Complicity made physical.
- `sts.chapter2.b0121`: The flush of pride, the hand rising off his knee, and 'he was not in the audience for it. He was in the credits.' The emotional core of the chapter.
- `sts.chapter2.b0021`: The unnamed illustrator asks the question the whole book answers: 'what do they get?'
- `sts.chapter2.b0023`: 'because bonuses are also covered by terms of service' and the revenue line 'like a fence going in', which plants the enclosure argument.
- `sts.chapter2.b0123`: 'You don't have to build anything. You just have to build the fence.'
- `sts.chapter2.b0028`: 'That's the hedge,' Elijah said. 'I helped plant it.' The best line in the chapter, and it needs b0122 and b0123 to land.
- `sts.chapter2.b0124`: Closes the scene and hands the stages to the Foundations: 'what happens to people when the work is no longer theirs, but the bills still are.'
- `sts.chapter2.b0105`: Stage 2 in two plain sentences: 'When intelligence gets cheap, a lot of very profitable businesses stop being profitable. That's the panic.'
- `sts.chapter2.b0129`: 'The machine changed what's possible. The arrangement decides what happens next.' The book's thesis in twelve words.
- `sts.chapter2.b0119`: The most CT block in the chapter: washing machine, dishwasher, 'Give me that.', 'I don't believe most of them', 'So would I.' Direct, funny, honest.
- `sts.chapter2.b0115`: Stage 4 is a decision available now, not a phase to wait for. The chapter's actionable claim.
- `sts.chapter2.b0132`: 'Anger that's aimed at a machine is usually grief with a brick in its hand'. Pull-quote material that the chapter never pulls.
- `sts.chapter2.b0112`: Tone aside, it carries the anti-technocracy line the co-op arc depends on: knowing how to run the equipment is not a mandate to govern.
- `sts.chapter2.b0135`: Clean hand-off to Chapter 3 in two sentences.
- `sts.chapter2.b0139`: The P-04 mechanism, including the line that rules aimed at a specific harm can do real good.
- `sts.chapter2.b0117`: Practice item 2, 'Audit your own moats', is the sharpest piece of advice in Part I so far.


## Chapter 3: The Leap to ASI & the Machine Exodus (Stages 6 to 7)

**Verdict.** The Elijah half is some of the best writing in Part I and it is wired straight into the Conclusion. The argument half is one original idea (the machine leaves instead of fighting) asserted in about 300 words, then retracted in the next paragraph, followed by a Stage 7 grab bag and a precedent whose lesson doesn't match the chapter. Worst of all, the chapter's own reading route tells readers to skip it and gets wrong what they lose.

**Publishable as is?** No. Only with fixes. The scene (b0008 to b0025) could ship tomorrow. The Foundations can't: the reading route (b0098) misstates what skipping costs, Stage 6 never meets the obvious objection and then disowns itself, Stage 7 is three topics with no spine, P-05 teaches a lesson about decay in a chapter about departure, the same Falcon Heavy launch appears twice, and the chapter heading carries an en dash between the stage numbers against CT's no-dash rule. For the 50,000-word core, keep the scene and roughly 300 words of argument (b0086, b0063, b0065, b0087, b0067, b0089). By the chapter's own admission in b0098, the rest is the most cuttable argument in Part I.

### Failure modes

- **critical**: The reading route invites readers to skip Chapters 3 and 4 and understates the cost. The mother call and the Hollis sale are planted here and fire later: Ch12 (the Hollis outfit), Ch13 (the stove photo to his mother), and the Conclusion's best moment, sts.conclusion.b0056 (the 9:40 Sunday call, the radio and the sink, the pot in the rack, 'her frame', Manny and the pressure switch). A reader who takes the route hits the ending's emotional payoff without its setup. Second consequence for the cut: the route tells the truth about the argument. Nothing downstream depends on the Stage 6 to 7 Foundations. (sts.chapter3.b0098, sts.chapter3.b0014, sts.chapter3.b0015, sts.chapter3.b0017, sts.chapter3.b0021, sts.chapter3.b0085, sts.chapter3.b0023) Quote (CT, author voice): "You'll miss a stretch of Elijah's story, but nothing the later chapters argue depends on Stages 6 to 9."
- **major**: The chapter's central and most original idea, the exodus, is never tested against the objection every singularity regular will raise: a capable optimizer doesn't need war to take a planet's resources or to ignore us the way a road crew ignores an anthill. The chapter skips that objection, then disowns its own scenario two paragraphs later. That's 300 words of assertion and 60 words of apology, which leaves a reader not knowing what CT actually believes. (sts.chapter3.b0063, sts.chapter3.b0065, sts.chapter3.b0068, sts.chapter3.b0088) Quote (CT, author voice): "Now the honest part. None of that is a finding."
- **major**: Stage 7 crams three topics into 350 words with no through-line: an AI-medicine caveat, a one-paragraph Transhumanist/Naturalist split, and a simulation teaser that exists only to point at Chapter 4. The AI-medicine caveat duplicates the Introduction, which already said it (sts.introduction.b0113, CT: 'A model that predicts a protein's structure hasn't cured a patient.'). The section's own heading is a list of three topics. (sts.chapter3.b0057, sts.chapter3.b0079, sts.chapter3.b0080, sts.chapter3.b0082, sts.chapter3.b0072) Quote (CT, author voice): "One is a research tool; the other is an experimental drug candidate. Neither result, by itself, puts an approved treatment in a pharmacy."
- **major**: P-05 teaches the wrong lesson for this chapter. The chapter is about a machine that leaves; the precedent is about a state that abandoned its own fleet, and about a visitor who captured a king. The mechanism (b0075) and rule (b0076) carry two unrelated morals: capabilities decay, and ask who set the destination. Practice item 2, the 'frontier hour', has nothing to do with Zheng He at all. A reader can't tell whether the fleet stands for the machine, for humanity, or for their own lapsed guitar lessons. (sts.chapter3.b0093, sts.chapter3.b0075, sts.chapter3.b0076, sts.chapter3.b0078) Quote (CT, author voice): "And a capability nobody keeps up doesn't wait in reserve. It decays."
- **major**: Fact-check hedges have leaked into the prose. Nearly every argument paragraph carries a disclaimer clause, and the precedent apologizes for itself twice. This is the defensive register of a book bracing for reviewers, not the voice of someone telling you what's coming. (sts.chapter3.b0080, sts.chapter3.b0088, sts.chapter3.b0091, sts.chapter3.b0092, sts.chapter3.b0097) Quote (CT, author voice): "The reasons were political and expensive and are still argued over; China didn't stop trading or sailing, and one budget decision doesn't explain five centuries."
- **minor**: The narrative lands the same beat three times. b0085 explains the failed call. b0024 restates it as the exodus-as-distance thought. b0025 writes it in the notebook a third time. The Voice Guide's narrative rule is that the scene never announces its lesson. b0085 has to stay because the Conclusion quotes its 'frame' idea, so b0024 and b0025 compete for one slot. (sts.chapter3.b0085, sts.chapter3.b0024, sts.chapter3.b0025) Quote (Elijah (narrated thought)): "He thought: it won't be dramatic. That's what nobody understands."
- **minor**: The 'but dinner' puncture appears twice in this chapter and twice more in Chapter 4 (b0072, b0083). By the fourth time it reads like the book apologizing for having an imagination. (sts.chapter3.b0060, sts.chapter3.b0073) Quote (CT, author voice): "None of this is a prerequisite for dinner."
- **minor**: The 'In this chapter' bullets spend Elijah's punchline before the scene can earn it. 'Compound interest on cognition.' appears in bullet 1 and again as the payoff of b0013. Bullet 2 is a teaser, not a claim with teeth. (sts.chapter3.b0060, sts.chapter3.b0013) Quote (CT, author voice): "Recursive self-improvement is the tipping point: a process that upgrades the thing doing the upgrading. Compound interest on cognition."
- **minor**: Production redundancy: the same Falcon Heavy launch appears twice, once as a photo (b0002) and once as an AI collage (b0096), and the collage's caption hedges the scenario a fourth time. The chapter heading also carries an en dash between the stage numbers. (sts.chapter3.b0002, sts.chapter3.b0083, sts.chapter3.b0096, sts.chapter3.b0097, sts.chapter3.b0001) Quote (caption, editorial voice): "the scenario remains speculative."

### What readers would complain about

- *newcomer*: "The book told me I could skip chapters 3 and 4, so I did. Then the ending hangs on a phone call from chapter 3 that I never read. Thanks."
- *singularity regular*: "His one original idea is that superintelligence just leaves. No engagement with instrumental convergence, no answer to why it wouldn't strip Earth on the way out. Then he says 'None of that is a finding.' So why is it a chapter?"
- *skeptic*: "Every claim comes wrapped in three disclaimers. By the end I couldn't tell you what the author actually thinks."
- *critic*: "The fiction is good, genuinely good. The essay stapled behind it reads like it was written by a committee scared of a fact-checker."
- *history buff*: "Zheng He gets a tidy encyclopedia summary, two apologies, and then a homework assignment to pick my old instrument back up. What does any of it have to do with robots leaving Earth?"
- *hostile podcast host*: "So the survival plan is: the robots pack their bags and leave. Incredible. Next chapter."

### Weak writing

- `sts.chapter3.b0013` "because the room had done the thing rooms do when you stand up too fast, except he was sitting down." : Writerly cleverness that deflates the vertigo it describes; the 'except' clause explains its own joke.
- `sts.chapter3.b0080` "That chain is where hope turns into medicine. A model's fluent answer isn't a substitute for it." : Lecture tone and abstract nouns. About half of a 125-word paragraph is caveat, and this line repeats the Introduction.
- `sts.chapter3.b0082` "A healthier future is more room to live, not an order to become a different kind of human." : 'Not X, but Y' plus stacked abstractions; reads like a policy statement.
- `sts.chapter3.b0082` "Both camps will contain thoughtful people, and both will contain zealots." : Both-sides filler. True of every group of humans ever, so it says nothing.
- `sts.chapter3.b0088` "Capability and care are separate questions, and I'd be irresponsible to dress my hope up as a theorem." : Defensive academic drift; the author is arguing with a reviewer, not talking to the reader.
- `sts.chapter3.b0089` "If the machine left tomorrow, the great illusion would shatter." : Cliche ('the great illusion would shatter') right in front of the chapter's best line.
- `sts.chapter3.b0068` "Maybe it's the sort of neighbor that steps in before somebody presses the big red button." : Cliche ('big red button'), and part of a three-'maybe' run that hedges by rhythm.
- `sts.chapter3.b0068` "That's the hope the phrase *mutually assured survival* is trying to name" : Coins a phrase, says it 'is trying to name' something, then drops it forever. Weak verb, orphaned idea.
- `sts.chapter3.b0065` "It wastes resources, time, and energy" : List of three for rhythm; 'resources' already contains the other two.
- `sts.chapter3.b0075` "A capability lives only as long as somebody pays for it, practices it, and keeps the equipment sound." : Template list of three, in the Greene register.
- `sts.chapter3.b0074` "Keep the horizon wide enough for imagination and the next task narrow enough to check." : Balanced fortune-cookie construction; sounds wise, does no work.
- `sts.chapter3.b0091` "That's the voyagers' own account, not the story told by the people on the receiving end." : 'Not X, but Y' hedge that ends the precedent's best paragraph on a footnote.
- `sts.chapter3.b0011` "The lesson wasn't that growth was fast. The lesson was that the curve *lies to you* at the beginning." : The narrator states the moral inside the scene, in 'not X, but Y' form. It's earned here, but it's the pattern the Voice Guide warns about.
- `sts.chapter3.b0060` "the machine's answer to a hostile planet isn't war. It's exit." : 'Not X, but Y' as a teaser bullet, and the line is repeated almost verbatim as b0062 ('Here's the scenario I keep coming back to.').

### Not in your voice

- `sts.chapter3.b0088` "A capacity to travel isn't a motive for leaving, and nobody can tell you what a mind like that would value. The work of making powerful systems accountable doesn't stop because a hopeful scenario exists." : CT's 2024 draft never apologizes for a scenario. It says 'the singularity isn't just coming, it's here' and 'hint: we’re a LOT closer than you think' and moves on. Calling a paragraph 'the honest part' implies the rest wasn't. This is the fact-check harness talking, not CT.
- `sts.chapter3.b0082` "Everyone keeps the right to the answer *no*, including to a cure." : Rights language from a policy memo. The 2024 CT handles the same tension with a joke and a shrug ('maybe just maybe, we'll even learn to love our robot overlords. Kidding. Mostly.').
- `sts.chapter3.b0076` "Examine what sustains a capability before assuming it will persist, and ask who set the destination before admiring the ship." : Bold-label aphorism template ('The rule.'). Nothing in the 2024 draft is built like this; CT lands on short punches ('know it’s already over.'), not two-clause maxims.
- `sts.chapter3.b0092` "But the great state-sponsored fleets didn't sail again, and the skills and ships that made them possible weren't kept ready." : Historian's register, qualified and passive. The 2024 voice is second person and physical ('What if the shovel dug its own hoe?').
- `sts.chapter3.b0080` "Turning a promising candidate into medicine still takes testing, manufacturing, and access work." : Grant-proposal vocabulary ('access work'). CT's 2024 science paragraph is 'Imagine a world where Einstein is your entry-level tech support.'

### Where a newcomer gets lost

- `sts.chapter3.b0086`: 'ASI' is in the chapter title but isn't defined until this block, 1,200 words of story later.
- `sts.chapter3.b0009`: 'three weeks after the all-hands' and 'the resurrected tower' assume Chapters 1 and 2. In a cut book, those setups have to survive or these references dangle.
- `sts.chapter3.b0085`: 'no sentence for the realtors either' assumes Chapter 0's Bear Flag scene.
- `sts.chapter3.b0068`: 'mutually assured survival' riffs on mutually assured destruction without saying so, and the phrase is never used again.
- `sts.chapter3.b0082`: 'Transhumanists' and 'Naturalists' are capitalized like established movements but get one sentence each.
- `sts.chapter3.b0063`: 'Its improvement loop closes' depends on the scene's explanation (b0084). If the cut drops the scene, the argument loses its only plain explanation of recursive self-improvement.
- `sts.chapter3.b0098`: 'Stages 6 to 9' assumes the reader is holding the nine-stage map from the Introduction and Chapter 2, which the route invites them to stop following.

### Skim problems

- 'The Foundations' heading (b0027) is followed immediately by another H2 (b0061), so the table of contents shows an empty section.
- 1,181 words of narrative come before the first argument heading, and nothing marks where the scene ends and the claim begins.
- The same Falcon Heavy launch appears twice (b0002 and b0096) with two captions.
- The Stage 7 heading lists three topics, so a skimmer can't tell which one is the claim.
- The 'In this chapter' bullets don't map to sections: bullet 3 (capability vs motive) is buried mid-paragraph in b0088, and bullet 4 is a 36-word coda.
- P-05 practice item 3 hides its actual instruction in italics inside a long conditional sentence.

### Best blocks

- `sts.chapter3.b0011`: Star thistle as the exponential: the clearest newcomer explanation of the curve anywhere in the book, in concrete farm terms. Paid off in Ch16 b0120 ('the one that lies to you at the beginning').
- `sts.chapter3.b0084`: Explains closing the loop in plain words and ties the Chapter 1 tower to the Chapter 5 thermodynamics ('It was watts and silicon').
- `sts.chapter3.b0013`: 'Compound interest on cognition.' A line you could tattoo.
- `sts.chapter3.b0014`: Opens the mother call ('9:40 on Sunday'), which the Conclusion echoes word for word.
- `sts.chapter3.b0015`: The radio, the water, the pot in the rack: all fired again in sts.conclusion.b0056.
- `sts.chapter3.b0017`: Plants the Hollis sale (pays off in Ch12) and Manny's pressure switch (pays off in the Conclusion).
- `sts.chapter3.b0019`: Elijah trying to say the exodus to his mother: the thesis delivered as a human failure, before the argument states it. A dark mirror of CT's own 2024 image, 'Your mom doesn't call you for tech help anymore; she's got Einstein on speed dial.'
- `sts.chapter3.b0021`: The mother's 'It's just tools, baby.' The most believable skeptic in the book, given love instead of contempt.
- `sts.chapter3.b0085`: 'Her frame had room in it for tools, weather, blight, banks' and 'There was no sentence he could build that would carry it' are the setups for the Conclusion's 'her frame had always had room in it for everything that mattered'.
- `sts.chapter3.b0023`: 'Tell Manny it's the pressure switch.' Perfect deflection, paid off in the Conclusion.
- `sts.chapter3.b0087`: 'It packs its bags.' CT's 2024 dark humor ('So the robots have taken over. Congratulations! You've lost.') at its best.
- `sts.chapter3.b0067`: 'we weren't even worth destroying. We were just something it outgrew.' The insult that makes the scenario stick.
- `sts.chapter3.b0089`: 'The threat was never man versus machine. It's always been man versus man.' The book's thesis, and the same idea as CT's 2024 'at its heart, the Singularity is about humanity.'
- `sts.chapter3.b0086`: The newcomer's only definition of ASI, plus the weather-forecast framing that licenses everything after it.


## Chapter 4: Universal and Multiverse Apex (Stages 8 to 9)

**Verdict.** The LADDER.xlsx scene is a small masterpiece, and its lesson (your map ends before the territory does) is the whole chapter. Everything after the scene restates that lesson in the author's voice, adds a LaTeX equation and a probability seminar the chapter itself calls irrelevant to dinner, and tells the reader to 'remember' a Chapter 2 scene that doesn't exist. Strip the scene out and there's nothing left a newcomer needs except P-06's rule.

**Publishable as is?** No. b0062 tells the reader to 'Remember' tailored suits and a boardroom table that Chapter 2 never shows, and that alone blocks publication. Beyond it, the roughly 900 words of Foundations are the most cuttable stretch in the book: Stages 8 and 9 are asserted and retracted, the simulation section ends by declaring itself beside the point, and b0082 explains the scene in the author's voice. For the 50,000-word core, keep the scene, b0065 whole (for its last sentence) if any Stage 8 text survives, and P-06's mechanism, rule and practice (b0089, b0090, b0091).

### Failure modes

- **critical**: Phantom callback. Chapter 2's Stage 2 is a Commerce Department license and 'an incumbent with a legal pad'. No tailored suits, no boardroom table, no pounding executives anywhere in the book. The only 'tailored' in the annotated text is Reuben's coat in Ch9. The reader is told to remember something they never read. (sts.chapter4.b0062) Quote (CT, author voice): "Remember the people in tailored suits drafting a license requirement? The executives pounding the boardroom table?"
- **major**: The chapter argues itself down to zero. It announces speculation (b0058), footnotes it (b0065), admits the map runs off the paper (b0068), hedges the math (b0079, b0070), and then declares the whole simulation section irrelevant (b0072). If it changes nothing, a reader is entitled to ask why it takes 2,500 words. (sts.chapter4.b0058, sts.chapter4.b0065, sts.chapter4.b0068, sts.chapter4.b0079, sts.chapter4.b0070, sts.chapter4.b0072) Quote (CT, author voice): "None of which changes what's for dinner."
- **major**: The author walks back onstage to explain the scene. The Voice Guide says plainly: 'If a narrative passage ends by explaining what it meant, cut the explanation.' b0016 to b0020 already said it in Elijah's own notebook, the caption b0093 explains it again, and b0022 signposts the next chapter. (sts.chapter4.b0082, sts.chapter4.b0093, sts.chapter4.b0022) Quote (CT, author voice): "The important move in Elijah's night with the spreadsheet isn't the units error. It's his willingness to find the edge of his own picture and stop pretending the map continues."
- **major**: The simulation section is a seminar inside a survival book: 392 words, a Bostrom list, a LaTeX formula, 'nulliparous', and hedges on hedges. CT's 2024 draft handled simulation in two joking sentences ('You're lines of computer code in a simulation.'), which is the right weight for this book. The LaTeX may also render as raw markup in plain formats. (sts.chapter4.b0080, sts.chapter4.b0069, sts.chapter4.b0076, sts.chapter4.b0073, sts.chapter4.b0077, sts.chapter4.b0078, sts.chapter4.b0081, sts.chapter4.b0079, sts.chapter4.b0070, sts.chapter4.b0071) Quote (CT, author voice, presenting Kipping's model): "$$P(\text{base reality} | \text{simulations exist}) = 1 / (N_{\text{sim}} + 1)$$"
- **minor**: A physics ceiling is claimed without a name or a source, in a chapter that cites everything else. A physics-literate reader sees the hand wave immediately. (sts.chapter4.b0066) Quote (CT, author voice): "There's a ceiling to how much computation can exist before you run into the boundaries of space and time themselves."
- **minor**: Triple return-to-Earth. Elijah's notebook (b0020) already says 'Work the near end'. The author then says it twice more (b0072, b0083), on top of Chapter 3's two versions. (sts.chapter4.b0020, sts.chapter4.b0072, sts.chapter4.b0083) Quote (CT, author voice): "You don't need an answer about the ultimate nature of the universe to care whether your neighbor can eat."
- **minor**: 'USI' is a coinage, not a standard term. It appears in a heading before it's defined, and a regular will read it as invented jargon dressed as a field term. (sts.chapter4.b0059, sts.chapter4.b0061) Quote (CT, author voice): "becomes a universal superintelligence, a USI"
- **minor**: Mechanics: the chapter heading carries an en dash between the stage numbers, against CT's no-dash rule. (sts.chapter4.b0001)

### What readers would complain about

- *newcomer*: "There is an actual equation in a book about surviving AI. I closed it."
- *singularity regular*: "Bostrom and Kipping, summarized for the thousandth time. I've read both papers. Nothing new, and he admits it doesn't matter."
- *physicist*: "'There's a ceiling to how much computation can exist', he says. Which ceiling? Name it or don't bring it up."
- *careful reader*: "'Remember the people in tailored suits'? I don't. I went back to Chapter 2 and neither does it."
- *critic*: "The spreadsheet scene is wonderful, and then the author walks back onstage to tell me what it meant. Twice."
- *hostile podcast host*: "Chapter 4: the robots rearrange the stars. Also he doesn't know. Also none of this changes what's for dinner. His words. So why am I reading it?"

### Weak writing

- `sts.chapter4.b0064` "We reached for a leash and closed our hand on infinity." : Purple. Grand abstraction that sounds like a trailer voiceover.
- `sts.chapter4.b0061` "It stops being an artificial superintelligence orbiting a pale blue dot" : Borrowed cliche (Sagan's 'pale blue dot'), followed in the same sentence by a second cliche, 'a rounding error'.
- `sts.chapter4.b0067` "Branches of physics we lack the hardware to comprehend. Pocket universes. Simulations so vast that everything we know could be a forgotten line in one of its subroutines." : Three fragments stacked for rhythm in place of an argument.
- `sts.chapter4.b0068` "An unknown possibility isn't an engineering plan. It's a good place to stand and look up." : 'Not X, but Y' plus a soft landing that blunts the admission before it.
- `sts.chapter4.b0065` "Or more lives with room for curiosity, affection, art, rest, and adventure?" : Five-item list as padding; it dilutes the strong line that follows it.
- `sts.chapter4.b0079` "Those are assumptions, not a census of universes. *If.*" : Hedge, then a one-word italic sentence for drama.
- `sts.chapter4.b0070` "The result depends on those assumptions. It isn't a measured probability that our world is artificial." : A second hedge on the same model, one block after the first.
- `sts.chapter4.b0078` "and if they do, the arithmetic is brutal:" : Oversells the setup: 'brutal' is followed two blocks later by 'below fifty percent'. A whiplash with no payoff.
- `sts.chapter4.b0087` "kept making breakfast, falling in love, and planting gardens." : List of three for rhythm, and yet another food-as-sanity beat.
- `sts.chapter4.b0088` "There was no single reaction and no single ending." : A hedge that undercuts the mechanism in the very next block.
- `sts.chapter4.b0056` "The right response isn't paralysis. It's humility, and a shorter horizon." : 'Not X, but Y' in the chapter bullets; the bullet runs three sentences.
- `sts.chapter4.b0022` "That envelope, the near end arriving on schedule, is where the next chapter begins." : Signposting that turns the scene's last image into a table of contents.

### Not in your voice

- `sts.chapter4.b0065` "The honest footnote: *universal* isn't a measurement. More computation still runs into energy, matter, error, and the speed of light, and nothing about a good language model today proves this expansion must happen, or that it would be kind." : Reviewer-proofing. The 2024 CT commits ('We’ll explore how close we are to crossing that threshold and what signs to watch for (hint: we’re a LOT closer than you think)'). He doesn't footnote his own speculation in the body.
- `sts.chapter4.b0058` "It should also tell you when it's left observation behind." : Asks the reader's permission to imagine. The 2024 draft opens with brains in jars and alien sims and never asks.
- `sts.chapter4.b0070` "Under his choice to give the two hypotheses equal prior weight, the calculated probability that we're simulated stays below fifty percent, approaching that limit as the hypothetical number of simulations grows." : A journal-abstract sentence. The 2024 CT treats simulation as a gag that sets up 'Regardless of the true nature of reality, it is happening to you.'
- `sts.chapter4.b0086` "and named the churchmen who'd urged him on, Cardinal Nicolaus Schönberg and Bishop Tiedemann Giese among them." : Name-stacking in a history-paper register; nothing in CT's own draft reads like this.
- `sts.chapter4.b0090` "Leave room for meaning without making humanity the center of every explanation." : Abstract, committee-balanced. CT's 2024 register would say it with a joke or a profanity, not a seminar clause.

### Where a newcomer gets lost

- `sts.chapter4.b0062`: Calls back to a scene that doesn't exist; a newcomer will assume they missed something.
- `sts.chapter4.b0081`: LaTeX notation with no plain-language reading, and the same N_sim notation recurs in b0079.
- `sts.chapter4.b0070`: 'nulliparous' gets a gloss, but it's a speed bump chosen for show.
- `sts.chapter4.b0060`: 'upload or stay in the meat' assumes mind uploading, which Chapter 3 never names (it says 'to merge, to upgrade').
- `sts.chapter4.b0012`: 'the Bear Flag glow' needs Chapter 0. The present-tense aside 'He is not proud of this.' (and 'He wants that noted' in b0017) only makes sense once the Conclusion reveals Elijah wrote the book; before that, a first-timer reads it as a tense error.
- `sts.chapter4.b0016`: 'the map is not the territory' is credited to an unnamed 'philosopher' (Korzybski), so a newcomer can't follow it up.
- `sts.chapter4.b0010`: 'Half an order of magnitude a year' is unexplained for a reader without the math.

### Skim problems

- Another empty 'The Foundations' heading (b0024), followed immediately by b0057.
- Five H2s in about 900 words of Foundations, so a skimmer gets headings without claims.
- The only actionable content in the chapter, P-06's practice, is its last 139 words.
- The illustration and caption (b0092, b0093) sit between the scene's climax and its final line (b0022), breaking the scene.
- Bullet 3 of 'In this chapter' runs three sentences and buries the takeaway, 'a shorter horizon'.
- The 221-word b0016 is the longest block in the three chapters and carries the scene's whole turn, so a skimmer misses it.

### Best blocks

- `sts.chapter4.b0008`: 'a religion with formatting problems.' The best opening line in Part I.
- `sts.chapter4.b0013`: The joules versus joules-per-second error, 'off by a factor of time itself.' A concrete, funny, true failure.
- `sts.chapter4.b0015`: '*Fixing it wouldn't make it true.*' Elijah's turn in six words.
- `sts.chapter4.b0016`: 'a guess wearing a number's clothes', the mural, and the eyelash mites with their wrong cell Q31. The chapter's argument made physical and funny.
- `sts.chapter4.b0018`: Elijah's notebook: 'Anyone who says otherwise is selling the mural.' The chapter's thesis in the character's voice.
- `sts.chapter4.b0020`: 'Work the near end, the part with weather and dirt and bills. That part I can farm.' The bridge from Part I speculation to the book's practical half.
- `sts.chapter4.b0021`: The fence line, and 'His grandmother had never once worried about the valley she couldn't farm.' Coffee at the window is paid off in Ch8 b0231.
- `sts.chapter4.b0065`: Only for its last sentence: 'A future with godlike machines and hungry people would be a spectacular failure of priorities.' A CT hammer worth keeping if any Stage 8 text survives.
- `sts.chapter4.b0089`: P-06 mechanism: 'A bigger universe has never crushed a human being's breakfast. It pressures the authorities that were charging rent on the smaller one.'
- `sts.chapter4.b0090`: The rule is the chapter's one transferable tool for a newcomer: translate 'AI makes human life meaningless' into asking 'whose authority requires the old map'.
- `sts.chapter4.b0091`: The undemotable list, and 'It makes you hard to recruit into other people's despair.' The only Monday action in the chapter.


## Chapter 5: The Thermodynamics of Survival

**Verdict.** This is the chapter the book's argument stands on (Chapter 1 calls it 'The book's one amendment'), and its spine is excellent: a $211 bill, a hundred-watt human, separate ledgers, free at the door like a library, and a horse priced in its own currency. It's also two chapters under one title. A 1,130-word essay on moral panics and cyberdecks reruns the Preface, contradicts Chapter 7 on Sousa, duplicates Appendix E, overclaims a Raspberry Pi as a counterattack, and scolds the older readers most likely to buy the book. Then the energy audit appears twice.

**Publishable as is?** Only with fixes. The narrative, the energy argument and P-07 are close to publishable. The blockers: Sousa is mocked here as a panic (b0164, b0140) and defended in Chapter 7 (sts.chapter7.b0190, CT: 'He wasn't a crank.'); the audit is duplicated (b0156 and b0163 item 1); the efficiency table doesn't show what the sentence above it claims and is disclaimed twice; and caption b0166 carries an en dash. For the 50,000-word core, the cyberdeck run (b0133 to b0153) is the biggest single cut available in Part I. If any of it stays, keep b0141, b0151 and b0153 and let Appendix E carry the anatomy. Note that b0127 ends by pointing at the kids, so its last two sentences dangle if the section goes.

### Failure modes

- **major**: The book contradicts itself on Sousa. Here he's lined up next to 'Dungeons & Dragons was Satanic' as a moral-panic fool. Chapter 7 (sts.chapter7.b0190) says 'He wasn't a crank,' and its P-10 shows that recorded sound really did empty the orchestra pits. The details drift too: here his band is 'one of the best-selling recording acts', while Chapter 7 says 'one of the most recorded acts' and that 'Sousa himself rarely entered the recording studio.' b0141's 'grain of something real' doesn't repair it. (sts.chapter5.b0164, sts.chapter5.b0140, sts.chapter5.b0141, sts.chapter5.b0151) Quote (CT, author voice, quoting John Philip Sousa (real person)): "warning that the phonograph would wither the human voice: "the vocal cords will be eliminated by a process of evolution." His own band was already one of the best-selling recording acts in the country."
- **major**: One title, two chapters. Of 4,114 words, about 1,130 are a youth-culture essay. The panic history reruns the Preface, which b0120 admits outright. The cyberdeck anatomy and the Gibson coinage duplicate Appendix E, which b0146 also admits. The chapter's title promises thermodynamics; four of its eleven headings are about kids. (sts.chapter5.b0133, sts.chapter5.b0120, sts.chapter5.b0117, sts.chapter5.b0118, sts.chapter5.b0164, sts.chapter5.b0140, sts.chapter5.b0144, sts.chapter5.b0119, sts.chapter5.b0146) Quote (CT, author voice): "You already met the novel panic at the end of the Preface. It had company."
- **major**: The Tiffany Doctrine aims its heat at a segment of the readership. The Voice Guide's standard is anger at systems, never at the reader. Older readers are a core audience for a preparedness book, and they get warned it 'is going to sting', addressed as 'Grandpa', and handed a strawman who complains about TV antennas. b0151, the invitation to teach, is the redemption, and it should have been the whole section. (sts.chapter5.b0148, sts.chapter5.b0149, sts.chapter5.b0150) Quote (CT, author voice): "So: Grandpa. I'm sorry Tiffany wasn't around when they put a man on the moon."
- **major**: Overclaim. The chapter has just shown 485 terawatt-hours of data-centre demand and then calls a hobbyist computer in a case 'The counterattack'. b0150 also confuses a cyberdeck (a computer) with a laundry-folding robot, and b0146 says it's 'this whole book at model-railroad scale'. A hostile reader gets a free punchline. (sts.chapter5.b0096, sts.chapter5.b0146, sts.chapter5.b0150, sts.chapter5.b0125) Quote (CT, author voice): "The counterattack starts small and already has a name: the cyberdeck. The kids are building it."
- **major**: The energy audit appears twice. The chapter's closing practice repeats the 'Step one' audit from a page earlier, so the chapter ends with two action lists back to back and overlapping items. (sts.chapter5.b0154, sts.chapter5.b0156, sts.chapter5.b0157, sts.chapter5.b0158, sts.chapter5.b0163) Quote (CT, author voice): "1. Run the energy audit from this chapter on paper"
- **major**: The efficiency table doesn't support the claim above it. Human muscle (14 to 27 percent) overlaps combustion engines (10 to 50 percent), and only the motor row makes the point. The table is sourced to Wikipedia, then caveated twice (b0121, b0176), the second time saying the rows shouldn't be compared directly. The jab also hits the wrong camp: tech-utopians are the people who argue loudest that human bodies are inefficient. (sts.chapter5.b0108, sts.chapter5.b0137, sts.chapter5.b0121, sts.chapter5.b0176) Quote (CT, author voice): "Here's a hard truth the tech-utopians skip: as an engine, you're not very efficient."
- **minor**: The narrative explains itself, and the author lifts the character's line. The stove scene gets an editorial correction bolted onto its end (community, not self-sufficiency), then a 'not X, but Y' moral in b0129. b0158 takes Elijah's private thought from b0126 ('machines that did not love him') and turns it into the author's slogan. (sts.chapter5.b0128, sts.chapter5.b0129, sts.chapter5.b0126, sts.chapter5.b0158) Quote (narrator (Elijah scene)): "There had been a whole small arrangement around that stove. His grandmother knew what it needed and who she could call. That was part of the warmth."
- **minor**: 'free at the point of access' duplicates the Introduction, which already made the same clarification (sts.introduction.b0114, CT: 'Free doesn't mean costless, either.'). In a cut book one of them goes; b0132's library line is the better of the two. (sts.chapter5.b0131, sts.chapter5.b0132) Quote (CT, author voice): "When I say food should be free at the point of access, I don't mean steel, land, labor, or electricity stop costing money."
- **minor**: 'Somebody owns the meter' is a tic: twice in this chapter (b0113, b0171) and once in Chapter 1 (sts.chapter1.b0129). A slogan repeated three times stops sounding like an insight and starts sounding like a catchphrase. (sts.chapter5.b0113, sts.chapter5.b0171) Quote (CT, author voice): "Somebody owns the meter."
- **minor**: Redundant visuals and an off-point statistic. There are two Sun images, one an AI cutout of the same SDO photo (b0002, b0172). The aside, the chart and its caption repeat the same two IEA numbers three times. The 280-fold figure is about price, as b0169 itself concedes, so it doesn't belong in an energy box. Caption b0166 carries an en dash in its year range. (sts.chapter5.b0002, sts.chapter5.b0172, sts.chapter5.b0173, sts.chapter5.b0169, sts.chapter5.b0174, sts.chapter5.b0175, sts.chapter5.b0166) Quote (CT, author voice): "This measures price at a benchmark threshold, not energy consumed per task."
- **minor**: Thin sourcing on a ridicule beat. The 1816 Times waltz quote comes secondhand through a FEE think-tank article, not the paper itself, and that's the kind of citation a hostile reviewer pulls on first. (sts.chapter5.b0117) Quote (citation, editorial): "The Times (July 1816), as quoted in John Hood, Hayek, Strauss, and the Political Waltz, FEE"

### What readers would complain about

- *older reader / prepper*: "I'm 64, I can solder, and this book called me Grandpa and warned me it was going to sting. I'm the one buying preparedness books. Pass."
- *careful reader*: "Chapter 5 says Sousa was a moral panic. Chapter 7 says he wasn't a crank and the musicians really did lose their jobs. Pick one."
- *hostile podcast host*: "His answer to gigawatt datacenters is a Raspberry Pi in an ammo can. 'The counterattack,' folks."
- *skeptic*: "He cites Wikipedia for his core efficiency table, then tells me not to compare the rows. Then what's the table for?"
- *newcomer*: "Single-board computer, e-ink, software-defined radio stick, LoRa, Meshtastic, MMLU. I don't know what any of these are and the book doesn't stop to tell me."
- *singularity regular*: "Horses and tractors as the AI-jobs analogy is the oldest bit in the genre. The Watt contract detail is nice, but it reads like he thinks he found it."
- *younger reader*: "Being called the 'continuity plan' by a guy who uses 'Tiffany' as the stock name for a girl with a soldering iron is a lot."

### Weak writing

- `sts.chapter5.b0101` "And no, I don't mean the "I'm really vibing with your aura" kind. Nobody ever stayed warm in January by aligning their chakras." : The exact 'Let's talk about X. And no, I don't mean...' throat-clearing template the Voice Guide flags in Chapter 13. It punches at a strawman mystic who has no part in the argument.
- `sts.chapter5.b0133` "I want to show you what the kids are building. And first, I need to show you a couple of centuries of adults being wrong about what the kids are building." : Three 'show you' clauses of throat-clearing before the content starts; padding.
- `sts.chapter5.b0126` "in a fine mist, everywhere, always, invisibly, to machines that did not love him." : Adverb pileup; three of the four modifiers say the same thing.
- `sts.chapter5.b0125` "The whole coming war, right there in one line of notebook arithmetic." : Overclaim; 'war' inflates a good observation that didn't need it.
- `sts.chapter5.b0105` "A heat engine operating in a complete cycle can't turn all the heat it takes in into work, and real machines dissipate energy through friction and other losses." : Textbook lecture; academic drift with an inline OpenStax citation.
- `sts.chapter5.b0112` "Thermodynamics checks a claim against the physical world. It doesn't tell you what a person deserves or who gets to eat." : Lecture tone, defending against a misreading nobody has made yet.
- `sts.chapter5.b0114` "Installed capacity isn't delivered service." : The third 'isn't' in a row, after two concrete ones; it lands abstract and breaks the run.
- `sts.chapter5.b0118` "The thing actually on the move wasn't anybody's face. It was women's freedom of movement." : Explains the joke in 'not X, but Y' form.
- `sts.chapter5.b0148` "Here's the part I need the older readers to sit with, because I'm on your side and this is going to sting." : Throat-clearing plus condescension: announcing that a scolding is coming and that it's for your own good.
- `sts.chapter5.b0150` "She needs your help and your patience while she builds a goddamn cyberdeck." : Spends part of the book's small profanity budget on scolding a reader, which isn't load-bearing.
- `sts.chapter5.b0150` "Your job is to help her handle frustration, not to hand her more of it." : 'Not X, but Y' wordplay that ends the paragraph on a lecture.
- `sts.chapter5.b0152` "The bicycle girls rode into the suffrage era." : Implied causation that a historian would contest; overclaim for rhythm.
- `sts.chapter5.b0152` "Tiffany and her cyberdeck aren't the collapse of civilization. They're the *continuity plan*." : 'Not X, but Y' again; the section uses the construction four times.
- `sts.chapter5.b0144` "Not picked off a menu. Designed. Fabricated. Owned the way you can only own something you could rebuild from a pile of its own parts." : Fragment stack for rhythm.
- `sts.chapter5.b0157` "the task, what it needs, who's responsible, what it still depends on from outside, and what happens during an interruption." : A five-item consultant checklist buried mid-sentence, with nothing to tell the reader which item matters.
- `sts.chapter5.b0156` "Keep the units straight: count the first in watts and hours, the subscriptions in dollars, the fuel by quantity, and doomscrolling in time you don't get back." : Fussy; the instruction is buried under its own bookkeeping.
- `sts.chapter5.b0129` "She hadn't been outside the world. She had known how to make a place in it." : The narrator states the scene's moral in 'not X, but Y' form.

### Not in your voice

- `sts.chapter5.b0176` "These rows have different boundaries. A motor's figure starts with electricity already delivered to it; a power plant's starts with fuel." : An engineering reviewer's caveat. The 2024 CT never qualifies a comparison; he swings ('Imagine a world where Einstein is your entry-level tech support. Really think about it.').
- `sts.chapter5.b0119` "Measure the board under your intended load, then add the screen, radios, storage, and power-conversion losses. Single-digit watts is a target for some builds, not a whole-system guarantee." : Spec-sheet hedging inside what should be the chapter's most fun list. The fact-check harness is writing here, not CT.
- `sts.chapter5.b0112` "Calling someone a meat engine can make the physics easy to feel. It must never make their worth depend on how cheaply they compete with a motor." : The author pre-empting a misquotation. The 2024 CT trusts the reader ('You're nothing more than a sim in an alien game.') and doesn't append a values disclaimer.
- `sts.chapter5.b0157` "Then pick one task you actually care about, food first if you can, and write a short inventory with the people who already do it" : Consultant and nonprofit-workshop register. CT's own imperatives are blunter ('grab your favorite drink and your favorite tinfoil hat').
- `sts.chapter5.b0105` "Energy can remain on the books while becoming less available to do work." : Physics-textbook phrasing; nothing in the 2024 draft explains a law before showing what it does to you.
- `sts.chapter5.b0128` "For a moment he wanted to count the cost as nothing but fuel and muscle. Then he remembered the borrowed trailer, the neighbor who helped when her shoulder went, the work of keeping a chimney clear." : An editorial correction wearing the narrative's clothes. The scene stops being about the stove and starts being about the book's thesis on community provision.

### Where a newcomer gets lost

- `sts.chapter5.b0119`: A jargon dump: single-board computer, e-ink bistability, software-defined radio stick, LoRa, Meshtastic, mesh messaging. None of it is defined.
- `sts.chapter5.b0144`: 'the "cyberspace deck" Case jacks into' reads as a typo unless you know Case is Neuromancer's protagonist.
- `sts.chapter5.b0169`: 'MMLU benchmark', 'GPT-3.5' and 'terawatt-hours' are given without a sense of scale.
- `sts.chapter5.b0137`: 'Combined-cycle gas plant' is undefined.
- `sts.chapter5.b0156`: 'Who's in your pod?' is the first use of 'pod' in the book, and it isn't defined.
- `sts.chapter5.b0120`: The panic history depends on the reader remembering the end of the Preface.
- `sts.chapter5.b0125`: 'The night the tower had cooked itself into a shutdown' depends on Chapter 1. If the cut keeps this scene, the Chapter 1 tower scene has to survive too.
- `sts.chapter5.b0129`: 'the way she used to put his hand on the madrones' depends on Chapter 0's grandmother and trees.

### Skim problems

- The title promises thermodynamics, but four of the eleven headings (b0138, b0139, b0143, b0147) are about kids and cyberdecks, with H3s nested under an H2, so the table of contents is uneven.
- Two Sun images open the chapter and the Foundations (b0002, b0172).
- The aside (b0169), the chart (b0174) and its caption (b0175) show the same two numbers three times.
- A skimmer who reads only the efficiency table gets a muddled message, and the two caveat paragraphs around it make that worse.
- The chapter ends with two action lists back to back (b0156 to b0158, then b0163) whose first items overlap.
- Bullet 2 of 'In this chapter' (b0096) crams three separate claims into one bullet.
- At 4,114 words this is the longest of the three chapters; the precedent alone is 634 words.

### Best blocks

- `sts.chapter5.b0009`: The $211.46 bill. The best hook in Part I: concrete, funny, true to life.
- `sts.chapter5.b0124`: 'printed on paper and mailed to his door by the utility company like a grade.' The gap between thinking about AI and running it, as one invoice.
- `sts.chapter5.b0125`: 'Intelligence was *energy, arranged*.' and gigawatts against sandwiches: the chapter's thesis as a single image.
- `sts.chapter5.b0126`: Plants the plug-in power meter habit that Chapter 15 inverts (per the Elijah Protocol), and 'a tenant who paid no rent'.
- `sts.chapter5.b0127`: 'His phone drew five to make him dumber.' Pure CT, the same irreverence as his 2024 draft.
- `sts.chapter5.b0128`: The Fisher stove, planted here and fired in Ch13 (first fire) and the Conclusion (the replacement firebrick). Its first two-thirds are the strongest physical writing in the chapter.
- `sts.chapter5.b0130`: 'He left his hand on it a little longer.' A quiet ending that trusts the reader.
- `sts.chapter5.b0017`: 'the first watt he ever took back.' The chapter's turn from despair to agency in one line.
- `sts.chapter5.b0102`: 'No algorithm can patch them out. No lobbyist can buy them off.' Argument-register hammer in CT's voice.
- `sts.chapter5.b0106`: The strawberry in February, and 'how far you live from your own dinner.' Supply chains made personal for a newcomer.
- `sts.chapter5.b0109`: 100 watts, 20 for the brain: the numbers the rest of the book leans on, including P-07's rule.
- `sts.chapter5.b0114`: Separate ledgers. 'A pile of zucchini isn't a year's diet. Power at noon isn't power during a cloudy week.' Field Manual at its best.
- `sts.chapter5.b0132`: 'A library has a budget and charges you nothing at the door.' The clearest answer in the book to the question of who pays for free food.
- `sts.chapter5.b0141`: The translation key ('a tool I don't understand is making someone I can't supervise powerful'). If any of the panic history survives, this is the line.
- `sts.chapter5.b0151`: Turns the scolding into an invitation: 'You know how to solder. You know why the fuse blows.' The only part of the Tiffany section that respects both readers.
- `sts.chapter5.b0153`: 'Your first machine doesn't need to be pretty. It needs to be yours. Build one with her.' A closer you could tattoo.
- `sts.chapter5.b0115`: 'James Watt had a marketing problem.' The best precedent opening in these three chapters.
- `sts.chapter5.b0160`: 'Sentiment just never appeared as a row in anybody's books.'
- `sts.chapter5.b0162`: 'The horse found out its price after the fact. You still get to set some of the terms.' The rule that makes the whole chapter actionable.


## Part II: How Humans React: The Cooperative Transition

**Verdict.** A 129-word divider that mostly works, but it uses up Chapter 6's best reveal before the chapter starts and introduces the wrong cast. It says 'nineteen households' twice and names Marta as 'the fabricator' before Chapter 6 lets Elijah walk into her shop not knowing who she is. The cast portrait includes Priya, who has no scene until Chapter 8, and leaves out Curtis, who drives the Chapter 7 fight and carries payoffs all the way to the Conclusion.

**Publishable as is?** Only with fixes. The heading carries two subtitles and two colons, and the figure plus caption (sts.part-2.b0009, b0010) give away the Chapter 6 reveal. For the core cut only sts.part-2.b0001 and sts.part-2.b0008 need to survive.

### Failure modes

- **minor**: Gives away Chapter 6's turn. Marta's 'Co-op. Nineteen households.' (sts.chapter6.b0014, Marta) is the moment the shop becomes the answer to Elijah's arithmetic, and by then the divider has told the reader twice and named her in alt text. (sts.part-2.b0008, sts.part-2.b0009, sts.part-2.b0010) Quote (caption, CT author voice): "The people the rest of this book is about. Nineteen households; these four you get to know."
- **minor**: Wrong cast in the portrait. Priya has no scene in Chapter 6 or 7. Curtis, who appears in Chapters 7, 9, 11, 16, 17, 18 and the Conclusion and fixes the check-engine light, is missing. A newcomer is told to track four people and meets a fifth who matters more in the very next chapter. (sts.part-2.b0009, sts.part-2.b0010)
- **minor**: The heading stacks two subtitles behind two colons, so a skimmer can't tell which one is the Part's promise. (sts.part-2.b0001) Quote (heading, CT author voice): "How Humans React: The Cooperative Transition"

### What readers would complain about

- *newcomer*: "The picture says these four are the people I'll get to know. Then the very next chapter's big fight is with some guy named Curtis who isn't even in the picture."
- *critic*: "Told me 'nineteen households' twice before the welder ever got to say it, so when she does it lands flat."

### Weak writing

- `sts.part-2.b0001` "How Humans React: The Cooperative Transition" : Heading (CT). Two subtitles joined by a colon inside a title that already has one. It reads like a syllabus entry rather than a promise.
- `sts.part-2.b0008` "This part follows that fear into the arrangements that produce it, and into a shed where nineteen households are trying something else." : CT author voice. 'The arrangements that produce it' is an abstract noun standing in for concrete ones (rent, payroll, the county's folder). The second half, the shed, is concrete and works. The first half is academic drift.

### Not in your voice

- `sts.part-2.b0008` "This part follows that fear into the arrangements that produce it" : CT's 2024 draft opens a section with a punch: 'So the robots have taken over. Congratulations!' and 'What the fuck is going on?' This is a course-description sentence. The block's opening ('A useful machine should give us something to look forward to.') is closer to him.

### Where a newcomer gets lost

- `sts.part-2.b0001`: 'The Cooperative Transition' is never defined here, and a reader who skipped Part I doesn't yet know a co-op is coming.

### Skim problems

- A skimmer sees a two-colon title and a cast portrait. The single sentence that states Part II's problem (sts.part-2.b0008) sits under a full-width image.
- The caption (sts.part-2.b0010) is the most quotable line on the page, and it is the one that gives away the Chapter 6 reveal.

### Best blocks

- `sts.part-2.b0008`: Its first two sentences state Part II's whole problem in plain words: 'A useful machine should give us something to look forward to. Instead, for a lot of people, it arrives as a threat to dinner and rent.' This is the one block a reader needs.
- `sts.part-2.b0001`: Structural. The book needs a Part II marker, though the title could lose one subtitle.


## Chapter 6: Is the Singularity a Done Deal?

**Verdict.** The opening scene (sts.chapter6.b0008 to b0025) is some of the best writing in the book, and almost all of it has to survive because it sets up payoffs in Chapters 9, 16, 18 and the Conclusion. The Foundations don't hold up. They run 1,796 words under seven headings, never answer the chapter's own title, show the same capability-versus-access idea three times, repeat their own statistics in a sidebar, and contradict the headline promise that nobody's money gets taken. The summary bullet promising survivors of an automation wave 'who aren't on LinkedIn' is never delivered.
