**Publishable as is?** No. The scene could ship today. The Foundations need about two thirds cut and one contradiction resolved (no redistribution vs. public funding). At 3,449 words the chapter reaches its roughly 1,700-word half by keeping the scene, the capability-access point once, the preservation thesis, Whose robots? with Wiener, the ladder, the fourth-floor apartment, and the Grain Trap's hook, mechanism and rule.

### Failure modes

- **critical**: The chapter title asks a question that the chapter answers in one hedged paragraph and then tells the reader to stop asking. sts.chapter6.b0113 says yes, then says the answer doesn't matter. The Grain Trap rule (b0077) and practice (b0078) cap 'whether' debates at five minutes, and the chapter title is a whether debate. None of the seven Foundations headings (b0090, b0097, b0103, b0124, b0045, b0110, b0105) is about whether the Singularity is settled. A reader who came for the title gets a chapter on food-access policy. (sts.chapter6.b0056, sts.chapter6.b0113, sts.chapter6.b0077, sts.chapter6.b0078) Quote (CT author voice): "You don't have to agree with me on either. This chapter works either way, because the part that matters, what happens next, isn't done at all."
- **major**: Contradiction at the thesis. b0101 promises no one's money gets taken and no redistribution. b0082 then has a public body fund the food service, which is tax money, and b0126 concedes that owners of servers, models and land decide what the machines serve. The hardest objection in the book ('Whose robots?') gets two historical citations (Wiener b0128, Ostrom b0129) and a forward pointer to Chapter 19 (b0130) instead of a mechanism. A hostile interviewer opens with this. (sts.chapter6.b0101, sts.chapter6.b0082, sts.chapter6.b0126, sts.chapter6.b0129, sts.chapter6.b0130) Quote (CT author voice): "We don't need to take anyone's money. We don't need a hostile redistribution of wealth, and we don't need to win every argument about economics first."
- **major**: The chapter repeats itself. Capability vs. access appears in a paragraph (b0092), a figure and caption (b0118, b0119), and a second figure and caption (b0122, b0123). The By the numbers aside (b0114 to b0117) repeats the ILO figure from b0094 and the 2x vs. 5x ratio from b0079, and says so in print. The Two kinds of fear typology (b0095 to b0099) is the same split Chapter 7 relabels as Entitled vs. Ready (sts.chapter7.b0170). Keep one of each. (sts.chapter6.b0092, sts.chapter6.b0118, sts.chapter6.b0119, sts.chapter6.b0122, sts.chapter6.b0123, sts.chapter6.b0114, sts.chapter6.b0115, sts.chapter6.b0116, sts.chapter6.b0117, sts.chapter6.b0095, sts.chapter6.b0096, sts.chapter6.b0098) Quote (CT author voice (sidebar)): "These are different measures, as the paragraph above explains."
- **major**: The chapter summary makes a promise the chapter doesn't keep. No Foundations block introduces, locates or quotes anyone who survived an earlier automation wave. The only such person is Marta in the fiction (b0089), and the bullet tells the reader to go find real ones without saying how. (sts.chapter6.b0057) Quote (CT author voice (chapter summary)): "Some people have already survived an automation wave. Find them. They aren't on LinkedIn."
- **major**: Fact-check caveats are bolted into the prose, which turns CT's argument into a committee voice. Each caveat is defensible on its own. Stacked up, they make the author sound like he's arguing with his own footnotes, and the punchline of each paragraph becomes a disclaimer. (sts.chapter6.b0079, sts.chapter6.b0107, sts.chapter6.b0121, sts.chapter6.b0085, sts.chapter6.b0094) Quote (caption, CT author voice): "These two selected groups do not represent every income category, and the comparison does not establish causation."
- **minor**: Evidence is in the wrong place. The section for people under twenty-five (b0045, b0104, b0079, b0080) is followed by a chart of food insecurity by income group (b0120, b0121) that says nothing about age. The young reader the section addresses gets a chart about someone else. (sts.chapter6.b0045, sts.chapter6.b0120, sts.chapter6.b0121)
- **minor**: The scene is explained twice: once by the narrator inside it (b0010 decodes the check-engine light) and again by the Foundations (b0091 restates what Elijah and Marta each bring). The voice guide's own rule is that the scene never announces its lesson. (sts.chapter6.b0010, sts.chapter6.b0091) Quote (CT author voice): "Elijah arrives with a forecast. Marta has a workshop, a history, and a bill for the welding gas. They need things from each other, but first he has to stop confusing his prediction with her future."
- **minor**: Point-of-view slip. Elijah is the POV character and hasn't been told her name, yet the narration gives her full name mid-scene. Marta never introduces herself. (sts.chapter6.b0088) Quote (narrator (Narrative register)): "because Marta Okonkwo had crossed her arms"
- **minor**: A recycled tic. b0010 repeats Chapter 0's 'He didn't have a plan. He wants that on the record.' (sts.chapter0.b0071) almost word for word, and the same move comes back at sts.chapter9.b0010. Three uses wear it out. (sts.chapter6.b0010) Quote (narrator (Narrative register)): "He had no plan. He wants that on the record too."
- **minor**: Overclaim, and the third restatement of the forecast. A model running on a desk is offered as evidence that 'we're already inside the Singularity', and the Thanksgiving 2027 date is restated after the Introduction (sts.introduction.b0088) and Chapter 1 (sts.chapter1.b0111) have both stated it. A singularity regular will call the evidence anecdotal. A newcomer will find the date repetitive. (sts.chapter6.b0113) Quote (CT author voice): "my evidence is sitting on a desk: a model that does real intellectual work, running on consumer hardware, with no data center behind it."
- **minor**: A forward reference that isn't defined where it's used. 'Rung 5' doesn't exist yet for a reader moving in order. (sts.chapter6.b0130) Quote (CT author voice): "That's the point of Rung 5 in [](sts:chapter19)"

### What readers would complain about

- *singularity regular*: "Chapter called 'Is the Singularity a Done Deal?' The answer is yes, because he has a model on his desk. Then twenty pages about food pantries."
- *hostile podcast host*: "Page one: we don't need to take anyone's money. Two pages later: a public body funds the food. With what, good intentions?"
- *skeptic*: "He forecasts AGI by next Thanksgiving, then quotes the ILO saying most jobs get transformed rather than replaced, and his response is 'Good.' Pick a lane."
- *newcomer*: "The realtors, the ingestion pipeline, the enclosure, the Bear Flag, Claypot, Rung 5. I needed a glossary to get through a road trip."
- *renter under 25*: "The section 'for people under twenty-five' gives me one housing ratio, then a chart about income and food that has nothing to do with my age, then a sidebar repeating the same ratio. Nothing to do about any of it."
- *critic*: "The chapter summary promised me people who survived an automation wave. They never show up."
- *bored reader*: "Same diagram twice. Capability isn't access. I got it the first time."

### Weak writing

- `sts.chapter6.b0091` "Elijah arrives with a forecast. Marta has a workshop, a history, and a bill for the welding gas." : CT author voice. Explains the scene the reader just watched, with a list of three for rhythm.
- `sts.chapter6.b0092` "But capability, deployment, ownership, and access are different events." : CT author voice. Four abstract nouns stacked, with no person in the sentence. The three examples that follow carry the idea better than this line does.
- `sts.chapter6.b0093` "Make a lost paycheck less able to turn an ordinary evening into an emergency." : CT author voice. Convoluted policy syntax: a paycheck that is 'less able to turn' an evening into something. The idea is 'losing your job shouldn't mean missing dinner.'
- `sts.chapter6.b0096` "When you're drowning, even a rescue boat can look like a threat. That's not a character flaw. It's what exhaustion does." : CT author voice. A cliché (drowning) followed by a 'not X, it's Y' correction.
- `sts.chapter6.b0098` "the vertigo of having to redefine themselves outside the job title that's been doing the defining." : CT author voice. Abstract nouns and a wordplay loop ('redefine' and 'doing the defining'). Academic drift.
- `sts.chapter6.b0100` "Here's the move that makes the fear unnecessary." : CT author voice. Throat-clearing signpost and an overclaim: no food plan makes the fear 'unnecessary', and the chapter's own caveats say so.
- `sts.chapter6.b0101` "We don't need to take anyone's money. We don't need a hostile redistribution of wealth, and we don't need to win every argument about economics first." : CT author voice. A 'We don't need' run of three for rhythm, and an overclaim that b0082 contradicts.
- `sts.chapter6.b0127` "Ownership has to reach the terms you depend on." : CT author voice. Abstract to the point of meaning nothing to a newcomer. Ownership doesn't 'reach' anything.
- `sts.chapter6.b0129` "Those findings give us a useful starting point for shared machines: examine who makes the rules, who enforces them, and how people settle disputes." : CT author voice. Academic drift ('a useful starting point') and a list of three. The Nobel is cited for authority instead of the story (fishermen, forests) being told.
- `sts.chapter6.b0130` "The same capability can serve different arrangements. We have work to do on both." : CT author voice. Vague ending with no clear referent: work on both what?
- `sts.chapter6.b0079` "The measures differ, a value then and a sale price now, but nobody reading them doubts the direction." : CT author voice. A defensive hedge as the last word of a stat paragraph, which has three inline citations already.
- `sts.chapter6.b0081` "A promise from a generous founder is welcome. A service that keeps running when the founder gets sick is stronger. A right you can still claim when an administrator says no is stronger still." : CT author voice. A rhetorical three-step ladder about a founder and an administrator the reader has never met. Pure abstraction.
- `sts.chapter6.b0082` "None of those words makes anything fair on its own. Together they describe work we already know how to start: assigning resources, responsibilities, and a usable claim on the result." : CT author voice. Stacked abstract nouns in grant-application prose.
- `sts.chapter6.b0085` "They can't stand in for every city, every climate, or every person who doesn't know somebody at the table." : CT author voice. A hedge built as a list of three for rhythm.
- `sts.chapter6.b0107` "The evidence points more than one way, which is what real evidence usually does." : CT author voice. A hedge that undercuts the precedent's own example and then congratulates itself for doing so.
- `sts.chapter6.b0078` "Watch how few people can switch questions. Be one who can." : CT author voice. Smug. It invites the reader to feel superior to the people around them, the contempt the voice guide warns against.
- `sts.chapter6.b0078` "Not to exit the system. Because the person with an alternative negotiates from a different posture than the person without one." : CT author voice. A 'not X, but Y' built from two sentence fragments.
- `sts.chapter6.b0010` "the *old* economy asserting itself, combustion and gaskets, problems you could hold." : Narrator. Explains the symbol. The light on the dashboard already says it, and it pays off in Chapter 9 without help.
- `sts.chapter6.b0014` "the look of someone who has hired and fired and buried, and something in the shop's whole arrangement must have loosened her" : Narrator. A list of three for rhythm, then a vague abstraction ('the shop's whole arrangement') where a reason should be.
- `sts.chapter6.b0022` "exactly like an engine after a long haul, exactly like a tower in a dark apartment. Elijah stood in it and felt the pitch die and something older stand up in its place." : Narrator. A double simile followed by an abstraction ('something older'). The scene works without the block.
- `sts.chapter6.b0054` "The tool wall. Shadow-boarded like an evidence display." : Caption. Repeats the simile from b0011 ('shadow-boarded so precisely it looked like an evidence display') two blocks later.
- `sts.chapter6.b0003` "Tools become useful through people who can operate, maintain, and share them." : Caption, CT author voice. States the chapter's moral before the story has earned it, as a list of three.

### Not in your voice

- `sts.chapter6.b0082` "Together they describe work we already know how to start: assigning resources, responsibilities, and a usable claim on the result." : CT in 2024 wrote 'If humanity could get its collective head out of its collective ass, we could be living in the Star Trek future or the Jetsons.' This reads like a grant application. No heat, no image, no person.
- `sts.chapter6.b0129` "Elinor Ostrom supplied evidence for another piece of the argument." : The 2024 draft used Frankenstein and the Golem to make a fear land emotionally. Here a source is introduced as a brick in a literature review. That's academic drift, not CT.
- `sts.chapter6.b0121` "These two selected groups do not represent every income category, and the comparison does not establish causation." : No human voice at all. It's a legal disclaimer. CT's original never hedged in the same breath as a claim. He swung ('hint: we're a LOT closer than you think').
- `sts.chapter6.b0093` "Make a lost paycheck less able to turn an ordinary evening into an emergency." : Bureaucratic imperative. The 2024 CT talks to one scared person ('You're afraid. I get it.'), not to a program design.
- `sts.chapter6.b0126` "When I talk about oligarchs, I mean that concentration of wealth and decision-making power." : A definitional lecture. The 2024 CT names the force and swings at it: 'Capitalism fights to the last breath and takes a lot of people with it.' This version defines the term and then goes quiet.
- `sts.chapter6.b0113` "You don't have to agree with me on either. This chapter works either way" : The 2024 CT made bold calls and owned them ('we're a LOT closer than you think'). Here the bold call and its retraction share a paragraph, and the retraction wins.
- `sts.chapter6.b0086` "But you learn who a speech is for by imagining giving it, and this one was for him." : A polished literary register that doesn't appear anywhere in the 2024 draft. It's good writing, but when CT rewrites for v0.12 he should know the fiction is a different hand from his solo voice.

### Where a newcomer gets lost

- `sts.chapter6.b0086`: 'the realtors, the ingestion pipeline with his name in the commits, the enclosure' are all callbacks to Chapters 0 to 2. A reader who skims Part I or opens here is lost in the first paragraph after the hook.
- `sts.chapter6.b0087`: 'The way he had at the Bear Flag' needs Chapter 0.
- `sts.chapter6.b0008`: 'eleven months after the toast' needs Chapter 0.
- `sts.chapter6.b0113`: 'my AGI date' and 'By this book's definition' assume the reader holds the Introduction's and Chapter 1's definitions. The chapter never restates what the Singularity means here in one line.
- `sts.chapter6.b0130`: 'Rung 5' is a Chapter 19 term, used here before it exists.
- `sts.chapter6.b0088`: 'Claypot' and 'Marta Okonkwo' both arrive with no introduction. The name in particular arrives before anyone in the scene says it.

### Skim problems

- Two headings sit back to back with no text between them: sts.chapter6.b0027 '## The Foundations' then sts.chapter6.b0090 '## The part we get to decide'.
- Seven H2 headings in 1,796 words of Foundations (The part we get to decide, Two kinds of fear, The preservation solution, Whose robots?, The ladder with the bottom rungs sawed off, Security is something you can rely on, A workshop is a beginning). Read in order, they don't answer the chapter title.
- The book's core thesis (sts.chapter6.b0101) sits under 'The preservation solution', a heading a skimmer can't decode.
- sts.chapter6.b0079 puts three inline citation links in the middle of one paragraph, so a skimmer hits URLs instead of the 2x vs. 5x punchline.
- The By the numbers box (sts.chapter6.b0114 to b0117) is two-thirds repetition. A skimmer who reads only the box gets one new number (47.9 million food insecure).
- The chapter summary (sts.chapter6.b0057) points to a section that doesn't exist, so a skimmer hunting for it finds nothing.

### Best blocks

- `sts.chapter6.b0008`: Narrator. 'Elijah quit on a Tuesday, eleven months after the toast, and the resignation meeting took four minutes because nobody argued.' One sentence carries the whole indifference of the new economy.
- `sts.chapter6.b0086`: Narrator. 'on-boarded by a person, off-boarded by a light' is one of the best lines in the book and the chapter's emotional entry point.
- `sts.chapter6.b0010`: Plants the check-engine light that Curtis fixes in sts.chapter9.b0185 and the reverse drive in sts.conclusion.b0055. Cutting it breaks two payoffs.
- `sts.chapter6.b0014`: Marta's introduction. Marta: 'We make what we can't afford to buy and fix what the market says to throw away.' That one line is the co-op's entire ethic.
- `sts.chapter6.b0088`: Elijah's pitch in Claypot grammar (scale, platform, app). Chapter 16 (sts.chapter16.b0048, narrator) brings 'the old grammar' back as the temptation he finally resists.
- `sts.chapter6.b0017`: Marta: 'You done?' Two words, and the pitch is over. Keep with b0018 ('He was done.').
- `sts.chapter6.b0019`: Marta: 'You know what doesn't scale? Nineteen households who all know whose kid is whose. That's not a limitation, college. That's the load-bearing wall.' Quoted back at sts.chapter16.b0018. The book's argument against scale lives here.
- `sts.chapter6.b0089`: Marta: 'So I know something you don't, which is what it's like *after*. And you know something I don't, which is apparently the schedule.' Sets up Chapter 8's 2009 story and names the exchange the whole co-op runs on.
- `sts.chapter6.b0025`: Marta: '"Swarf goes in the red bin," she said. "We'll see."' The broom pays off when Elijah hands it on in Chapter 18. Keep with b0023 and b0024.
- `sts.chapter6.b0092`: The capability-versus-access distinction is the hinge of the whole book. Keep this block and drop both figures that restate it.
- `sts.chapter6.b0099`: CT: 'It's to change the rule that makes the fear accurate.' This is the most important reframe in Part II, and it survives even if the typology around it goes.
- `sts.chapter6.b0101`: The book's thesis in plain words (use the robots for food and shelter, let people keep what they have). It must survive, but CT needs to settle the money contradiction with b0082 in v0.12.
- `sts.chapter6.b0102`: CT: 'That's food first, stated as plainly as I can state it.' The clean two-sided payoff for the overwhelmed and the status-invested.
- `sts.chapter6.b0125`: CT: 'Use the robots to grow the food. Fine. Whose robots?' Ten words that raise the hardest objection in CT's own voice. Keep with b0126.
- `sts.chapter6.b0128`: Wiener's 1949 letter to Reuther is the most surprising fact in the chapter, and it frames ownership as an old, serious idea rather than a fringe one.
- `sts.chapter6.b0080`: CT: 'Costs up. Bottom rungs going.' The sharpest few lines for the young reader. Keep with b0104.
- `sts.chapter6.b0084`: CT: 'A serious proposal has to reach that apartment too.' The book argues against its own privileged protagonist, which disarms a critic.
- `sts.chapter6.b0076`: CT: 'The first singularity was a seed.' Six words, and the best hook in the chapter.
- `sts.chapter6.b0109`: The Grain Trap mechanism (adoption is driven by group advantage, not individual welfare) is one of the book's few genuinely original arguments. Keep with b0108 ('No one voted.') and the rule in b0077.


## Chapter 7: The Battle Lines: The Entitled vs. The Ready

**Verdict.** This is the longest chapter in the book (5,912 words; only Appendix B is longer) and the clearest case of bloat. The dinner scene and the Rosa delivery are the best fiction in Part II, and both set up payoffs in Chapters 9 and 18 and the Conclusion. The Foundations argue against themselves. The title calls one group 'The Entitled' and three blocks take it back. The battle line is defined two incompatible ways. The Luddites are told three times and the musicians' campaigns twice. The music-history section quietly makes the 'new jobs will appear' argument Denny has just demolished.

**Publishable as is?** No. About 2,000 words cover opposition campaigns and repeat themselves: Marta's speech, b0140, the letterhead table, P-09 and P-10. The title framing needs a decision from CT. The chapter reaches its roughly 2,950-word half by keeping both scenes nearly whole, Denny's question, the employee-as-liability arithmetic, the Jahoda table, the best lines of the egalitarian pivot, the voice-before-the-decision block, and ONE telling of the music campaigns (either the table plus b0197 and b0200, or P-10 alone). Cutting P-09 means CT also has to deal with the ledger in Appendix D, the reference at sts.appendix-g.b0014, and the Introduction's count of twenty-four precedents (sts.introduction.b0151).

### Failure modes

- **critical**: The title insults, and then the chapter takes it back. The title names one side 'The Entitled'. b0128 says the table isn't good people vs. bad people, b0130 says nobody has to be a villain, and b0164 says reading the title as a sorting will make you prepare the wrong thing. The Pasteur epigraph (b0169) praises exactly the individual preparedness that b0164 calls 'losing more slowly'. The reader most likely to be offended by 'Entitled' (a professional worried about status) is also the reader most likely to buy a singularity book. (sts.chapter7.b0086, sts.chapter7.b0169, sts.chapter7.b0170, sts.chapter7.b0128, sts.chapter7.b0130, sts.chapter7.b0164) Quote (CT author voice): ""The Entitled vs. the Ready" reads, at first pass, like a sorting of winners from losers. Read it that way and you'll prepare the wrong thing."
- **critical**: The battle line is defined two incompatible ways. b0127 says the fight is between two mindsets that cut through every class and even through one person. The P-09 mechanism (b0182) says the fight is over who sets the terms, owners against workers. The summary bullets (b0087) carry both. A reader can't say what the chapter's central claim is. (sts.chapter7.b0087, sts.chapter7.b0127, sts.chapter7.b0182) Quote (CT author voice): "the battle line was never human versus machine. It was, and still is, a fight over who sets the terms."
- **major**: The Luddites are told three times. Marta's dinner speech (b0145) delivers the history as a weapon mid-argument, which is what the Elijah protocol asks for. b0140 brings them back through Merchant. P-09 (b0113 to b0108, about 506 words) re-lectures the same history, down to the same number. Marta's b0147 already concedes the dark side ('Some of them threatened people, too') that P-09's b0180 repeats. (sts.chapter7.b0145, sts.chapter7.b0140, sts.chapter7.b0113, sts.chapter7.b0179, sts.chapter7.b0180, sts.chapter7.b0186) Quote (CT author voice (echoing Marta's b0145: 'Parliament made breaking a frame a hanging offense and sent twelve thousand soldiers into the counties.')): "Parliament's reply was the Frame Breaking Act, which made machine-wrecking a capital crime, and twelve thousand soldiers sent into the disturbed counties."
- **major**: The music campaigns are told twice. The letterhead table (b0176, 231 words) plus its source note (b0185) already covers Sousa 1906, the AFM 1929 to 1931, Petrillo 1942 to 1948 and 'Won the fund'. P-10 (978 words) retells all of it and the fund. Together that's about 1,200 words for one lesson, and the table gives away P-10's payoff. (sts.chapter7.b0176, sts.chapter7.b0185, sts.chapter7.b0190, sts.chapter7.b0196, sts.chapter7.b0197)
- **major**: The chapter contradicts itself. b0105 and b0198 claim the medium each campaign fought became how 'the people it was defending got paid'. The 22,000 pit musicians in b0201 were not the people who later lived on recordings. That's the reskilling ladder Denny tears apart in b0143, rebuilt in the author's voice ten pages later. A skeptic will use the book against itself. (sts.chapter7.b0105, sts.chapter7.b0198, sts.chapter7.b0201, sts.chapter7.b0143) Quote (CT author voice): "the medium the campaign was formed to stop became, within a couple of decades, one of the main ways the people it was defending got paid."
- **major**: The robotaxi table is misleading. b0174 puts a coning group's 'own rules' (only empty vehicles) beside a crowd setting a Waymo on fire, which implies the same people did both, and it lists federal investigations under 'What happened on the street'. b0140 then tells the activists what they really meant. Both are fairness exposures a hostile reviewer will quote. (sts.chapter7.b0174, sts.chapter7.b0140, sts.chapter7.b0181) Quote (CT author voice): "Underneath ran something older."
- **major**: Hedging caveats and academic drift run through the Foundations. Jahoda's follow-up research gets vague quantities (b0133), the scarcity study gets a disclaimer (b0135), the garment example is admitted to be untypical and then called sharp (b0136), the egalitarian pivot ends in a list of what the study didn't test (b0106), and b0111 pre-defends the argument. (sts.chapter7.b0133, sts.chapter7.b0135, sts.chapter7.b0136, sts.chapter7.b0106, sts.chapter7.b0111) Quote (CT author voice): "The harvest study gives us a reason to take financial pressure seriously; it didn't test a food baseline or a cooperative."
- **major**: The fiction is over-explained. The beans are passed in dialogue (b0147), in action (b0148), in an illustration with an explanatory caption (b0203, b0204), and again in narration (b0149). The narrator then says the notebook page was 'the most useful data', and the Foundations explain both scenes again (b0125, b0167). The voice guide rule is that the scene never announces its lesson. (sts.chapter7.b0148, sts.chapter7.b0203, sts.chapter7.b0204, sts.chapter7.b0149, sts.chapter7.b0125, sts.chapter7.b0167) Quote (caption, CT author voice): "Fictional scene: pass the beans. The conversation turns from retraining to the floor people need beneath them."
- **minor**: An off-topic aside about the book's own methods: 109 words on cases the book chose NOT to include (a Western Union memo, a Bavarian decree), with pointers to Chapter 10 and Appendix D. A newcomer has never heard of either myth and is now being told about them only to be told they're false. (sts.chapter7.b0104) Quote (CT author voice): "Real history is a strong enough argument. Appendix D has the rest of the blacklist."
- **minor**: Material repeated from other chapters. b0166's line cook is the Introduction's (sts.introduction.b0107) and returns in sts.chapter11.b0162. The summary bullet 'The cook can lose the job and still eat' nearly repeats the Introduction's 'The line cook can lose his job and still eat.' The scarcity point in b0135 restates sts.chapter6.b0096. The Entitled/Ready table (b0170) restates Chapter 6's overwhelmed vs. status-invested (sts.chapter6.b0096, b0098). (sts.chapter7.b0166, sts.chapter7.b0087, sts.chapter7.b0135, sts.chapter7.b0170)
- **minor**: The characters work as mouthpieces. Denny gets 362 words of monologue in polished aphorisms (b0143, b0144), and Marta, a welder, recites dates and troop numbers at dinner (b0145). Elijah's notebook even points out the problem. The protocol's 'no speeches' guard is broken twice in one scene. (sts.chapter7.b0143, sts.chapter7.b0144, sts.chapter7.b0145, sts.chapter7.b0149) Quote (Elijah (in his notebook)): "Where does a welder learn that?"
- **minor**: The summary bullets give away Denny's reveal before the scene starts, and they turn Denny's line into the author's summary voice. (sts.chapter7.b0087) Quote (CT author voice (chapter summary), adapting Denny's line from b0144: 'It's what the comfortable prescribe for the falling.'): ""Reskilling" is what the comfortable prescribe to the displaced. Ask someone who's done it twice."
- **minor**: 'Stage 5' is used without definition. It's a Chapter 2 term (sts.chapter2.b0094). (sts.chapter7.b0139) Quote (CT author voice): "Stage 5 isn't a theory."
- **minor**: Mechanical rule breach. The year ranges in the 1929, 1942 and 1999 rows of the campaign table use en dash characters, which the book's no-dash rule forbids. (sts.chapter7.b0176)

### What readers would complain about

- *professional reader worried about their job*: "He calls me 'The Entitled' in the chapter title and then spends three paragraphs saying he doesn't mean it. Then don't call me that."
- *singularity regular*: "Thirty pages on the Luddites and 1929 movie-theater orchestras. I've read Blood in the Machine. Where is the AI in the AI book?"
- *skeptic*: "Denny says reskilling is a lie because the machine reaches every rung. Ten pages later the author tells me the musicians lost but recorded music ended up paying musicians anyway. That's the 'new jobs will appear' line Denny just shredded."
- *hostile podcast host*: "His robotaxi table puts a protest group's 'only empty cars' rule right next to a mob burning a Waymo, like they're the same people. Then he tells the protesters what they really meant."
- *bored newcomer*: "Luddites at dinner, Luddites in a book citation, Luddites in a case file. Twelve thousand soldiers, twice. I got it."
- *working-class reader*: "Every working person in this book talks like a footnote. The welder knows the troop counts, the dispatcher speaks in bumper stickers, and only the tech guy is allowed to say something dumb."
- *literary critic*: "The beans get passed four times, and then a caption explains what passing the beans meant."
- *skimmer*: "Three tables and two paragraphs that are nothing but links. I came for a book and got a bibliography."

### Weak writing

- `sts.chapter7.b0127` "The fight over this transition isn't humans against machines. It's between two mindsets" : CT author voice. A 'not X, but Y' construction, and the chapter later contradicts it (b0182).
- `sts.chapter7.b0128` "That table isn't a list of good people and bad people. It's a list of reflexes." : CT author voice. A 'not X, but Y' that takes back the chapter title.
- `sts.chapter7.b0129` "That's not a conspiracy; it's arithmetic." : CT author voice. A 'not X, but Y' correction. The same device is used in b0127, b0128, b0133, b0137 and b0165, so in this chapter it has become a reflex.
- `sts.chapter7.b0133` "Researchers built a questionnaire around her model, the Latent and Manifest Benefits scale, and have used it across a body of studies involving thousands of people. Separate large surveys have found that these hidden functions account for a big share of the link between unemployment and poor mental health." : CT author voice. Academic drift with vague quantities ('a body of studies', 'a big share'), and no source on the second claim.
- `sts.chapter7.b0135` "The finding concerns those tests and conditions, not a fixed loss of ability in every person short of money." : CT author voice. A fact-check caveat in the middle of the paragraph's best moment, just before 'Their mind is fully booked.'
- `sts.chapter7.b0136` "and says plainly that the case isn't typical of the whole sector" : CT author voice. Admits the single factory isn't representative, then calls the point 'narrow and sharp'. Telling the reader a point is sharp doesn't make it sharp.
- `sts.chapter7.b0136` "for people whose anxiety about technology has only recently gotten personal" : CT author voice. A sneer aimed at the reader, the contempt the voice guide says to aim at systems, not people.
- `sts.chapter7.b0137` "That isn't confusion. It's exactly the problem we're proposing to change." : CT author voice. Another 'not X, but Y', and 'proposing to change' a 'problem' is policy-speak.
- `sts.chapter7.b0141` "The other half is quieter, better dressed, and far better funded" : CT author voice. A list of three for rhythm.
- `sts.chapter7.b0165` "That isn't a warm sentiment. It's the coldest economics in this book." : CT author voice. A 'not X, but Y' and an overclaim. The paragraph has no economics in it, cold or otherwise.
- `sts.chapter7.b0106` "Egalitarian isn't the moral garnish on this strategy; it's the load-bearing wall." : CT author voice. A 'not X, but Y' that takes Marta's signature line (sts.chapter6.b0019) for the narrator and weakens its callback in sts.chapter16.b0018.
- `sts.chapter7.b0163` "Here's the move the battle-line framing hides" : CT author voice. A signpost. The 'framing' it says is hiding something is the chapter's own title.
- `sts.chapter7.b0167` "Rosa's meal makes the point small enough to see." : CT author voice. Explains a scene that already made its point. The voice guide's rule: if a narrative passage ends by explaining what it meant, cut the explanation.
- `sts.chapter7.b0125` "Neither question needed an answer about the ultimate limits of machine intelligence. Both needed somebody to decide what they'd provide." : CT author voice. Re-explains Denny and Rosa a page after the reader met them.
- `sts.chapter7.b0111` "My argument doesn't need every complaint about AI to be a complaint about economics." : CT author voice. Defensive. It answers an objection nobody in the chapter raised.
- `sts.chapter7.b0104` "this book only carries cases that survive a primary source" : CT author voice. The book praising its own sourcing standards, which is inside baseball for a newcomer.
- `sts.chapter7.b0149` "and it was the most useful data he'd gathered in two years." : Narrator. States the lesson of the notebook page outright. The list of names already makes the point.
- `sts.chapter7.b0148` "Denny held out his hand for the bowl. Marta passed it." : Narrator. Repeats the action of the line before it ('Pass the beans.') and of the next block ('The beans got passed.').
- `sts.chapter7.b0009` "It started, the way most real fights do, over something small." : Narrator. Cliché.
- `sts.chapter7.b0088` "that he learned where the battle lines actually ran" : Narrator. Drops the title into the scene and announces its lesson before it happens.
- `sts.chapter7.b0108` "every hour spent furious at the machine buys one hour building your position. Keep the ledger in your notebook." : CT author voice. A self-help gimmick. Nobody will keep an anger-hours ledger, and suggesting it trivializes the anger the chapter just validated.
- `sts.chapter7.b0199` "organized, funded, national, lawful, sympathetic" : CT author voice. A list of five adjectives used as a drumroll.

### Not in your voice

- `sts.chapter7.b0133` "Researchers built a questionnaire around her model, the Latent and Manifest Benefits scale" : Literature-review prose. In 2024 CT asked the reader questions that 'keep me up at night'. He didn't summarize instrument validation.
- `sts.chapter7.b0106` "The harvest study gives us a reason to take financial pressure seriously; it didn't test a food baseline or a cooperative." : A fact-checker's caveat written in the author's voice. The 2024 CT never qualified a claim in the same breath. He undercut himself with jokes ('Kidding. Mostly.'), not with methodology.
- `sts.chapter7.b0111` "My argument doesn't need every complaint about AI to be a complaint about economics." : Defensive, lawyerly. The 2024 voice is confrontational and warm ('You're not like all these other folks still committed to the status quo'), not pre-emptively apologetic.
- `sts.chapter7.b0136` "The point is narrow and sharp" : Self-assessment instead of argument. CT in 2024 showed the point with an image (Einstein as your mom's tech support). He didn't grade it.
- `sts.chapter7.b0135` "The finding concerns those tests and conditions, not a fixed loss of ability in every person short of money." : A disclaimer register that doesn't exist anywhere in the 2024 draft. The same block's close, 'Their mind is fully booked.', is pure CT.
- `sts.chapter7.b0143` "every man who tells you 'just climb faster' is standing on a floor, not a ladder." : Spoken by Denny (character). It's a polished, tattooable aphorism. That's strong craft, but it's the author's line-making in a dispatcher's mouth, which a working-class reader will notice. Flagged so CT can decide whether Denny should sound like CT.

### Where a newcomer gets lost

- `sts.chapter7.b0139`: 'Stage 5' is a Chapter 2 term, not redefined here.
- `sts.chapter7.b0166`: 'The fired line cook' arrives with a definite article, as if we've met him. He comes from the Introduction (sts.introduction.b0107), and there is no cook in this chapter.
- `sts.chapter7.b0104`: Refers to a Western Union memo, a Bavarian decree, 'one eloquent abbot' whose 'file is at the end of Chapter 10', and Appendix D's 'blacklist'. A newcomer has context for none of it.
- `sts.chapter7.b0176`: Lea Act, Sony v. Universal, MPAA, Jack Valenti, RIAA and Napster are all named with no gloss. A reader under thirty may know none of them.
- `sts.chapter7.b0010`: 'a Claypot word' depends on Part I.
- `sts.chapter7.b0149`: '(stages and watts and orders of magnitude)' calls back to Chapters 2 and 5 without explaining them.

### Skim problems

- Eleven H2 headings and two precedent case files after the scene. A skimmer reading headings sees 'The battle lines', 'The backlash is already in the street', 'The campaign has a letterhead', 'Precedent P-09', 'Precedent P-10' and can't tell which one is the chapter's point.
- Three tables (b0170, b0174, b0176) plus two italic source paragraphs that are almost all URLs (b0181, b0185). A skimmer meets bibliography where the argument should be.
- The battle line is defined in the summary bullets, in b0127 and in b0182, three different ways, so a skimmer who catches only one of them comes away with a different chapter.
- The summary bullets (b0087) give away Denny's twist, so a reader who skims them first gets nothing from the scene.
- The single most useful sentence for skimmers, b0197's meal-for-concert swap, is buried in the ninth paragraph of the second case file, near the end of the chapter.

### Best blocks

- `sts.chapter7.b0143`: The chapter's heart. Denny: 'how many times do I have to climb this ladder before you stop moving it'. It's the most persuasive anti-reskilling passage in the book, and it's told as a life, not a stat.
- `sts.chapter7.b0144`: Denny: 'where do my daughter and I stand for those six months?' The Foundations close on 'Denny's question' (b0112), so this block has to survive.
- `sts.chapter7.b0010`: Narrator: 'It was a Claypot word. It had been on a slide.' Elijah's complicity in ten words.
- `sts.chapter7.b0011`: Narrator: 'an expression that was not unkind and was worse than unkind. It was *practiced*.' Sets Denny up before he speaks.
- `sts.chapter7.b0009`: Introduces Curtis and the fence. The fence pays off at sts.chapter9.b0185 ('the man who wanted to fence out the world') and in b0109.
- `sts.chapter7.b0147`: Marta: 'You build the floor Parliament wouldn't. That's what this table *is*.' and 'Pass the beans.' The best scene ending in Part II. Keep with b0145 as the ONE telling of the Luddites.
- `sts.chapter7.b0149`: Elijah's first page of names, not numbers. It pays off as the names column in sts.chapter18.b0144. Drop only the last clause if CT trims.
- `sts.chapter7.b0154`: Narrator: 'a washing machine reached the uneven part of its spin cycle'. The Rosa scene is the most restrained writing in the chapter, and its payoffs run through sts.chapter9.b0141, sts.chapter18.b0140 and sts.conclusion.b0082. Keep b0150 through b0123 nearly whole.
- `sts.chapter7.b0119`: Curtis: 'Doesn't change your dinner,' The food-first argument in four words, said by the man who wanted the fence.
- `sts.chapter7.b0122`: Curtis: 'Through next month. Then they need funding again.' Plants the funding thread that drives Chapter 18 and the Conclusion's 'needed money beyond December'.
- `sts.chapter7.b0123`: Curtis marks the box 'delivered'. It closes the scene without stating a moral.
- `sts.chapter7.b0129`: The employee-as-liability arithmetic is plain and unarguable. Keep it with b0130's 'It only takes all of us letting it.'
- `sts.chapter7.b0172`: The Jahoda table is the best skimmable newcomer content in the chapter. It answers 'why would anyone miss a job they hate' in five rows.
- `sts.chapter7.b0134`: CT: 'It's a much better problem than hunger. It's still a problem.' A classic CT closer on a reference singularity regulars expect.
- `sts.chapter7.b0142`: CT: 'Name the defect (the machine has no soul). Predict the death of the art. Buy the ads. Demand a legal remedy. Lose the argument about whether the machine exists, which was never the winnable one.' The whole history of anti-technology campaigns in five sentences, in CT's voice.
- `sts.chapter7.b0164`: CT: 'A lone household with a generator and a rifle isn't ready. It's just losing more slowly, with better lighting.' The dark humor of the 2024 draft, still alive.
- `sts.chapter7.b0107`: CT: 'stop climbing and start weaving' and 'a network with one indispensable node is just a boss with extra steps'. Field-manual specific, and funny.
- `sts.chapter7.b0109`: CT: 'The pivot is toward a table that keeps adding chairs, not a fence with a nicer gate.' Pays off Curtis's fence in the author's voice.
- `sts.chapter7.b0110`: CT: 'A liberation plan that runs on an exhausted kitchen crew has lost the plot.' The book takes its own labor problem seriously.
- `sts.chapter7.b0168`: CT: 'Nobody should first meet a new system as an instruction to train their replacement.' The most actionable block in the Foundations.
- `sts.chapter7.b0197`: CT: 'A performance free to its audience, with the musicians paid. Read that sentence again with a meal in place of the concert.' The single best bridge from labor history to the book's food thesis. Keep it whichever version of the music history survives.
- `sts.chapter7.b0200`: CT: 'Argue about the machine's soul and you can spend twenty years getting nothing. Argue about the machine's bill and you can build an institution that outlives you.' The chapter's most quotable rule.


## Chapter 8: The Psychology of the Collapse

**Verdict.** The opening scene is the strongest writing in the chapter: the Companion 2.0 spiral, Marta's story of Gary and Sal with its turn, and "Soup's on at noon." It must survive almost whole. The 2,400-word Foundations behind it read like a digital-minimalism pamphlet that keeps arguing with itself. There is a heading its own paragraph denies, a Shannon entropy formula brought in only to be disowned, five conceptual figures whose captions each say they are not evidence, and three separate to-do endings. The words "singularity" and "AI" never appear in the chapter text, so the book's subject drops out exactly where a newcomer needs the link.

**Publishable as is?** No. The scene (b0221 to b0232) is publishable once b0016, b0224 and the Gary portrait with its caption (b0249, b0250) are dropped. The Foundations are not. About 370 of their words exist mainly to hedge, disclaim or correct claims the reader never heard (b0242, b0243, b0211, b0212, b0213, b0256, b0214). Another 240 are figures and captions that disclaim themselves. The chapter also closes three times (b0284, b0285, b0292). For the core cut, keep the scene, the no-notifications protocol, boredom on purpose, the tomb, the rule that food is never withheld, the inconvenient-day test, Start with three, and Murphree's correction of the Bernays legend.

### Failure modes

- **critical**: The Foundations argue with themselves. The heading says the cognitive budget is finite; the paragraph under it says the energy figure is not a budget of decisions and pivots away. The slot-machine subsection brings in Shannon entropy and a summation formula, then spends 78 words saying the formula measures none of the things the chapter cares about. The reader is handed math only to be told to ignore it. This is the defect the VOICE-GUIDE describes as an Argument section that 'got embarrassed and retreated into academic', in its purest form. (b0191, b0243, b0255, b0213, b0256, b0214) Quote (CT, author voice): "The formula doesn't prove what a platform intends or what its feed does to your mind."
- **critical**: Five conceptual figures illustrate one idea (interruptions cost something), and every caption is a disclaimer: not a diagnosis, not a measurement, not a recovery time, not an optimum. The figure at b0253 has unnumbered axes by design. If a chart needs a caption saying it isn't data, it is decoration, and a stack of five disclaimers reads like a compliance review, not a book. (b0251, b0252, b0253, b0254, b0258, b0259, b0262, b0263, b0294, b0295) Quote (figure caption (editorial voice)): "The drawing gives no fixed recovery time, interruption threshold, or measured effect size."
- **major**: The book's subject vanishes. Neither 'singularity' nor 'AI' appears in the chapter text. The only explicit link between attention capture and the machine that takes jobs is in Marta's mouth (b0229). The header image is Sophia the humanoid robot, who is never mentioned, under a caption that means nothing. The title promises the psychology of 'the Collapse'; the chapter delivers doomscrolling advice and co-op governance. (b0001, b0002, b0003, b0229) Quote (figure caption (editorial voice)): "Sophia. The mirror humans keep flinching at."
- **major**: The Gary portrait's caption reverses the scene. Marta withdraws her own moral (Gary was driving his wife to appointments in a truck that needed work; his problem wasn't the news), but the caption under his picture delivers exactly the moral she withdrew, and art-catalog.json tags the plate 'cautionary-tale'. The portrait is a PixelLab sprite, an AI-generated image of a fictional man, in a book whose Introduction takes on the 'AI slop' complaint. (b0249, b0250, b0223, b0225) Quote (figure caption (editorial voice)): "Gary. Stayed informed."
- **major**: The chapter's own story undercuts its longest section. Marta's turn is that Sal and Gary differed in room to move, not in attitude. The Foundations then spend 1,366 words on attention protocols (Sal's method, done alone) and 803 on the relational half the story actually argues for. b0234 and b0248 try to bridge the gap, but the word count tells the reader the phone is the main problem. (b0225, b0234, b0239, b0248, b0270) Quote (Marta (fictional character)): "Same severance. Different room to move."
- **major**: Marta becomes an exposition machine. The ELIJAH-PROTOCOL forbids characters delivering lectures, and b0229 is the author's attention-economy thesis in a welder's mouth. It opens on a stock false claim: plenty of people tell you this, at least since The Social Dilemma in 2020. (b0229) Quote (Marta (fictional character)): "And here's the part nobody tells you, because there's no money in telling you."
- **major**: Derivative and uncredited. The attention-extraction frame, grayscale, batched email, deep-work blocks and scheduled boredom are the stock advice of Cal Newport, Tristan Harris, Johann Hari and Jenny Odell, and the book cites none of them anywhere. Torches of Freedom is one of the most retold stories in media criticism. A singularity regular will recognize all of it. Only Murphree's correction (b0289) is fresh. (b0241, b0246, b0266, b0267, b0219, b0286, b0287) Quote (CT, author voice): "A lot of the new economy runs on extracting attention."
- **major**: Citations cut against the claims beside them, and the text never squares them. Mark's 2008 experiment found that interrupted people finished faster. Axelrod's winning strategy works by retaliating, and the next paragraph forbids retaliating with the one resource that matters, without saying why the game theory still holds. The slot-machine analogy rests on variable-ratio reinforcement from behavioral psychology, not on information theory, so the formula is the wrong science as well as a disowned one. (b0211, b0274, b0275, b0213) Quote (CT, author voice, summarizing Mark, Gudith, and Klocke (2008)): "interrupted participants completed work faster but reported more stress, frustration, effort, and time pressure."
- **major**: The chapter ends three times: Start with three (b0284), then a meeting exercise (b0285), then the Precedent's practice list (b0292). On top of those come five protocols and a check-in paragraph (b0247). That's about twenty instructions in one chapter, and the reader can't tell which one matters. (b0247, b0284, b0244, b0285, b0292) Quote (CT, author voice): "At the next meeting about a food project, write the promise in one sentence"
- **minor**: A prepper strawman. The lone-bunker prepper is set up and knocked down with fake physics. Preppers who already organize as groups go unmentioned, and the prepper reader the book wants to win over is insulted on arrival. (b0208, b0209) Quote (CT, author voice): "The lone-prepper model fails on the physics."
- **minor**: Structural sloppiness. Two H2 headings are stacked with nothing between them, and the heading 'The great cognitive extraction' oversells a section that is mostly caveats. (b0023, b0233, b0239)

### What readers would complain about

- *newcomer*: "Bought a book about the singularity. Chapter 8 is a phone detox with a log formula the author then tells me doesn't measure anything. Why is it there?"
- *singularity regular*: "Digital Minimalism and The Social Dilemma, years late and uncredited. Grayscale your phone? Batch your email? I read this on Medium in 2018."
- *skeptic*: "Every chart comes with a caption saying it isn't data. Unnumbered axes, on purpose. If it isn't evidence, don't draw it."
- *prepper*: "Calls my family's food storage a tomb and says it fails 'on the physics'. Preppers have organized in groups for decades. Strawman."
- *hostile podcast host*: "'as Marta proved'. His evidence is a character he made up. And he cites tit for tat, then bans the tat."
- *reader living with anxiety*: "A chapter called 'Psychology' shows a man in a panic spiral and hands me grayscale mode and an email schedule. The word 'anxiety' never appears."
- *reader who hates AI art*: "The book complains about AI slop and then illustrates Gary with an AI pixel sprite, captioned with a cheap shot the story just took back."
- *literary critic*: "The scene is terrific until the narrator starts pointing at his own motifs: the water glass, 'making sure he heard the next part', 'because Marta had made him hear it'."

### Weak writing

- `b0220` "The last battlefield isn't the grid or the market. It's the three pounds of wet tissue behind your eyes." : An 'isn't X. It's Y' construction, used three bullets running ('Your phone isn't just a tool. It's a harvesting machine', 'Panic isn't the same as information. It's a state'). The construction is CT's own habit from the 2024 draft ('This isn’t about tech support; it’s about revolutionizing curiosity'), so it is in voice, but three deep it reads as a template. 'last battlefield' overclaims for a chapter about notifications.
- `b0220` "states can be interrupted, starved, and survived" : A list of three for rhythm. Starving a 'state' is not a concrete act.
- `b0010` "the honest record of it matters more than his dignity, so:" : Narrator throat-clearing, and 'honest' comes back three lines later ('by any honest accounting'). 'Honest' is a tic across both chapters (b0010 twice, b0141).
- `b0010` "his heart going like a bird against a window" : A stock simile in an otherwise precise paragraph.
- `b0013` "with the manner of a woman with one meeting left on her calendar" : An unreadable simile. Retired? Unhurried? Dying? The reader can't tell.
- `b0016` "the same gesture as Denny's water glass, and Elijah wondered distantly if everyone who'd been through the fire came out with the same tells" : The narrator points at its own motif and explains it, and 'through the fire' is a cliche. VOICE-GUIDE: the scene never announces its lesson.
- `b0224` "She watched Elijah's face, making sure he heard the next part." : A stage direction that announces a twist is coming.
- `b0234` "When I say our mindset is part of the problem, I don't mean a frightened person needs a better attitude." : Defensive throat-clearing. It answers an objection nobody raised and refers to a claim this chapter never makes.
- `b0235` "Those assumptions become budgets, contracts, eligibility tests, and the questions a room lets itself ask." : Stacked abstract nouns. The paragraph's three assumptions are the book's core thesis, said in its vaguest register.
- `b0237` "Security gives hope somewhere to stand." : One abstract noun acting on another. It sounds wise and says little.
- `b0240` "It's cold, it's inert, and it weighs less than half a pound." : A list of three for rhythm, set up for a punchline borrowed from the humane-tech movement.
- `b0241` "A good share of the time, the thing in your hand is managing you." : The hedge kills the punch the setup built.
- `b0242` "That experience isn't a diagnosis, and a feed doesn't affect everyone the same way." : A disclaimer nobody asked for.
- `b0211` "Their return-time measure included that intervening work; it wasn't a stopwatch on how long a brain needs to regain focus." : It corrects the popular claim about how many minutes a brain needs to refocus without ever telling the reader that claim, so a newcomer gets a rebuttal of nothing. Academic drift: 'return-time measure', 'intervening work'.
- `b0212` "Neither finding gives you a universal recovery time or a maximum number of interruptions per hour." : The same pattern: it denies numbers the text never offered.
- `b0213` "The slot-machine comparison needs a boundary too." : It puts a boundary on a comparison the text hasn't made yet. Only the heading has.
- `b0215` "put a firewall between your working memory and the firehose. You wouldn't drink untreated water from a puddle behind a refinery." : Server, firewall, firehose, puddle, refinery: three metaphors in 48 words. 'working memory' is a cognitive-science term used loosely.
- `b0266` "Mastery of a craft" : A productivity-blog binary table. Batching email does not produce mastery of a craft.
- `b0267` "You run the queries; the queries don't run you." : A stock chiasmus.
- `b0246` "the screen gets boring, clinical, and dead" : Another list of three. The claim that 'app designers test their reds and oranges hard' is unsourced in a chapter that sources everything else.
- `b0248` "The other half, as Marta proved, is somebody at the door." : A fictional character can't prove anything. A hostile host will read this line aloud.
- `b0209` "running an energy deficit that only goes one way" : Pseudo-physics dressed up as rigor.
- `b0271` "That's load-sharing you can check." : An auditor's tic for an ending. It flattens the tomb line two blocks earlier.
- `b0272` "True autonomy isn't individual. It's relational." : Abstraction plus the isn't/it's construction: a tattoo line the paragraph hasn't earned.
- `b0275` "and it's the most important sentence in this section:" : Explaining the joke. The bold type already does that job.
- `b0276` "A promise of universal access needs an answer that survives annoyance, fatigue, and disagreement." : A concrete run of three 'Someone' sentences collapses into a triple of abstractions.
- `b0278` "A community isn't a hierarchy; it's a topology." : A false contrast: a hierarchy is a topology (a tree). Jargon for a newcomer, too.
- `b0282` "Nineteen households are a beginning. Humanity is the ambition." : A grandiose closer after a careful paragraph.
- `b0291` "*food first* should earn its force from a meal somebody can actually get." : Abstract and awkward, and it assumes the slogan.

### Not in your voice

- `b0242` "That experience isn't a diagnosis, and a feed doesn't affect everyone the same way." : CT's 2024 draft never apologizes in advance. It says 'the singularity isn't just coming, it's here' and 'we’re a LOT closer than you think'. This sentence belongs to a liability reviewer.
- `b0252` "Conceptual illustration, not a diagnosis or a model of brain chemistry." : Legal boilerplate. The 2024 draft's register is 'grab your favorite drink and your favorite tinfoil hat'.
- `b0211` "Gloria Mark and colleagues observed information workers moving through other activities before returning to interrupted work." : Methods-section register. The draft reaches for references through pop culture (Frankenstein, the Golem, The Terminator, Siri) and talks straight to the reader.
- `b0214` "But bits of uncertainty don't measure pleasure, compulsion, or mental fatigue." : A lecture correcting its own analogy. CT's draft swings at the analogy and keeps moving ('It’s already here, kind of.').
- `b0281` "That's an estimate, not a membership limit, and even a small circle needs written agreements." : A hedge on a hedge. The draft asserts and lets the reader push back.
- `b0264` "This delay is for commentary, not evacuation orders, urgent health notices, or a person who needs help now." : A liability carve-out in the middle of a rule. Nothing in the draft sounds like this.
- `b0271` "Count the actual work saved before promising a smaller burden." : An auditor's voice deflating CT's own line two blocks up ('You've built a tomb.'), which is pure CT.
- `b0229` "And here's the part nobody tells you, because there's no money in telling you." : This is CT's own Argument voice (the draft's 'here's the thing') transplanted into Marta. It's on voice for CT and off voice for a welder whose other lines are 'college' and 'It's lunch.'
- `b0236` "That's a different starting point for a human mind." : Essayist abstraction. The draft puts a person in the frame ('Your mom doesn't call you for tech help anymore'), not 'a human mind'.

### Where a newcomer gets lost

- `b0009`: 'Claypot', 'Companion 2.0' and 'his own notebook' are planted in Chapters 0 to 2. In a cut edition the spiral has no trigger unless those setups survive.
- `b0016`: Denny's water glass is a Chapter 7 gesture. Without it the line is noise.
- `b0231`: 'the morning after LADDER.xlsx' refers to Chapter 4.
- `b0230`: 'college' is Marta's nickname for Elijah from Chapter 6. A newcomer reads it as a sneer.
- `b0243`: 'the rough twenty-watt comparison from Chapter 5' assumes Chapter 5.
- `b0209`: 'the tunnel vision Chapter 7 described' assumes Chapter 7's scarcity study.
- `b0256`: A summation formula with a base-2 logarithm, with no gloss beyond P(x_i).
- `b0215`: 'working memory' is used as though the reader knows the cognitive-science term.
- `b0261`: 'Semantic hygiene' is never defined. The heading means nothing to a newcomer.
- `b0270`: 'Relational autonomy' is a philosophy term of art used as a heading, with no definition.
- `b0274`: The 'iterated prisoner's dilemma' is never explained. The newcomer doesn't know what the dilemma is.
- `b0278`: 'topology' is undefined.
- `b0285`: It assumes the reader already sits in meetings about a food project.
- `b0291`: '*food first*' is the book's slogan, used as if established.

### Skim problems

- Two H2 headings stack with nothing between them: 'The Foundations' (b0023), then 'What I mean by mindset' (b0233).
- The heading 'Your cognitive budget is finite' (b0191) states the claim its paragraph (b0243) withdraws, so a skimmer leaves with the wrong idea.
- Five near-identical conceptual figures (b0251, b0253, b0258, b0262, b0294) break up the page on one idea. A skimmer sees charts and assumes data.
- The chapter's most important sentence, the bold rule in b0275, is buried after five protocols and a game-theory paragraph, under a heading ('Why cooperation holds') that doesn't signal it.
- Three closing action lists (b0284, b0285, b0292) on top of five protocols. A skimmer can't tell which instruction is the one.
- The Bernays Precedent ties back to 'Semantic hygiene' roughly 1,400 words earlier, not to the relational section it follows.
- The 'In this chapter' bullets promise the 'last battlefield'; the headings deliver Protocols 1 to 5. The scale doesn't match.

### Best blocks

- `b0009`: 'He knew their names. He knew their kids' names.' Job loss made concrete in two sentences.
- `b0010`: The spiral itself, ending on 'Nothing but cortisol and tabs.' The truest and funniest paragraph in the chapter.
- `b0012`: 'in a nineteen-household co-op the mesh network is mostly people.' The book's thesis in one clause.
- `b0222`: Gary: 'The picture was so big there was no place in it small enough for him to stand.'
- `b0223`: Sal: 'Something with an end to it.' The method, in a welder's words.
- `b0225`: The turn: 'Same severance. Different room to move.' It moves the chapter from moral to structural, which is the book's whole argument.
- `b0227`: 'He didn't owe us a gutter before lunch.' Unconditional care in one line.
- `b0229`: Keep despite its lecture opener. 'One machine eats your paycheck and the other eats the attention you'd need to do anything about it' is the only bridge in the chapter from AI to attention.
- `b0230`: 'Soup's on at noon. That's not a reward, college. It's lunch.' Food first, dramatized instead of argued.
- `b0231`: The physical acts (notifications off, phone in a drawer that closes) plus Sal's rule and 'somebody came by at eight.'
- `b0232`: An honest aftermath with no cure claimed: 'At noon he put the tools down and went to eat.'
- `b0210`: Protocol 1 is concrete and doable tonight; 'a block of dead obsidian' is a good image.
- `b0219`: 'Your brain will complain and beg for stimulation. Let it.' CT's voice, short and imperative.
- `b0208`: 'you haven't built a shelter. You've built a tomb.' CT's voice.
- `b0275`: The bold rule that food is never withheld is the book's central ethic, plus 'you've just rebuilt the employment test with a friendlier face.'
- `b0276`: The inconvenient-day test: the most concrete governance writing in the chapter.
- `b0284`: Start with three: a newcomer can do it this month.
- `b0289`: Murphree's correction is the one fresh angle on Bernays: 'The myth of the all-powerful manipulator asks you to give up the one thing that beats him: your capacity to notice.'
- `b0290`: 'Persuasion at scale rarely argues with your values. It wears them.'


## Chapter 9: Deglobalization and the Neighborhood Factory

**Verdict.** The scene is the strongest writing in the chapter: the resin-potted controller, Marta's 11, 1, 0, "Ugly is a maintenance feature," Reuben's "You thought a noun was a permit," and Curtis's two dollars of hose. It must survive. The Foundations abandon the title. The neighborhood factory gets one bullet, then about 1,300 words go to nitrogen chemistry, FarmBot, a Mycodo install script, a PID formula and a nameless five-household vegetable delivery told four times. The chapter also contradicts itself on its own terms, and neither "singularity" nor "AI" appears in its text except in one image credit.

**Publishable as is?** No. The scene (b0008 to b0186) is publishable only with fixes: b0010 has an unparseable sentence, CSA is undefined at first use (b0008), and the Raspberry Pi redesign fails Marta's own single-supplier test (b0177, b0178). The Foundations are not publishable. They don't deliver the chapter's title, they contradict the scene (b0162 against b0182), and most of the technical material belongs in Chapter 15 or an appendix. For the core cut, keep the scene, b0176, b0162, b0164, b0165, b0141, b0174, b0175, and the Bronze Age precedent with its mechanism, rule and practice.

### Failure modes

- **critical**: The chapter never delivers its title. After the scene, the neighborhood factory gets one bullet (b0162) and one abstract paragraph (b0166), and no later heading names it. Nothing explains what a plasma table, a 3D printer or a small CNC shop can make, what it costs or who runs it. Instead about 1,300 words go to nitrogen fixation, a garden gantry, a greenhouse-controller install and a vegetable delivery, material the text keeps handing off to Chapter 15. A skimmer would call this the gardening chapter. (b0162, b0166, b0151, b0142, b0143, b0145, b0146, b0147, b0171, b0148, b0203, b0150, b0154, b0167, b0210) Quote (CT, author voice): "(Chapter 15 goes down into the dirt.)"
- **critical**: The chapter contradicts itself. The Foundations say the backyard prototype needs nobody's permission, right after a scene that spent a page on a county yellow tab and counsel. The opening promises an alternative 'so obviously better that switching to it becomes a no-brainer'; the closing section admits a timer or one more paid shift is sometimes the better buy. 'Manhattan Project scale' is offered as the model for an 'out-loud' build-out, though the Manhattan Project was secret and built a bomb, and the same chapter ends on 'Start small enough to learn.' (b0162, b0182, b0188, b0174, b0165, b0158) Quote (CT, author voice): "None of it waits on anybody's permission."
- **major**: The redesign fails Marta's own test. She condemns the module because only one supplier makes it. The fix is a Raspberry Pi, a single-vendor board that was famously hard to buy during the 2021 to 2023 shortage. Any maker will catch this, and it undercuts the best scene in the chapter. (b0177, b0178, b0129) Quote (narrator (CT, Narrative register)): "a Raspberry Pi the regional supplier had in stock"
- **major**: The worked example is told four times and drops the cast. The same eight-plus-two kilograms appear in prose (b0172), a seven-row table (b0153), an isometric figure (b0218) and its caption (b0219), with an interactive marker in between. It is written as a grant proposal about 'An established garden' and 'A local food group', though the book has a named co-op, a soil scientist (Priya) and a delivery driver (Curtis) standing right there. (b0168, b0169, b0170, b0172, b0153, b0217, b0218, b0219, b0173) Quote (CT, author voice): "Here's an illustrative project, not a reported trial."
- **major**: Equations and code as decoration. A Part II chapter for general readers carries two chemical equations, a PID control law in calculus notation, a bar chart in gigajoules per tonne and a curl-to-bash install. The PID formula is followed by the admission that the valve only opens or closes, which is exactly where PID doesn't apply. Install commands go stale in print within a year. Piping a downloaded script straight into a shell is a habit security people warn against, in a book whose Chapter 11 turns on a security leak. (b0199, b0200, b0208, b0167, b0215, b0216, b0152, b0207) Quote (CT, author voice): "A valve that only opens or closes still needs limits on run time and total water"
- **major**: Reuben is reduced to a disclaimer. The ELIJAH-PROTOCOL casts him as a 'legal hacker' who treats loopholes as the API. On the page he defers to counsel, files a registration and holds a yellow tab, in prose so vague it names no actual rule. His one great line survives (b0182). The rest reads as if written to avoid liability, and the same compliance language leaks into the build (b0183) and the delivery (b0184). (b0179, b0180, b0182, b0183, b0184) Quote (Reuben Vance (fictional character)): "Then I found people whose job it is to answer the parts I can't."
- **major**: Images repeat, and one says it was AI-adapted. The header photo (b0002) and the later cutout (b0213) come from the same Wikimedia file, so the reader sees one 3D printer twice, the second time captioned 'AI-adapted'. The controller and Reuben plates are PixelLab sprites, and both captions repeat the prose a few lines above word for word. (b0002, b0003, b0213, b0214, b0129, b0130, b0131, b0132) Quote (figure caption (editorial voice)): "A machine takes shape around its work. AI-adapted cutout from"
- **major**: No economics. 'Deglobalization' is a loaded word in current politics, and the chapter never meets the obvious objection that local production costs more. It calls the build-out a jobs program of 'well-paid' work without saying who pays, then in the next paragraph hands 'the back-breaking work to machines', which shrinks those jobs. (b0162, b0164, b0165) Quote (CT, author voice): "That's not a fantasy. It's a jobs program: honest, well-paid, local work for the trades, the fabricators, the growers, and everyone who keeps a place running."
- **minor**: A cheap shot the VOICE-GUIDE rules out ('Diagnosis beats denunciation'). 'rocket hobbies while veterans sleep outside' borrows a political talking point that will lose half the room before the argument starts. (b0188) Quote (CT, author voice): "people rich enough to fund rocket hobbies while veterans sleep outside"
- **minor**: Callbacks the chapter can't stand without. Sal (a figure inside Marta's Chapter 8 anecdote), Curtis as 'the man who wanted to fence out the world' (Chapter 7), the check-engine light (Chapter 6) and Rosa (Chapter 7) land only if those setups survive the cut. (b0017, b0185, b0141) Quote (narrator (CT, Narrative register)): "an assembly line that Sal himself would have recognized from 1985"
- **minor**: A dead interactive marker. b0217 is an HTML comment counted as a paragraph. In print and EPUB it renders nothing. (b0217) Quote (markup, no speaker): "<!-- interactive:food-delivery -->"

### What readers would complain about

- *maker / hardware engineer*: "Marta trashes a single-source module and the 'repairable' fix is a Raspberry Pi. Then a PID formula for a valve that's either open or shut, and curl piped into bash in a printed book."
- *newcomer*: "Two chemical equations, a calculus formula and a terminal script in the chapter that was supposed to tell me what a neighborhood factory is. Also, what's a CSA?"
- *singularity regular*: "Not a word about AI or what automation will actually do to manufacturing. The robot in the robot section is a gantry that waters a raised bed."
- *economist*: "Deglobalization with no prices. He calls it a jobs program and never says who pays or why anyone buys the dearer local part."
- *farmer*: "Ten kilos of vegetables for five households, explained four times, plus a reminder that a valve doesn't decide a plant is safe to eat. That's a church garden, not a food system."
- *hostile podcast host*: "'Manhattan Project scale.' 'out-loud'. He picked the most secret program in American history, the one that built the bomb, as the model for an open neighborhood build."
- *reader who hates AI art*: "The same 3D printer photo twice, once 'AI-adapted', plus AI pixel-art portraits of the cast."
- *politically centrist or conservative reader*: "'rocket hobbies while veterans sleep outside'. I was with him on supply chains until he started recycling campaign lines."
- *literary critic*: "Captions repeat the dialogue right above them word for word. 'Screw terminals instead of poetry' lands once. The second time it's a slogan."

### Weak writing

- `b0008` "The co-op's first real manufacturing run, the thing Elijah would later describe to strangers as *the neighborhood factory booting up*, though at the time it was just a January argument in a cold shed, was forty greenhouse controllers for the CSA network two valleys over." : The subject and verb sit 30 words apart. The scene's first sentence is the hardest in the chapter to parse, and it contains an undefined acronym.
- `b0134` "Real money, real deadline, real customers with real seedlings on the line." : 'Real' four times for rhythm.
- `b0010` "He wants that on the record, with the caveat that so does everyone in this story every time he says it." : Unparseable. It also recycles a narrator tic ('He wants that on the record' appears in Chapter 6).
- `b0017` "it was during the second one, everyone deburring enclosures and crimping ferrules in an assembly line that Sal himself would have recognized from 1985, that the door opened" : A 62-word cleft sentence with an orphaned callback in the middle.
- `b0180` "A tax adviser had marked the purchases that needed separate treatment." : Bureaucratic vagueness. It names no rule, no tax and no consequence.
- `b0184` "With the power isolated and help from the support call, they could change the documented part without discarding the controller." : Safety-manual prose dropped into a scene.
- `b0188` "It's to build a working alternative so obviously better that switching to it becomes a no-brainer." : The back half of a 'not X, but Y' pair, and an overclaim the chapter retracts in b0174. The jab before it is CT's 2024 register ('get its collective head out of its collective ass'), so it is in voice. The problem is calibration, not authorship.
- `b0162` "Sensor rigs that turn a raised bed into something closer to a Tamagotchi than a chore." : The metaphor backfires: a Tamagotchi is the definition of a chore, and it dies if neglected.
- `b0163` "Density is a feature, not a flaw" : A cliche, and a 'not X' construction.
- `b0165` "So ask the only question that matters" : Overclaim. The question that follows is strong enough without the drumroll. 'Resilience, not secession.' is another 'not X'.
- `b0165` "Think of it at the scale it deserves, which is Manhattan Project scale." : The wrong analogy: a secret weapons program as the model for an 'out-loud' civilian build.
- `b0166` "The neighborhood factory earns its name when somebody can keep doing useful work because it exists." : Abstract and circular. The only Foundations sentence about the title subject says nothing about it.
- `b0146` "Legumes, clover, peas, beans, vetch, host *Rhizobium* bacteria in their roots" : The examples lost their parentheses. It reads as five separate subjects, with 'Legumes' listed beside its own members.
- `b0147` "That's not the same job as the fungi that help roots take up phosphorus and water" : It corrects a confusion the reader never had. The fungi appear only to be dismissed.
- `b0148` "so keep the claim the size of the documented work." : The author scolds an imaginary overclaimer in public.
- `b0149` "That's a reason to investigate useful automation and who can afford it, not proof that any one installation feeds a town." : A citation brought in to prove nothing.
- `b0196` "distance alone does not establish fair treatment." : A non sequitur disclaimer. Fair treatment wasn't the topic.
- `b0216` "A benchmark is not proof every existing plant can achieve it." : A caption hedging a chart the reader didn't need.
- `b0175` "That's a small, explicit wire between a technical gain and a fed person." : An abstract metaphor where a plain sentence about who gets the money would do.
- `b0158` "Start small enough to learn. Keep the destination big enough to matter." : A balanced antithesis for rhythm. This is also the second chapter in a row to close on 'the next meeting' (compare Chapter 8, b0285).
- `b0191` "A rich network isn't the same thing as a secure household, and even then, people could tell the difference." : 'even then' dangles, and the claim about what Bronze Age people perceived is unsupported.
- `b0192` "not from one blow but from interacting pressures, drought, conflict, migration, political breakdown, and interrupted trade, unevenly, in different places at different times." : A 'not X but Y' construction plus a list that lost its colon, so 'interacting pressures' reads as one item among six.
- `b0211` "You're not paying extra for a product. You're paying for which side of the cascade your household sits on." : Another not-X/Y construction, and it repeats 'which side of the cascade' from b0193 a few lines up.

### Not in your voice

- `b0168` "An established garden has permission to grow food on a suitable site. A local food group already has handling and distribution arrangements." : Grant-proposal register. CT's draft builds hypotheticals around a person ('Your mom doesn't call you for tech help anymore; she's got Einstein on speed dial').
- `b0182` "This stays open until somebody with authority answers the actual question. And the invoice goes past counsel before it goes to a customer." : A risk-management memo in Reuben's mouth. It isn't CT's voice, and it isn't the 'legal hacker' the protocol describes.
- `b0183` "two growers tried the first units with their ordinary controls still available." : QA-report language. The draft never writes like a test plan.
- `b0149` "The UN Food and Agriculture Organization's 2022 report on agricultural automation looks at the opportunities alongside the barriers, especially for smaller farms." : Literature-review register. The draft's evidence is vivid and personal, not 'opportunities alongside the barriers'.
- `b0196` "Imported parts, materials, energy and human work remain; distance alone does not establish fair treatment." : The draft never qualifies a claim before making it.
- `b0170` "Recipients say whether it arrived and whether they could use it." : Flat procedure, with no person, no image and no stakes.
- `b0173` "The donated time and equipment get written down too, not called free" : An auditor's voice. CT's draft is loud about stakes and silent about bookkeeping.

### Where a newcomer gets lost

- `b0008`: 'CSA' appears here for the first time in the book and is never defined in the chapter text. The glossary entry (Appendix F) points to Chapter 12.
- `b0010`: 'PCB' and 'system-on-module' are undefined, and the joke depends on knowing why they're fragile.
- `b0134`: 'a server in Virginia' is an insider joke about cloud data centers. A newcomer reads it as random.
- `b0178`: 'PETG', 'screw terminals' and 'capacitive probes' are unexplained.
- `b0185`: 'OBD dongle' is unexplained. 'the man who wanted to fence out the world' assumes Curtis from Chapter 7, and the check-engine light assumes Chapter 6.
- `b0017`: 'Sal' is a man inside Marta's Chapter 8 story, not a character Elijah has met.
- `b0141`: Rosa and her one working hand assume Chapter 7's delivery scene. 'food first' is used as an established slogan.
- `b0162`: 'Deglobalization, not decentralization.' argues with a word the newcomer didn't bring. The book's internal style rule leaks into the text.
- `b0163`: 'hyper-local' is used as established vocabulary.
- `b0199`: Haber-Bosch arrives as an equation with no plain explanation of why the nitrogen triple bond is hard to break.
- `b0200`: ATP, ADP, P_i and electrons: biochemistry notation with no gloss.
- `b0208`: The PID control law in integral and derivative notation.
- `b0206`: 'SHT31-D', 'relay board', and capacitive versus resistive probes, with no context.
- `b0207`: 'sudo', 'apt' and 'curl' assume the reader uses a terminal.
- `b0215`: Gigajoules per tonne with no everyday anchor.

### Skim problems

- After the scene, no heading mentions the neighborhood factory. The headings read 'The controller isn't dinner', 'The nitrogen problem', 'What a garden robot actually does', 'The greenhouse brain'. A skimmer concludes this is the gardening chapter.
- The worked example appears as prose (b0172), a seven-row table (b0153), an isometric figure (b0218) and a caption (b0219), with an invisible interactive marker (b0217) in between.
- The same 3D printer photo appears twice (b0002, b0213).
- Captions repeat the line directly above them (b0130, b0132).
- Three handoffs to other chapters (b0162 to Chapter 12; b0147 and b0167 to Chapter 15) tell the skimmer the real material is elsewhere.
- Two action lists close the chapter (b0210, b0211), and b0210's soil and seed tips overlap Chapter 15.
- Four formula and code blocks (b0199, b0200, b0207, b0208) plus a chart (b0215) form a technical wall mid-chapter. A casual reader flips past it, and with it the payoff section 'What would make the machine worth it?' (b0174, b0175).

### Best blocks
