- `b0176`: 'A factory used to be a billion dollars and a river. Now it's a plasma table, a 3D printer, and somebody's cousin who can weld.' CT at his best: funny and concrete.
- `b0010`: The renders beat ('He presented it to the shop on a Tuesday with renders. *Renders.*') despite the garbled middle sentence.
- `b0177`: Marta's 11, 1, 0 puts the whole case against fragile global supply chains on one whiteboard.
- `b0015`: 'Ugly is a maintenance feature. Ugly means the next guy can see how it works.'
- `b0178`: 'The firmware did less and survived more.' plus the laminated repair card zip-tied inside the lid.
- `b0182`: 'You thought a noun was a permit. Happens all the time.' The best line in the chapter.
- `b0021`: 'Nobody asks Reuben' and 'Reuben *notices*.' (Priya): a whole character in one exchange.
- `b0184`: 'Marta put the repair emails up on the corkboard like other shops hang their first dollar.'
- `b0185`: Curtis's hose: a twelve-hundred-dollar dealer quote beaten by two dollars of hose, from the man who wanted the fence.
- `b0162`: Defines deglobalization and survival by charter with real instruments (the co-op bylaw, the land-trust covenant). This is the chapter's actual idea.
- `b0165`: 'if the trucks stopped tomorrow, how long could your region feed its people? Most places don't know. That gap is the work.'
- `b0141`: 'The crop was fine. The offer was wrong.'
- `b0175`: 'A machine doesn't pick. The organization around it does.'
- `b0192`: The Bronze Age collapse is the strongest argument for the chapter's thesis, and it is sourced.
- `b0159`: 'Interdependence is efficiency in fair weather and contagion in foul.'
- `b0211`: 'Map your tin' and the neighbor census: the best practice list in these two chapters.
- `b0212`: The Kropotkin epigraph is exactly on topic.


## Part III: How to Survive the Transition

**Verdict.** This is a 141-word opener for the half of the book that is supposed to be the field manual, and it hedges, scolds and contradicts itself in consecutive paragraphs. It shrinks the book's headline forecast from 'AGI by Thanksgiving 2027' to 'serious work on improving themselves', then says survival 'isn't about predicting the future'. There is no hook, no number, no joke and no heat. Nothing here sounds like the man whose first draft opened 'So the robots have taken over. Congratulations! You've lost.'

**Publishable as is?** No. It is filler, and the contradiction between b0008 and b0007 is the first thing a skeptic will quote. For the core cut, keep the heading (b0001) and b0007 only. b0007's 'Begin with a person who needs something' is the method Chapter 11 closes on (sts.chapter11.b0131, sts.chapter11.b0199). A reader loses nothing if b0008 and b0009 go.

### Failure modes

- **major**: The divider contradicts itself in adjacent paragraphs. b0008 opens on a dated bet; b0007 immediately says surviving has nothing to do with prediction. The reader gets a forecast and its retraction within 108 words. (sts.part-3.b0008, sts.part-3.b0007) Quote (CT, author voice): "Surviving this isn't about predicting the future. It's about building something that works whatever the forecast turns out to be."
- **major**: The forecast drifts. The Introduction (sts.introduction.b0088, 'My call, for the record: AGI by Thanksgiving 2027.') and Chapter 1 (sts.chapter1.b0111) commit to a named date and a named event. The divider restates it as a smaller, vaguer claim. A skeptic reads that as the author quietly backing off his own headline. (sts.part-3.b0008, sts.introduction.b0088, sts.chapter1.b0111) Quote (CT, author voice): "my bet is that by 2027 these systems will be doing serious work on improving themselves"
- **minor**: It scolds the frightened reader. CT's own draft greets fear ('You're afraid. I get it. Who wouldn't be?') and then escalates. The divider calls the reader's likely reaction a mistake and offers only an abstraction in its place. (sts.part-3.b0008) Quote (CT, author voice): "Most people are responding by panicking or by waiting for someone else to handle it. That's a mistake."
- **minor**: It makes no concrete promise for Part III. No build, no number, no first step, no chapter is named. The one method sentence is a list of abstract nouns, and the closer is a greeting card. (sts.part-3.b0007, sts.part-3.b0009) Quote (CT, author voice): "This is how you build your own path, step by step."

### What readers would complain about

- *skeptical singularity regular*: "First page of Part III: 'my bet is 2027.' Next paragraph: surviving 'isn't about predicting the future.' Pick one. And what happened to AGI by Thanksgiving?"
- *bored newcomer*: "Three paragraphs telling me I don't need land, servers or a robot, and not one telling me what I DO need. I skipped to the chapter."
- *hostile podcast host*: "The 'how to survive' section opens by calling everyone who's scared a mistake, then hands you a mood board."

### Weak writing

- `sts.part-3.b0007` "Surviving this isn't about predicting the future. It's about building something that works whatever the forecast turns out to be." : [CT, author voice] An 'isn't about X. It's about Y' construction, and it deflates the claim instead of escalating it.
- `sts.part-3.b0007` "a dinner they can use" : [CT, author voice] Odd collocation. Nobody 'uses' a dinner. It reads as a phrase built to avoid saying 'food they can eat'.
- `sts.part-3.b0007` "Follow that need through the people, tools, land, and agreements that could meet it." : [CT, author voice] Four abstract plural nouns stacked for rhythm. 'Agreements' is policy-paper vocabulary.
- `sts.part-3.b0009` "This is how you build your own path, step by step." : [CT, author voice] Self-help cliché closer that carries no information.
- `sts.part-3.b0008` "It started with a 2017 change in how machines learn" : [CT, author voice] So vague it says nothing. The Transformer paper is the book's best concrete anchor, and here it shrinks to 'a change'.

### Not in your voice

- `sts.part-3.b0008` "Most people are responding by panicking or by waiting for someone else to handle it. That's a mistake." : [CT, author voice] A flat schoolteacher verdict. CT's draft meets panic with empathy and a joke ('You're afraid. I get it. Who wouldn't be?') and only then swings.
- `sts.part-3.b0008` "my bet is that by 2027 these systems will be doing serious work on improving themselves" : [CT, author voice] This sounds like a committee. CT's actual voice on the same claim is Chapter 1's 'My personal expected date for AGI is U.S. Thanksgiving 2027, November 25. Why that day? Because.' (sts.chapter1.b0111): specific, cocky, funny.
- `sts.part-3.b0009` "You can also be the person receiving help." : [CT, author voice] A defensive caveat in a Part opener. CT's draft never pre-apologizes. It promises: 'This book will teach you.'

### Where a newcomer gets lost

- `sts.part-3.b0008`: 'a 2017 change in how machines learn' depends on Chapter 1's Transformer section. A core-cut reader who lost it gets a date with nothing attached.
- `sts.part-3.b0008`: 'improving themselves' gestures at recursive self-improvement without naming or explaining it.
- `sts.part-3.b0009`: 'a rack of servers' shows up as a survival asset with no context. Nothing explains why a reader would own servers until Chapter 11.

### Skim problems

- Nothing is bolded and there is no number or list, so a skimmer carries nothing into Part III.
- The one usable instruction ('Begin with a person who needs something') is buried mid-paragraph in b0007, after a hedge.

### Best blocks

- `sts.part-3.b0007`: The only load-bearing prose here. 'Begin with a person who needs something' is the Part III method that Chapter 11's 'One request, all the way through' (sts.chapter11.b0131, sts.chapter11.b0199) turns into a worksheet.
- `sts.part-3.b0001`: Structural: the Part heading.


## Chapter 10: The "Create Over Consume" Protocol

**Verdict.** The opening scene is some of the best writing in Part III. The Foundations around it would get the chapter laughed off a podcast. The thesis, that a habit is 'the line' between people who come through and people who get flattened, is refuted by the book's own Chapter 8 and by Denny's own backstory, and a disclaimer hedges it before the reader is told to do anything. The middle third is a Nature-paper explainer with an integral the book disowns in the next paragraph. The Field Manual steps (clean an alley, volunteer, borrow an old neighbor's garage) have nothing to do with the media story the scene sets up, and the money question the scene opens on is never answered.

**Publishable as is?** No. The scene (b0141 through b0147) could ship nearly as is. The Foundations cannot: it contradicts Chapter 8, its leverage argument stands on a premise it concedes, and a newcomer hits $P_k(x)$ with no way to use it. In the core cut the chapter survives as the scene, the three judo rules (b0133, b0134, b0172) and the abbot-test practice (b0183). The model-collapse math (b0155 to b0168), Steps 1 to 3, the energy-bill tangent and the analog blind spot are the obvious drops.

### Failure modes

- **critical**: The book's own fiction refutes the chapter's thesis. Chapter 10 says a habit divides who comes through from who gets flattened. In Chapter 8, Marta (character) explicitly retracts that story: 'I used to tell that story as if Sal had found the right attitude and Gary hadn't.' (sts.chapter8.b0223) and 'Same severance. Different room to move. Took me too long to ask.' (sts.chapter8.b0225). In Chapter 7, Denny (character) did everything right and the ladder moved twice (sts.chapter7.b0143). A careful reader catches the book blaming the flattened for lacking a habit, two chapters after it taught them not to. (sts.chapter10.b0118, sts.chapter10.b0150, sts.chapter8.b0223, sts.chapter8.b0225, sts.chapter7.b0143) Quote (CT, author voice): "The line between the people who come through this period with some footing and the people who get flattened by it is a habit: **create more than you consume.**"
- **major**: The thesis is disclaimed before it is used. One paragraph after the thesis, b0151 spends 85 words saying the rule doesn't apply to dinner and that consuming is fine. The bullet list says it again, and Step 1 adds a third 'not an entry fee'. The habit ends up as both THE line between survival and ruin and an optional lifestyle tip. (sts.chapter10.b0118, sts.chapter10.b0150, sts.chapter10.b0151, sts.chapter10.b0135) Quote (CT, author voice): "Before anything else, the fine print that isn't fine print: nobody earns dinner this way."
- **major**: Model collapse is academic drift that never pays off. 512 words, an integral, a figure and three disclaimers support one sentence of advice. The very next block calls the equation 'not the paper's general equation'. Why print it? (sts.chapter10.b0128, sts.chapter10.b0155, sts.chapter10.b0136, sts.chapter10.b0166, sts.chapter10.b0131, sts.chapter10.b0132, sts.chapter10.b0137, sts.chapter10.b0167, sts.chapter10.b0168) Quote (CT, author voice): "Here $q_k$ is a schematic transition kernel for the training and sampling process, not the paper's general equation for a language model."
- **major**: The leverage argument stands on a premise the chapter concedes, and then switches markets. If the labs are already keeping real human data in the mix, its scarcity pays the labs, not a man posting a video. 'Real human data is valuable to model trainers' and 'your true story gets reach in a feed' are different claims, and the chapter slides from one to the other. A singularity regular who knows synthetic data is routinely used in training will write off the section. (sts.chapter10.b0161) Quote (CT, author voice): "The practical conclusion isn't that the machines are doomed. The labs know about this, and they work hard to keep real human data in the mix. It's that real human data just got more valuable."
- **major**: Three chapters are stapled together. The scene is about media. Steps 1 to 3 are about litter, volunteering and borrowing a garage. The Precedent is about medieval scribes. Step 1 duplicates Chapter 17's cleanup section (sts.chapter17.b0149) and sends the reader there. (sts.chapter10.b0149, sts.chapter10.b0153, sts.chapter10.b0135, sts.chapter10.b0122, sts.chapter10.b0154, sts.chapter10.b0162, sts.chapter10.b0163, sts.chapter10.b0123, sts.chapter10.b0164, sts.chapter17.b0149) Quote (CT, author voice): "Chapter 17 has more on doing this well."
- **major**: The scene opens on money, and the chapter never says how any arrives. Denny's line sets the question. The bullet promises 'The signal can help pay for the work.' In 3,161 words there is no mechanism, no figure and no revenue model; the answer only shows up in Chapter 12. The Field Manual test (a number or a named part) also fails inside the steps: 'own a few watts' gives no wattage and no price. (sts.chapter10.b0141, sts.chapter10.b0118, sts.chapter10.b0147, sts.chapter10.b0135) Quote (Denny Osei, character): "People pay for what we know. We give it away to anyone who walks into the shed. Why does the walking matter?"
- **major**: The lesson is stated five times. The bullet list states it (b0118). The narrator announces it before the scene starts (b0142), hedges it mid-scene (b0146), states it as the scene's moral (b0147), and the Foundations re-explains Denny (b0124) and repeats it (b0161). VOICE-GUIDE's own rule is that a scene never announces its lesson. (sts.chapter10.b0118, sts.chapter10.b0142, sts.chapter10.b0146, sts.chapter10.b0147, sts.chapter10.b0124, sts.chapter10.b0161) Quote (narrator, third-person scene): "This is the story of how he was wrong in a brand-new way, which by then he'd learned to recognize as the shape of his education."
- **major**: The fiction keeps turning into a law what the Foundations says isn't one. b0158 warns against treating one successful post as a law of the feed. The scene declares how 'true things move now', and in the next chapter the incident report goes viral too (sts.chapter11.b0153). Every honest act in this book gets rewarded with reach, and a critic will call that wish-fulfillment. (sts.chapter10.b0146, sts.chapter10.b0158, sts.chapter11.b0153) Quote (narrator, third-person scene): "moved the way true things move now: slowly for a day, then handed person to person like contraband."
- **minor**: Continuity hole. Denny stops after four lines and refuses the script (b0010, b0013). Yet an 'optimized cut' gets posted, and Elijah 'had spent two weeks teaching a human being to sound like a format.' What video was posted? (sts.chapter10.b0010, sts.chapter10.b0013, sts.chapter10.b0144, sts.chapter10.b0145) Quote (narrator, third-person scene): "Elijah posted both versions, because he was still a data scientist in his soul and you always run the A/B."
- **minor**: The Precedent's rule makes the chapter binary, make something or merely complain. That contradicts the chapter's own disclaimer: 'Reading, resting, watching, and receiving care are parts of a life.' (b0151). (sts.chapter10.b0182, sts.chapter10.b0151) Quote (CT, author voice): "you only get to choose whether you use them to make something or merely to complain."
- **minor**: Denny's best line is wrong on its own terms. Bait is food; a lure is a picture of food. Anyone who fishes will notice, and Rule 1 (b0133) repeats the image. (sts.chapter10.b0020, sts.chapter10.b0133) Quote (Denny Osei, character): "You tried to make bait. Bait's a picture of food."
- **minor**: The analog blind spot section stops for a barter-tax warning with an IRS citation. That implies the reader was planning to dodge tax and kills the section's momentum. 'That's the real play' (b0175) then follows from judo, not from the blind spot. (sts.chapter10.b0174, sts.chapter10.b0175) Quote (CT, author voice): "Outside a feed isn't outside the law."
- **minor**: The author disappears. From 'The Foundations' to the end there is no first-person CT. The only 'I' is a hypothetical video script ('I retrained twice...'). CT's draft is 'I' on every page. (sts.chapter10.b0150, sts.chapter10.b0151, sts.chapter10.b0155, sts.chapter10.b0158, sts.chapter10.b0174)

### What readers would complain about

- *bored newcomer*: "I bought a book about surviving AI. Chapter 10 told me to pick up litter, volunteer at a pantry, then showed me an integral sign. I closed it."
- *laid-off worker*: "You tell me the line between who makes it and who gets flattened is a habit. I had the habit. The ladder moved anyway, same as it did for your own character Denny two chapters ago."
- *skeptical singularity regular*: "Model collapse as my moat? The book admits the labs already handle it. Then it treats 'labs want human data' as if it meant 'my video gets views.' Weakest argument in the chapter, with the most math attached."
- *critic*: "Every time someone in this book tells the truth on camera it goes viral and strangers show up to help. That isn't a field manual. It's a fable with footnotes."
- *older reader*: "Chapter 10 suggests finding a neighbor 'whose house has more room than they use' and trading yard work for their garage, under the heading 'the intergenerational exchange.' I'm the neighbor. No thanks."
- *working creator*: "'Algorithmic judo' is: true hook, practical steps, call to action. That's every creator course from 2019 with a martial-arts name."
- *hostile podcast host*: "The chapter ends with a monk who used the printing press. The next one ends with Swatch. Same lesson twice in a row, and he still opened with the most overused epigraph in tech, Alan Kay's 'invent the future.'"

### Weak writing

- `sts.chapter10.b0118` "The algorithm can be ridden, but it can't be impersonated. Your unfakeable advantage is something that actually happened, told by the person it happened to." : [CT, author voice] An 'X can be..., but it can't be...' antithesis plus the marketing coinage 'unfakeable advantage'.
- `sts.chapter10.b0118` "Making things is a freedom, not a toll." : [CT, author voice] A 'not X' disclaimer in the briefing. The toll line comes back in b0151.
- `sts.chapter10.b0151` "Create over consume is a way to take your time back, not a toll." : [CT, author voice] The second 'not a toll' in about 100 words. Padding.
- `sts.chapter10.b0151` "Reading, resting, watching, and receiving care are parts of a life." : [CT, author voice] A list run for rhythm that lectures the reader about something they didn't claim.
- `sts.chapter10.b0123` "That doesn't mean renting a shop. It means finding space nobody's using." : [CT, author voice] A 'doesn't mean X. It means Y.' construction, one of about a dozen in the chapter.
- `sts.chapter10.b0162` "Labor moves you from the world of digital opinion to the world of physical results." : [CT, author voice] Abstract-noun parallelism ('world of X to world of Y') that gives the reader nothing to do.
- `sts.chapter10.b0162` "Make the introduction wherever you can, then give it a shared task." : [CT, author voice] Unclear antecedent. Does 'it' mean the introduction or the people?
- `sts.chapter10.b0164` "**The intergenerational exchange.**" : [CT, author voice] A bureaucratic label for asking an elderly neighbor for their garage.
- `sts.chapter10.b0156` "And the energy bill is real too." : [CT, author voice] Opens a tangent about paper books and offline manuals that the next block immediately dismisses ('Hiding in the woods with paper books isn't the plan, though.'). Padding.
- `sts.chapter10.b0131` "This isn't a claim that every use of synthetic training data causes collapse; selection, checking, and access to real data change the setup." : [CT, author voice] A hedge. With b0161 and the b0168 caption, it's the third disclaimer in about 200 words.
- `sts.chapter10.b0168` "This schematic is not a universal outcome or a timetable; real data, sampling and training choices affect the result." : [caption, author voice] A caption that apologizes for its own figure.
- `sts.chapter10.b0172` "The feed is a temporary delivery truck, not the destination." : [CT, author voice] Metaphor pileup. In one chapter: attention machine, ocean of beige, contraband, bait, picture of food, cargo under a truck, judo, delivery truck, silicon and stone, a copy of a copy.
- `sts.chapter10.b0180` "The unit of change is often smaller than the whole occupation: one task moves, another stays, and the terms change for the people doing them." : [CT, author voice] Academic drift: labor-economics jargon ('unit of change') tacked onto a story about a monk.
- `sts.chapter10.b0180` "The legend calls that hypocrisy. It's something more useful." : [CT, author voice] A 'not X, but Y' myth-bust. Chapter 11's Precedent opens with the same move (sts.chapter11.b0201).
- `sts.chapter10.b0183` "End the week as someone whose hands touched the new world instead of someone who only reviewed it." : [CT, author voice] A slogan built on an 'instead of' antithesis.
- `sts.chapter10.b0154` "The time you spend on digital fear is time you could spend in your physical community." : [CT, author voice] Generic self-help sentence.
- `sts.chapter10.b0174` "It can predict your next purchase with unsettling accuracy." : [CT, author voice] Cliché.

### Not in your voice

- `sts.chapter10.b0131` "Here $q_k$ is a schematic transition kernel for the training and sampling process, not the paper's general equation for a language model." : [CT, author voice] Peer-review voice. CT's draft explains the singularity with 'Imagine a world where Einstein is your entry-level tech support.' It never prints notation, let alone notation it then disowns.
- `sts.chapter10.b0151` "Before anything else, the fine print that isn't fine print: nobody earns dinner this way." : [CT, author voice] A nervous preface before the instruction. CT's draft opens a chapter with 'What the fuck is going on?' and gets straight to the point. It never disclaims before it claims.
- `sts.chapter10.b0174` "(In the United States, barter generally creates taxable income at the fair market value received; the IRS distinguishes some informal, noncommercial exchanges of similar services." : [CT, author voice] Compliance counsel sitting inside a section about slipping out of surveillance. CT's draft is conspiratorial and gleeful ('grab your favorite drink and your favorite tinfoil hat').
- `sts.chapter10.b0162` "**Direct exchange.** Show up and work. Labor moves you from the world of digital opinion to the world of physical results." : [CT, author voice] Corporate-workshop cadence with bold labels. CT's draft argues through pop culture (Frankenstein, the Golem, Terminator, Star Trek, the Jetsons), jokes and rhetorical salvos.
- `sts.chapter10.b0158` "Try it, watch who actually uses the result, and don't confuse one successful post with a law of the feed." : [CT, author voice] An editor's caution stapled to the chapter's boldest idea. CT escalates ('a LOT closer than you think'); this retreats.
- `sts.chapter10.b0146` "Elijah couldn't turn any of that into a law about what the internet rewarded. He could answer them." : [narrator, third-person scene] An editorial hedge dressed as narration. The scene had already landed. VOICE-GUIDE calls the narrative register the book's best asset and says it must not explain itself.
- `sts.chapter10.b0161` "The practical conclusion isn't that the machines are doomed." : [CT, author voice] CT does use this construction, but to escalate: 'We're not just building a tool; we're potentially crafting a new form of life.' and 'This isn’t about tech support; it’s about revolutionizing curiosity.' Here, as across v0.11, the same grammar shrinks the claim. Same construction, opposite temperature.

### Where a newcomer gets lost

- `sts.chapter10.b0018`: 'Sal's rule' is defined only in Chapter 8 (sts.chapter8.b0223 and Elijah's notebook). Read cold, it's a name with no rule attached.
- `sts.chapter10.b0143`: 'the arms coming in, the county's folder, the second ladder, the second folder' compresses Denny's Chapter 7 monologue (sts.chapter7.b0143) into shorthand that means nothing without it.
- `sts.chapter10.b0013`: 'the letter I got in 2019' (Denny, character) depends on Chapter 7's backstory.
- `sts.chapter10.b0010`: 'the greenhouse-controller story' and the 'repair card' (b0146, b0148) refer to Chapter 9's build.
- `sts.chapter10.b0163`: 'Find your node': 'node' is mesh-network vocabulary the book doesn't introduce until Chapter 17.
- `sts.chapter10.b0118`: 'Turn the co-op's work into signal' talks to a reader who has no co-op, and 'signal' is never defined.
- `sts.chapter10.b0136`: 'distribution', 'finite samples', $P_0(x)$, and in b0131 'transition kernel', with an integral in b0166. A non-technical reader gets no plain-language gloss to hold onto.
- `sts.chapter10.b0132`: 'tails' of a distribution is statistics jargon, only loosely explained.
- `sts.chapter10.b0158`: 'Run a Local Model' appears before the book explains what a local model is (Chapter 11).
- `sts.chapter10.b0180`: 'illumination, rubrication, and binding': 'rubrication' is undefined.

### Skim problems

- The headings are cute labels, not instructions: 'The slop-ocalypse', 'Algorithmic judo', 'The analog blind spot', 'Make the handoff small'. Skimming the headings tells you nothing about what to do.
- The numbering collapses. Steps 1 to 3, then an unnumbered de facto Step 4 ('Share a result people can judge'), then 'Three rules', then three Precedent practices. That's about thirteen actions in four formats with no single checklist.
- The best action in the chapter, the abbot-test tally (b0183, item 1), is the last block, behind a 437-word history.
- 512 words of model-collapse math sit in the middle of the chapter, right where a skimmer bails.
- The signpost to the next chapter (b0177) comes before the Precedent block, so the chapter ends twice.
- The 'In this chapter' bullets give away the scene's resolution before the scene starts.

### Best blocks

- `sts.chapter10.b0010`: 'He did everything correctly. That was the problem.' plus the dentures simile: learning through friction, as the Elijah Protocol asks, in two lines.
- `sts.chapter10.b0013`: Denny (character): 'I'm not talking to the algorithm. I'm talking to a guy on his couch who just got the letter I got in 2019.' The whole chapter in one line of dialogue. Keep b0011 and b0012 with it.
- `sts.chapter10.b0143`: The one-take monologue. 'Here's ours. Come see it. Bring your kid.' (Denny, character) puts the book's thesis in a character's mouth without it becoming a lecture.
- `sts.chapter10.b0145`: 'Forty-one views.' and 'He had spent two weeks teaching a human being to sound like a format.' (narrator): the failure, shown.
- `sts.chapter10.b0020`: 'I put out food.' (Denny, character) is the chapter's tattoo line, despite the bait slip.
- `sts.chapter10.b0147`: Load-bearing: Denny owning the channel pays off in Chapter 12 ('The down payment would take the channel's first real surplus', sts.chapter12.b0152) and Chapter 18 (sts.chapter18.b0012).
- `sts.chapter10.b0141`: Sets the scene's stakes in Denny's voice, and it's the only setup the media story needs.
- `sts.chapter10.b0133`: With b0134 and b0172, the three judo rules are the chapter's only portable Field Manual content tied to the scene.
- `sts.chapter10.b0175`: 'Their silicon, our stone.' is the one Foundations line with CT's swagger.
- `sts.chapter10.b0183`: The abbot test ('Don't moralize the numbers. Just get them.') is the best Monday action in the chapter and the closest to CT's own voice.


## Chapter 11: King and Queen of Your Own Robotic Court

**Verdict.** The chapter is titled for a robot court, and no robot does anything in it. Its Foundations is two unrelated chapters, wartime food policy and home-lab hardware, shuffled together with a third (objections about cleanup robots) that belongs in Chapter 17. The breach scene has the best stakes in Part III, then spends 500 words after Marta's hearth speech letting Priya, Reuben and the narrator recite incident-response doctrine. The Foundations reads like a white paper vetted by a liability lawyer: four Hansard citations, a NIST glossary entry, and a caveat behind nearly every number.

**Publishable as is?** No. The scene from b0008 through Marta's hearth speech (b0149) is publishable with trims. Everything after needs the knife. In the core cut, drop the British Restaurants history (b0186 to b0182), the misplaced objections (b0177 to b0172), the heat math (b0193 to b0197) and P-14, which duplicates P-13. Keep the ownership questions (b0173, b0175), the boundary rule (b0184), and 'One request, all the way through' (b0131, b0199). The second-reviewer rule in b0154 must survive in some form, because Chapter 17 amends it.

### Failure modes

- **critical**: The title is a bait-and-switch. The title, opening photo, caption and first bullet all promise robots working for you, and the same Spot photo appears twice (b0002, b0207). The chapter delivers an LLM on used servers and a hypothetical public food partnership, then ends conceding that a robot might fit in someday. (sts.chapter11.b0098, sts.chapter11.b0002, sts.chapter11.b0099, sts.chapter11.b0100, sts.chapter11.b0207, sts.chapter11.b0208, sts.chapter11.b0199) Quote (CT, author voice): "A robot may fit at one of those points as it gets more capable."
- **critical**: No thesis. The Foundations runs food access (b0158 to b0165), 1941 Hansard (b0179 to b0182), objections (b0166 to b0172), ownership (b0173, b0175), local compute (b0176, b0133), VRAM math, heat math, security (b0128 to b0185), the ethics of help (b0129, b0130), then food again (b0131 to b0200). Its first sentence apologizes for the title. No reader could say in one line what this chapter is about. (sts.chapter11.b0158, sts.chapter11.b0159, sts.chapter11.b0179, sts.chapter11.b0166, sts.chapter11.b0176, sts.chapter11.b0134, sts.chapter11.b0140, sts.chapter11.b0185, sts.chapter11.b0131) Quote (CT, author voice): "King and Queen of Your Own Robotic Court is a playful name for a serious change in who gets help."
- **major**: Incident-response doctrine buries the scene's climax. Marta's hearth speech (b0149) is the natural end. After it come Priya correcting her (b0155, b0151), Reuben and counsel (b0152), a public report reviewed for consent (b0153), a policy memo (b0154), and a narrator stating the moral (b0157). The Elijah Protocol's 'Kill the Exposition Machine' is broken by a soil biologist reciting data-minimization principles. (sts.chapter11.b0155, sts.chapter11.b0151, sts.chapter11.b0152, sts.chapter11.b0153, sts.chapter11.b0154, sts.chapter11.b0157) Quote (Priya Ramanathan, character): "We put information together without asking whether it needed to be together. Then we treated his confidence as our check."
- **major**: Caveats flatten the rise before the fall. 'For five weeks it was purely good' is undercut in the same paragraph by checking, a fabricated source and a cost log, and b0143 adds 'before he brought his questions to counsel.' The machine is never allowed to impress, so the breach has nothing to fall from. (sts.chapter11.b0145, sts.chapter11.b0143) Quote (narrator, third-person scene): "Priya's intern learned blight triage from it in an afternoon, then caught it inventing a source and wrote that failure on the board beside the successes."
- **major**: The book's AGI forecast and this chapter's model don't live in the same world. Silvana's open-weights descendant, 'three generations downstream of the thing that had started all of this', can suggest wording and find a likely bug. A singularity regular will ask why a book predicting AGI by Thanksgiving 2027 (sts.chapter1.b0111) shows its world-changing model as a grammar checker. (sts.chapter11.b0144, sts.chapter1.b0111) Quote (narrator, third-person scene): "capable enough to suggest wording, locate a likely bug, or turn a confusing manual into questions somebody could check"
- **major**: The objections section answers a different chapter. Three of the five objections are about cleanup robots and dangerous work, which is Chapter 17's territory (sts.chapter17.b0111, sts.chapter17.b0149). The biggest objection, jobs, gets 64 words and a deflection. Every answer opens with a concession. (sts.chapter11.b0177, sts.chapter11.b0167, sts.chapter11.b0168, sts.chapter11.b0170, sts.chapter11.b0171, sts.chapter11.b0172) Quote (CT, author voice): "That's what this whole book is arguing for."
- **major**: A second precedent sits inside the chapter. It's 329 words of 1941 and 1942 Hansard with four citations and undefined terms (British Restaurants, Billingsgate, Fishmongers' Hall), plus a historian's hedge, all before the chapter's real Precedent block. The one useful test, a kitchen that opens too late for the workers it serves, is buried in the third paragraph. (sts.chapter11.b0186, sts.chapter11.b0179, sts.chapter11.b0180, sts.chapter11.b0181, sts.chapter11.b0182) Quote (CT, author voice): "These were claims made in debate, not an independent audit."
- **major**: P-14 duplicates P-13 and doesn't fit this chapter. Both open by busting a legend ('It's something more useful' in sts.chapter10.b0180; 'stranger and more useful' here). Both rules say 'use the new tool'. Both third practices mock AI purists (sts.chapter10.b0183 item 3; b0206 item 3). P-14's practice is solopreneur advice in a chapter about free meals. And b0204 admits a big firm's options are never an individual's, then b0206 applies the firm's story to an individual. (sts.chapter11.b0201, sts.chapter11.b0204, sts.chapter11.b0205, sts.chapter11.b0206, sts.chapter10.b0180, sts.chapter10.b0182, sts.chapter10.b0183) Quote (CT, author voice): "The real story is stranger and more useful."
- **major**: The Field Manual test fails where it matters. There's no price for a retired server, a 24 GB card, the radiator or the pump, and no cost per meal or budget for the food partnership. The only hard numbers are physics constants. 'You don't need enterprise money' with no dollar figure is a promise, not a manual. (sts.chapter11.b0133, sts.chapter11.b0139, sts.chapter11.b0163) Quote (CT, author voice): "You don't need enterprise money."
- **minor**: Telegraphing kills the suspense. The bullet list gives away the breach (nineteen households, 41 hours), b0143 flags that Curtis's objection 'mattered later', and b0147 announces the breach before it happens. (sts.chapter11.b0100, sts.chapter11.b0143, sts.chapter11.b0147) Quote (narrator, third-person scene): "The breach began with an ordinary convenience. Their system had no reliable stop between that convenience and everyone else's private life."
- **minor**: Structural glitch: 'The Foundations' heading (b0025) is followed immediately by another H2 (b0156), leaving an empty section. (sts.chapter11.b0025, sts.chapter11.b0156)
- **minor**: Misused metaphor. A Trojan horse is a hidden attack from inside, not a cheap product that funds the craft. (sts.chapter11.b0203) Quote (CT, author voice): "The quartz Trojan horse helped pay for the craft it seemed to threaten."
- **minor**: Internal inconsistency. Strangers praise the first incident report they'd read 'that was not written by lawyers', one block after Reuben maps the exposure with counsel and 'The lawyer checked the notice requirements' (b0152). (sts.chapter11.b0152, sts.chapter11.b0153) Quote (narrator, third-person scene): "Strangers wrote to say it was the first incident report they had ever read that was not written by lawyers."
- **minor**: Technical plausibility, unverified. 'Two slashes' is C or JavaScript comment syntax, while common reverse-proxy configs (nginx, Caddy) comment with '#', and the briefing calls it a 'config line'. A custom proxy written in code would make it work, but an engineer reader will flag it at the turning point of the story. (sts.chapter11.b0013, sts.chapter11.b0100) Quote (narrator, third-person scene): "While chasing a bug he commented out the authentication line, two slashes, to rule it out as the problem."
- **minor**: Homesteader logic gap. The co-op dumps up to 1,000 watts of heat outdoors through a radiator while Priya runs greenhouses. The heat math treats waste heat only as an air-conditioning cost, never as a resource. (sts.chapter11.b0140, sts.chapter11.b0144) Quote (CT, author voice): "Inside an insulated building, that's heat you're paying an air conditioner to fight."
- **minor**: Repetition. 'Local compute is one of the last private places left to think' appears verbatim twice (b0100, b0176). The 'bad Friday' line appears twice (Priya in b0151; CT in b0185). 'Owe ... the truth' appears twice in consecutive blocks (b0151, b0152). 'Receiving help isn't a moral failure' is said in b0129 and b0168, after sts.chapter10.b0151 and sts.part-3.b0009. The line cook is on his third outing (sts.introduction.b0107, sts.chapter7.b0166). 'Forty-one' is the view count in Chapter 10 and the exposure hours here. And b0176 opens by referring to 'The scene's argument', which breaks the fourth wall. (sts.chapter11.b0100, sts.chapter11.b0176, sts.chapter11.b0151, sts.chapter11.b0185, sts.chapter11.b0152, sts.chapter11.b0129, sts.chapter11.b0168, sts.chapter11.b0162, sts.chapter11.b0014) Quote (CT, author voice): "a design shouldn't depend on one careful person never having a bad Friday."
- **minor**: Self-own in the art. Chapters 10 and 11 argue that real human-made material is the scarce input, and this one illustrates itself with an 'AI-adapted cutout'. A hostile reviewer will screenshot the caption. (sts.chapter11.b0207, sts.chapter11.b0208) Quote (caption, author voice): "Capability still needs an arrangement for access. AI-adapted cutout"

### What readers would complain about

- *bored newcomer*: "Chapter title: King and Queen of Your Own Robotic Court. Contents: a 1941 Parliament transcript, a VRAM formula and the specific heat of glycol. Where are the robots? Last page: 'A robot may fit at one of those points as it gets more capable.' Great."
- *skeptical singularity regular*: "Running a 70B model at 4 bits on two 24 GB cards is old hobbyist news, and here it comes with three disclaimers. Meanwhile the book's AGI-in-2027 model can 'suggest wording.'"
- *infosec engineer*: "Two slashes in a proxy config? Then four characters each take a page to arrive at 'get a second reviewer.'"
- *farmer or homesteader*: "You've got a kilowatt of free heat and a greenhouse next door, and you pipe the heat to a radiator on the north wall?"
- *small restaurant owner*: "My question was what happens to my restaurant. The answer: 'Some could close. I can't promise every existing margin survives.' Thanks."
- *hostile podcast host*: "Ask this book 'what about the jobs?' and you get 64 words ending in 'That's what this whole book is arguing for.' That's not an answer, it's a redirect."
- *critic*: "Two Precedents in a row with the same rule. The monk and Swatch both say 'use the new tool,' and both practices mock the people who won't."
- *fiction reader*: "Again the honest video goes viral and strangers drive out to help for free. By now I can predict the reward before the scene ends."

### Weak writing

- `sts.chapter11.b0158` "King and Queen of Your Own Robotic Court is a playful name for a serious change in who gets help." : [CT, author voice] Explains the joke. The title's swagger gets apologized for in the first sentence of the Foundations.
- `sts.chapter11.b0158` "without first becoming rich, technical, or employable in this year's fashionable field." : [CT, author voice] A list of three run for rhythm, ending in a sneer.
- `sts.chapter11.b0159` "What matters is a usable claim on the result." : [CT, author voice] Stacked abstract nouns. Policy jargon.
- `sts.chapter11.b0159` "Can you ask for a form you can actually eat?" : [CT, author voice] 'A form' of food is unclear and reads like a typo for 'food'.
- `sts.chapter11.b0162` "There's no contradiction between those two arrangements. There's an operating budget to arrange." : [CT, author voice] A 'not X, there's Y' construction with a clumsy 'arrangements'/'arrange' echo.
- `sts.chapter11.b0163` "A local public body funds a food partnership for a set period. The partnership pays growers for agreed supplies and a kitchen to prepare meals." : [CT, author voice] White-paper abstraction ('local public body', 'food partnership', 'set period', 'agreed supplies'). Nothing a reader can picture or do.
- `sts.chapter11.b0179` "But encouragement was one thing and authority another." : [CT, author voice] Stock construction.
- `sts.chapter11.b0170` "There are many ways to make an omelette." : [CT, author voice] A mangled cliché used as a limp closer.
- `sts.chapter11.b0167` "Different problems, different responses." : [CT, author voice] Filler fragment.
- `sts.chapter11.b0175` "So whatever the arrangement, shared ownership, public provision, or paying private providers, ask who can raise the price, change the rules, withdraw the service, or sell the equipment, and how the people receiving it and the people working it can challenge those decisions." : [CT, author voice] A 47-word sentence stacking two lists. The most important question in the chapter can't be read aloud in one breath.
- `sts.chapter11.b0130` "somebody still has to maintain the equipment, recognize a bad result, and know when to stop. Paying, training, and supporting those people belongs inside the program." : [CT, author voice] Two lists of three back to back.
- `sts.chapter11.b0200` "That's a court worth building: help with a destination in somebody's life." : [CT, author voice] A vague closer. 'Help with a destination in somebody's life' isn't an image.
- `sts.chapter11.b0206` "Leverage without allocation is just drift." : [CT, author voice] A business-book aphorism built from abstract nouns.
- `sts.chapter11.b0154` "The analogy gave them a place to start; the network needed its own procedures." : [narrator, third-person scene] The editor correcting a character's metaphor in the middle of the narrative.
- `sts.chapter11.b0157` "It taught him the difference between knowing a system and operating one" : [narrator, third-person scene] States the moral the scene already showed. VOICE-GUIDE: cut the explanation.
- `sts.chapter11.b0017` "You don't service a press that can take your hand off until its energy sources are isolated, the stored energy is dealt with, and the lockout is verified." : [Marta Okonkwo, character] A regulatory definition in the mouth of a furious woman. Nobody in a cold rage says 'the stored energy is dealt with'.
- `sts.chapter11.b0142` "That's the required flow for these assumptions, not a pump specification." : [CT, author voice] Hedge.
- `sts.chapter11.b0135` "The examples use $B = 1.2$, an illustrative 20 percent allowance, not a guarantee." : [CT, author voice] Hedge. The memory section has three of these plus a caption that disowns its own chart.
- `sts.chapter11.b0149` "She looked at Elijah, and it was not a rescue." : [narrator, third-person scene] The narrator steps in to interpret a look the dialogue already made clear.

### Not in your voice

- `sts.chapter11.b0163` "Here's one design to argue about, not an existing program." : [CT, author voice] Policy-paper register. CT's draft makes the same point as 'If humanity could get its collective head out of its collective ass, we could be living in the Star Trek future or the Jetsons.' Same idea, different species.
- `sts.chapter11.b0181` "These were claims made in debate, not an independent audit." : [CT, author voice] A historian's footnote. CT's draft runs Mary Shelley's Frankenstein, the Golem, Terminator and The Matrix together in two paragraphs without a single hedge.
- `sts.chapter11.b0185` "An *air gap* separates the protected system from outside networks, with no wired or wireless network path across the boundary; moving data across it is a deliberate human act." : [CT, author voice] Glossary voice with a NIST citation in the middle of the paragraph. Nothing in CT's draft reads like a standards document.
- `sts.chapter11.b0151` "We put information together without asking whether it needed to be together. Then we treated his confidence as our check." : [Priya Ramanathan, character] Neither CT nor a soil biologist talks like this. It's blameless-postmortem doctrine. The Protocol says characters use history to win a fight, not to recite policy.
- `sts.chapter11.b0168` "The worry underneath is worth taking seriously: dependence, lost skills, a life with fewer choices." : [CT, author voice] A therapist's concede-first tone. CT's draft answers an objection by agreeing loudly and then swinging ('And you're right.' followed by the collective-ass line).
- `sts.chapter11.b0142` "That's the required flow for these assumptions, not a pump specification." : [CT, author voice] A liability engineer's voice. CT's draft never protects itself mid-sentence.
- `sts.chapter11.b0147` "The breach began with an ordinary convenience. Their system had no reliable stop between that convenience and everyone else's private life." : [narrator, third-person scene] Systems-safety abstraction in the narrative register, which the Voice Guide calls the book's strongest writing. Elsewhere in this same scene the narrator works through 'two slashes' and a marine pump the size of a fist.

### Where a newcomer gets lost

- `sts.chapter11.b0100`: 'company store' is a coal-town scrip reference many readers won't catch. 'food baseline', 'config line' and 'postmortem' are undefined in the briefing.
- `sts.chapter11.b0143`: 'a $211 power bill' is Chapter 5's setup. Read cold, it's a random number.
- `sts.chapter11.b0144`: 'a granddaughter of Silvana, open weights' assumes Chapter 0's fictional model and the term 'open weights'.
- `sts.chapter11.b0013`: 'a tunnel out, a reverse proxy' and 'commented out the authentication line' get no gloss for a non-technical reader, at the exact moment the story turns on them.
- `sts.chapter11.b0014`: 'the mesh journal' appears here for the first time with no setup. The mesh network isn't explained until Chapter 17.
- `sts.chapter11.b0162`: 'The line cook from Chapter 7' depends on sts.introduction.b0107 and sts.chapter7.b0166.
- `sts.chapter11.b0179`: 'British Restaurants', 'communal feeding centers' and 'parliamentary secretary' never get a plain one-line definition.
- `sts.chapter11.b0181`: 'Billingsgate workers' and 'Fishmongers' Hall' are London references with no gloss.
- `sts.chapter11.b0135`: 'parameters', 'quantized', 'key-value cache', 'context length' and 'llama.cpp' are undefined for a newcomer.
- `sts.chapter11.b0141`: 'water-glycol', $C_p$, 'J/kg·°C' and the mass-flow symbol arrive with no plain-language gloss.
- `sts.chapter11.b0017`: 'college' as Marta's name for Elijah only works if Chapters 6 to 9 survive the cut.

### Skim problems

- A skimmer can't find a thesis: the headings move through food, history, objections, ownership, hardware, physics, security, ethics, and back to food.
- 'The Foundations' is an empty heading followed by another heading.
- The security checklist (patch, minimize data, separate services, second reviewer, test outside reach) is buried mid-paragraph in b0185 rather than numbered, in a chapter that is supposed to be a field manual.
- 562 words of memory and heat math sit in the middle with five LaTeX blocks. A skimmer hits equation after equation.
- The scene runs about 1,550 words, past the Protocol's 800 to 1,200 guide, and its last third is procedure.
- Four Hansard citations in four consecutive paragraphs read as a wall of links.
- The 'In this chapter' bullets give away the breach, its scale and its duration.

### Best blocks

- `sts.chapter11.b0008`: The best opening image in Part III ('built like filing cabinets, sold for less than the co-op had spent that month on welding gas') plus Elijah's (character) 'We're mailing our diary to the company store, one page at a time, and paying the postage.'
- `sts.chapter11.b0144`: The physical anchoring the Protocol asks for: the Civic radiator, brass bulkheads, a marine pump the size of a fist, and 'The fire that had burned down his old life, purring in a stove he had built.'
- `sts.chapter11.b0013`: The breach as one physical, understandable act: 'He meant to put it back. He deployed with the comment marks still in.'
- `sts.chapter11.b0014`: Concrete stakes, and it plants Marta's Tuesday physical therapy, which pays off in Chapter 18: 'Tuesdays haven't fixed it and won't.' (Marta, sts.chapter18.b0135). Cutting it breaks that payoff.
- `sts.chapter11.b0015`: 'you probably want to lock your journal, brother' (the subscriber, character) and 'The internet does not run your deletion for you.' (narrator).
- `sts.chapter11.b0148`: Accountability shown as an action: he rings the dinner bell on a Sunday.
- `sts.chapter11.b0017`: 'You put our hands in the press, college.' (Marta, character) is the scene's hardest line.
- `sts.chapter11.b0149`: Marta's hearth speech uses the Prometheus allegory as a weapon in a fight, exactly as the Protocol specifies. This is where the scene should end.
- `sts.chapter11.b0154`: Load-bearing despite the memo tone: the second-reviewer rule is what Chapter 17 amends ('the two-eyes rule', sts.chapter17.b0144).
- `sts.chapter11.b0173`: With b0175, this is the ownership argument at the center of the v0.11 edition, stated plainly: 'Suppose the machines work beautifully.'
- `sts.chapter11.b0184`: 'Give a tool work that can be checked before its mistakes become someone else's emergency.' The best Field Manual sentence in the chapter.
- `sts.chapter11.b0131`: With b0199: 'I need meals I can heat with one working hand' echoes Rosa's braced wrist from Chapter 7 and turns the Part III method into a worksheet.
- `sts.chapter11.b0165`: 'a worker's hunger shouldn't be the protection a business model depends on' is CT's anger, aimed at a system and well calibrated.


## Chapter 12: The Land Strategy

**Verdict.** A chapter called The Land Strategy never gives the reader a land strategy. It is three pieces under one title: a strong kitchen-table scene, a cautious seminar on New Communities, and a mutual-aid yard-cleanup demo that reruns Chapter 9's food arithmetic and has nothing to do with land. The Foundations hedge so often that the chapter keeps backing away from its own title. The closing Precedent's rule (move fast during the confusion) is the business model of the Sacramento LLC the scene asks you to hate, and the chapter opens on an epigraph from a fictional Confederate planter.

**Publishable as is?** No. The scene (b0008 through b0155) is close to publishable but deflates after the tractor payoff and then explains itself. The Foundations fail the title, contradict themselves on government programs, and spend about 1,100 words (b0119 to b0137) on material that belongs to the food chapters. For the core cut: keep the scene spine, b0160 and b0161, at most one New Communities paragraph as the honest counterweight, and the Precedent practice. Drop the Grandma demo as a duplicate of Chapter 9 and Chapter 14, and drop the stacked disclaimers.

### Failure modes

- **critical**: There is no land strategy in the Land Strategy chapter. The only acquisition advice is one paragraph (b0160) and the Precedent practice (b0139). Roughly 1,113 words (Grandma's request, What the machines did, Count the result at the door, Right now) cover a yard cleanup and food delivery that never touch land, and they repeat Chapter 9's supply, commitments and spoilage arithmetic (chapter9 b0172 already does kilograms per household). The chapter then tells the reader land isn't required at all. (sts.chapter12.b0159, sts.chapter12.b0164, sts.chapter12.b0119, sts.chapter12.b0140, sts.chapter12.b0141, sts.chapter12.b0122, sts.chapter12.b0123, sts.chapter12.b0143, sts.chapter12.b0168, sts.chapter12.b0144, sts.chapter12.b0125, sts.chapter12.b0127, sts.chapter12.b0128, sts.chapter12.b0129, sts.chapter12.b0131, sts.chapter12.b0132, sts.chapter12.b0145, sts.chapter12.b0133) Quote (CT, author voice): "nobody should have to become a property owner before they get dinner."
- **critical**: The Precedent teaches the villain's playbook. The scene's antagonist wins through speed during other people's crises (estates, widows, fourteen-day closes; the bullet calls it 'speed and loneliness'). The Precedent takes a land opening built on Indigenous dispossession, adds one paragraph of conscience, then draws the rule: position early, during the confusion, don't wait. A hostile reviewer will quote b0112 and b0175 side by side, and the chapter has no answer. (sts.chapter12.b0112, sts.chapter12.b0173, sts.chapter12.b0174, sts.chapter12.b0175) Quote (CT, author voice (Precedent rule)): "Ask whose land and whose opportunity the story describes. Then don't wait for clarity to start your paperwork."
- **major**: The epigraph is a landmine. The chapter opens on a land-worship line from Gerald O'Hara, Margaret Mitchell's slaveholding Georgia planter, in a chapter that also tells of Black farmers in Georgia forced off land for trying to register to vote (b0163) and of Indigenous dispossession (b0173). The line's claim that land 'lasts' is also contradicted by the chapter's own account of New Communities losing its land to foreclosure in 1985 (b0165). (sts.chapter12.b0147, sts.chapter12.b0163, sts.chapter12.b0165, sts.chapter12.b0173) Quote (Gerald O'Hara, fictional character in Margaret Mitchell's Gone with the Wind (epigraph)): "for 'tis the only thing in this world that lasts."
- **major**: Compliance language has leaked into the fiction and deflates the climax. Reuben's enclosure speech is the best Argument-in-dialogue in Part III, and it ends in liability boilerplate. The tractor payoff lands, and the next block opens with paperwork review. The narration describing the trust reads like a term sheet. (sts.chapter12.b0151, sts.chapter12.b0154, sts.chapter12.b0156) Quote (Reuben, fictional character): "Those promises need review. They won't end every possible dispute. They give us terms somebody can read and hold us to. That's why we brought the folders."
- **major**: The scene explains its own meaning three times, breaking the Voice Guide's rule that a scene never announces its lesson: once in narration after the data, once in narration after the deed, and once in CT's voice in the Foundations. The third reason in b0157 is also false to how recording works. A recorder's clerk stamps documents and does not close deals by trusting anyone, and in b0156 Doreen only stamps. (sts.chapter12.b0150, sts.chapter12.b0157, sts.chapter12.b0164) Quote (Narrator, third-person scene voice): "What closed it was a fuel line fixed honest in 2011, a stack of questions Irene got answered in writing, and a clerk who trusted the man across the counter."
- **major**: The stakes are invented and paid off in the same chapter, while a real setup is left hanging. Calder ground, the month-to-month lease, Irene, Walt, Dale and the sixty-dollar fuel line all appear for the first time here, and no earlier chapter mentions them. So the payoff comes three blocks after its setup. Meanwhile the Hollis-place link to Chapter 3 is planted as a conspiracy clue and never resolved. (sts.chapter12.b0113, sts.chapter12.b0018, sts.chapter12.b0019, sts.chapter12.b0149) Quote (Narrator, third-person scene voice): "He circled the link for Reuben to check."
- **major**: The chapter contradicts itself on institutions. The Right now section says you don't need a federal program. In the same chapter, New Communities is broken by credit access and partly rescued by a USDA discrimination award, the Precedent is a federal program, and the practice sends the reader to rural development grants. (sts.chapter12.b0135, sts.chapter12.b0165, sts.chapter12.b0166, sts.chapter12.b0180, sts.chapter12.b0138, sts.chapter12.b0139) Quote (CT, author voice): "You don't need permission from a central board, and you don't need to wait for a federal program or a change in the political weather."
- **major**: Disclaimers pile up, and some argue with claims the reader never saw. By count: not a legal template (twice), an illustration not a report, the drawing promises neither a yield nor an autonomous crew, the 2027 expectation doesn't promise, a good Saturday isn't a guarantee, subject to the actual agreement, not local offers. The caloric-multiple and 'autonomous' corrections answer earlier drafts, not anything on the page. (sts.chapter12.b0164, sts.chapter12.b0184, sts.chapter12.b0122, sts.chapter12.b0170, sts.chapter12.b0144, sts.chapter12.b0131, sts.chapter12.b0132, sts.chapter12.b0133, sts.chapter12.b0178, sts.chapter12.b0182) Quote (CT, author voice): "anyone promising you a guaranteed multiple of your caloric needs is selling something."
- **major**: The singularity is missing from the singularity book. The introduction forecasts AGI by Thanksgiving 2027, yet the showcase of machine help here is a scheduler and a greenhouse controller that 'may have helped.' A regular reader will ask why this chapter is in this book. (sts.chapter12.b0131, sts.chapter12.b0132, sts.chapter12.b0127) Quote (CT, author voice): "Software helped schedule. A greenhouse controller may have helped grow the vegetables."
- **minor**: The chapter mocks charity's paperwork while running on paperwork. It sneers at form-in-triplicate charity twice, but its win is Reuben's folders, Irene's written questions, and a call to keep separate records on five categories. (sts.chapter12.b0129, sts.chapter12.b0136, sts.chapter12.b0145, sts.chapter12.b0154) Quote (CT, author voice): "without the condescension and the paperwork that usually come wrapped around charity."
- **minor**: New Communities is so hedged and so under-introduced that a newcomer misses what happened. 'She said', 'she described' and 'she recalled' blur what appears to be supply sabotage. Neither Charles nor Shirley Sherrod is identified, and SNCC appears only in a link. (sts.chapter12.b0163, sts.chapter12.b0167, sts.chapter12.b0165) Quote (CT, author voice, reporting Shirley Sherrod's account): "after receiving deliveries that, she said, weren't what the farm had ordered."
- **minor**: Mixed units. Vegetables are weighed in kilograms in a US book that prices land in dollars per acre and sets its rural scenes in feet and pounds. Readers in the rural US will notice. (sts.chapter12.b0128, sts.chapter12.b0144, sts.chapter12.b0178) Quote (CT, author voice): "Someone brings five kilograms of vegetables from a greenhouse that had more than it could use"

### What readers would complain about

- *newcomer*: "The chapter is called The Land Strategy. I got a real estate closing, a Georgia history lecture, and somebody's yard getting weeded. I still don't know how to get land."
- *renter*: "He tells me to 'consider ground' and to set aside years of property taxes, then says I don't need to own anything to eat. I can't afford rent. Pick one."
- *hostile podcast host*: "It opens with a Confederate planter's ode to land, then teaches you the lesson of the Homestead Act is to grab land fast while everyone's distracted. That's the shell-company villain's business plan with a land acknowledgment stapled on."
- *singularity regular*: "The author predicts AGI by 2027, and his big demo of machines helping grandma is scheduling software and a greenhouse controller that 'may have helped.' Why am I reading this in a singularity book?"
- *skeptic (real estate lawyer)*: "Every other paragraph says it's not a legal template, then the plot is resolved by a county clerk 'trusting' someone. Recording clerks don't approve anything. If you won't stand behind the mechanics, don't write them."
- *critic*: "The best scene in Part III (the widow, the tractor, 'the tone of a woman entering evidence') is followed by a paragraph about document review and then three explanations of what it meant. The book doesn't trust its own fiction."
- *farmer*: "Five kilograms of vegetables. A formula for 'available to share.' A national average price per acre he admits means nothing locally. Nobody out here talks like this."

### Weak writing

- `sts.chapter12.b0164` "What matters here's the question that brought those people to the table: how do we keep useful capacity serving the people who depend on it?" : Speaker: CT. Grammatical break from a mechanical contraction pass ('here's' for 'here is' mid-clause), followed by consultant abstraction ('useful capacity', used twice in this block).
- `sts.chapter12.b0164` "The paperwork doesn't abolish responsibility. It gives responsibility a shape people can read and hold each other to." : Speaker: CT. Explains the joke: it restates Reuben's own line from b0154 ('terms somebody can read and hold us to') in abstract nouns, two pages later.
- `sts.chapter12.b0161` "every person should have what they need to meet their survival needs, either on their own property or through a city that manages that infrastructure better than it does now. This chapter is about the first half of that sentence, without needing to be rich." : Speaker: CT. 'Need ... needs' redundancy, and 'without needing to be rich' is a dangling modifier with no subject.
- `sts.chapter12.b0166` "The arrangement challenged one source of dependence while remaining exposed to others. Its members needed fair access to finance as well as land, and institutions beyond the property capable of enforcing that fairness." : Speaker: CT. Academic drift: stacked abstract nouns (dependence, access, finance, institutions, fairness) and no person, place or action.
- `sts.chapter12.b0163` "Security on the ground was part of making a political right usable." : Speaker: CT. A seminar summary sentence where a concrete image (a family evicted for registering to vote) was needed.
- `sts.chapter12.b0180` "Reuben's folders therefore belong beside a larger set of questions." : Speaker: CT. Journal-paper 'therefore' and an empty abstraction, 'a larger set of questions'.
- `sts.chapter12.b0157` "The dashboard had been right about the timeline and the pattern, and none of it closed the deal." : Speaker: narrator. Scene moralizing: the reader already watched the fuel-line story beat the dashboard.
- `sts.chapter12.b0150` "The data couldn't close the gap. It could only tell them how wide it was." : Speaker: narrator. Explains the lesson before Reuben's 'It's not a data problem' line in the very next block states it again.
- `sts.chapter12.b0013` "It's a people problem wearing a paperwork costume." : Speaker: Reuben. Paired with his 'It's not a data problem', this is a 'not X, but Y' thesis put in a character's mouth in the third block of the scene. Also, 'dealing folders around the table like cards' recycles Reuben's card-dealing simile from Chapter 9 (b0072).
- `sts.chapter12.b0151` "The draft protected Irene's right to remain in the house and provided for regular payments under an agreement she would have her own adviser review." : Speaker: narrator. Term-sheet prose inside a scene: legal hedging written into the fiction.
- `sts.chapter12.b0129` "We aren't doing charity in the condescending, form-in-triplicate sense. We're maintaining our own neighborhood." : Speaker: CT. 'Not X, but Y' construction, followed in the same block by another one ('Free to her means somebody arranged it, not that it cost nothing.').
- `sts.chapter12.b0131` "Be precise about the credit." : Speaker: CT. Lecture tone: a schoolteacher's instruction aimed at the reader.
- `sts.chapter12.b0132` "That's a forecast to test against real tasks, real reliability, and real availability, and my 2027 expectation doesn't promise it'll be on her road that year." : Speaker: CT. A list of three 'real's for rhythm, then a hedge on the book's own headline forecast.
- `sts.chapter12.b0133` "A small co-op isn't humanity, and a good Saturday isn't a permanent guarantee." : Speaker: CT. A double negation hedging against a claim nobody made, just before the best sentence in the section.
- `sts.chapter12.b0134` "Let's skip the hand-wringing and the endless theory." : Speaker: CT. Cliché, and it comes after the most hand-wringing stretch of the chapter (b0131 to b0133), so it reads as unintended irony.
- `sts.chapter12.b0138` "While every headline was consumed by the emergency, the federal government opened millions of acres, offering one hundred sixty acres to eligible claimants, including women heads of household and immigrants who'd declared their intent to become citizens, who would live on the land and improve it for five years." : Speaker: CT. A 58-word run-on with two stacked 'who' clauses; the reader can't tell who must live on the land.
- `sts.chapter12.b0172` "The ordinary five-year route therefore ran beyond the Civil War's end in 1865; Daniel Freeman's surviving certificate of eligibility dates to January 1868." : Speaker: CT. Footnote pedantry in the body text: it corrects a chronology the reader never questioned and names a man it never introduces.
- `sts.chapter12.b0174` "Every land policy creates opportunity for some people while landing on the people already there." : Speaker: CT. Weak, vague verb ('landing on'), and it makes dispossession sound like weather.
- `sts.chapter12.b0112` "Grandma's request, followed all the way to her door: what the neighbors do, what the machines do, and what reaches her kitchen." : Speaker: CT. A list of three for rhythm in a bullet the Voice Guide says must be a claim with teeth; this is a topic list.
- `sts.chapter12.b0184` "Useful ground comes with people and obligations. Shared use needs consent, clear terms, and a way to challenge decisions. Conceptual illustration, not a legal template." : Speaker: caption, editorial voice. Abstract nouns stacked in threes, plus the chapter's second 'not a legal template'.

### Not in your voice

- `sts.chapter12.b0164` "The trust in the scene changes a set of relationships: who may use the ground, who decides its future, what Irene receives, and what the co-op must keep paying." : Speaker: CT. The 2024 draft never steps outside its own story to annotate it; it says 'Here's the thing' and swings. This is an editor's gloss in policy-memo nouns.
- `sts.chapter12.b0166` "This is a harder and more useful history than the promise that collective title makes a community safe forever." : Speaker: CT. The 2024 draft reaches for Frankenstein, the Golem and Star Trek and says 'we're a LOT closer than you think.' It never grades its own sources as 'more useful history.' This is academic voice.
- `sts.chapter12.b0167` "Shirley Sherrod's recollections bring the institution down to its working parts." : Speaker: CT. A literature-review sentence. CT's draft would tell what happened in one blunt line and react to it.
- `sts.chapter12.b0131` "Calling the whole thing "autonomous" would give machines credit for work people did." : Speaker: CT. Defensive correction of an earlier draft's claim. The 2024 CT oversells with joy ('ChatEinstein') and doesn't footnote his enthusiasm.
- `sts.chapter12.b0145` "Keep separate records for food, equipment time, travel, money, and remaining labor." : Speaker: CT. Bookkeeper voice. Nothing in the 2024 draft reads like an audit procedure.
- `sts.chapter12.b0180` "Who hears a complaint when a lender or supplier abuses its power?" : Speaker: CT. Governance-seminar framing. The 2024 draft's questions are visceral ('What does it mean to be human...'), not institutional-design prompts.
- `sts.chapter12.b0154` "They won't end every possible dispute." : Speaker: Reuben. The character's voice slips from a fighter who has 'been waiting my entire adult life for this exact fight' (b0013) into a disclaimer. That is the edit pass speaking, not the character, and not CT.
- `sts.chapter12.b0133` "A small co-op isn't humanity, and a good Saturday isn't a permanent guarantee." : Speaker: CT. The 2024 draft says 'This book will teach you.' This sentence apologizes in advance for helping.

### Where a newcomer gets lost

- `sts.chapter12.b0113`: Irene, Walt, Dale, Calder ground and the month-to-month lease all appear here for the first time. Nothing earlier tells the reader the co-op sits on a widow's leased land, so the threat arrives from nowhere.
- `sts.chapter12.b0150`: 'present for the greenhouse controllers' assumes the reader remembers Chapter 9's failed presentation. Without it, the grin means nothing.
- `sts.chapter12.b0152`: 'the channel's first real surplus' assumes Chapter 10's media channel.
- `sts.chapter12.b0157`: 'MADRONE ≠ DRONE and Sal's rule' are two private references (Chapter 0 and Chapter 8) dropped without a gloss at the chapter's emotional close.
- `sts.chapter12.b0149`: The Hollis place is a Chapter 3 detail. The 'same agent' clue is never resolved, so a newcomer expects a conspiracy payoff that never comes.
- `sts.chapter12.b0163`: Charles Sherrod is not identified as a civil rights organizer. SNCC appears only inside a link. 'After attempting to register' doesn't say register for what.
- `sts.chapter12.b0167`: Shirley Sherrod is introduced with no identity or relation to Charles Sherrod.
- `sts.chapter12.b0127`: 'a local model on the co-op's own server' is undefined jargon for someone who doesn't know what a locally run language model is.
- `sts.chapter12.b0125`: 'a simple mesh radio' is undefined until Chapter 14 and Chapter 17.
- `sts.chapter12.b0132`: 'my 2027 expectation' is a renamed reference to the Introduction's 'AGI by Thanksgiving 2027' forecast. A skimmer won't connect them.
- `sts.chapter12.b0123`: The shouse is introduced here and said to be built in Chapter 13, where it is defined again twice. The newcomer meets the concept three times in two different proportions.
- `sts.chapter12.b0119`: The heading says 'Grandma', but the body never calls her that. The chapter already has a widow (Irene), and Elijah's grandmother is a running figure, so readers will conflate the three.
- `sts.chapter12.b0172`: Daniel Freeman is named without being identified as the first homestead claimant, and 'a paid route to earlier title' (commutation) is never explained.
- `sts.chapter12.b0105`: 'Precedent P-15' is a code with no in-chapter explanation of what a Precedent block is or why it is numbered.

### Skim problems

- Two H2 headings stack with nothing between them ('The Foundations' b0024, then 'A place the promise can stand' b0158). It reads as a formatting error.
- The Foundations headings are abstract and tell a skimmer nothing: 'A place the promise can stand', 'Land that could hold a right', 'Count the result at the door'. No heading mentions buying, leasing or trusts.
- The one actionable land checklist (access, water, permitted uses, insurance, property-tax reserve) is buried mid-paragraph in b0160 with no bold, list or pull quote.
- The best-formatted part of the chapter (the bold run-ins The nodes, The balance, The request, The coordination, The delivery) belongs to the Grandma demo, which isn't about land. So skimmers latch onto the off-topic section.
- The 'By the numbers' aside (b0176 to b0179) and the line chart (b0181) give national averages that the caption admits are 'not inflation-adjusted values or local offers', so the most scannable data is self-declared useless.
- The Precedent's bold 'The rule.' line (b0175) is the most skimmable sentence in the closing section, and it says 'don't wait for clarity'. A skimmer leaves with the villain's lesson and misses the Indigenous-land paragraph above it.

### Best blocks

- `sts.chapter12.b0008`: Narrator. 'The third letter came with a young man attached.' A perfect cold open: menace in eleven words, without explaining anything.
- `sts.chapter12.b0113`: Narrator. It sets the stakes in one paragraph through concrete things: Walt's equipment barn, the month-to-month lease, 'an arrangement that worked by being talked about over pie,' and Dale's one-word 'Mom.'
- `sts.chapter12.b0148`: Narrator. The predatory pipeline made specific (eleven LLCs, one agent, fifty parcels), with Reuben's honest caveat and Elijah coloring 'the uncertain links a different shade.' It is investigation shown, not asserted.
- `sts.chapter12.b0154`: Reuben. 'There's a machine that's been running since the fifteen hundreds, Dale. It turns commons into fences.' The chapter's thesis carried in character, through to 'What's on this table is that machine in reverse.' Keep it even if the disclaimer tail gets dropped by a block-level cut.
- `sts.chapter12.b0018`: Irene. 'Marta. Walt's tractor, back when. What did you charge him?' The turn of the whole scene, in one question.
- `sts.chapter12.b0019`: Marta. 'Sixty ... Dealer wanted nine hundred.' The payoff in numbers, with no moral attached.
- `sts.chapter12.b0155`: Narrator and Irene. 'In the tone of a woman entering evidence,' then she takes control: 'I want my questions written down before anybody signs.' Irene stays the decision-maker, which keeps the trust from reading as a rescue.
- `sts.chapter12.b0152`: Denny. 'So the machine that took my warehouse job is helping buy ground it can never take back,' then 'And we still have to keep the promise.' It ties Part III to the book's first wound in two lines.
- `sts.chapter12.b0160`: CT. The only real land advice in the chapter: buy what you can hold, reserve years of property tax, check access, water, permitted uses, insurance. 'A deed alone doesn't make a build possible.'
- `sts.chapter12.b0161`: CT. His actual thesis note, in his own words, and it points to Chapter 19. It is clumsy but it is the spine.
- `sts.chapter12.b0137`: CT. The best Field Manual sentence in the chapter: 'The first piece of infrastructure might be an accurate phone number and somebody who answers it.' Specific, doable this week.
- `sts.chapter12.b0133`: CT. Worth keeping for its last sentence alone: 'help will come, and she doesn't have to get poorer, more grateful, or more useful to keep getting it.' The best statement of the book's moral promise in Part III.
- `sts.chapter12.b0139`: CT. A concrete practice with an evening, a spreadsheet and a date: 'To turn "someday, land" from a mood into a document with numbers in it.'


## Chapter 13: The Shouse Protocol

**Verdict.** The scene is some of the best prose in the book. The pier, the vise, the cut, the Fisher stove and the mother's 'It looks like somewhere' should survive almost whole. The Foundations betray the title: a chapter called The Shouse Protocol has no dollar figure, no parts list and no protocol. It has a Fourier equation, a 368-word Vienna source-criticism seminar, and three separate passages telling the reader they may not need to build anything. A caption still refers to 'the chapter’s budget' and the text to 'a materials subtotal', but neither is anywhere in the chapter, so the hedges point at numbers that were cut.

**Publishable as is?** No. The scene (b0147 through b0153) is publishable nearly as is, once the compliance patches in b0152 and b0019 are weighed and the explanation in b0153 is reconsidered. The Foundations are not publishable. They contradict the title, define the shouse twice in conflicting proportions, cite a missing budget, and spend more words on Vienna's archives than on building. For the core cut: keep the scene, b0164, b0139 item 2's spirit via b0139, b0140, b0160, b0133, and the Sears Precedent (b0138, b0179, b0181). Drop the Fourier block, the Vienna section, b0155, b0156 and b0137.

### Failure modes

- **critical**: A Field Manual chapter with no numbers, parts or protocol. There isn't a single dollar figure in the chapter, though the Voice Guide's own model Field Manual line is 'Two steel boxes and roughly $25,000.' A caption and a paragraph still refer to a budget and a subtotal that aren't there. The tools list names no product. A reader cannot answer 'how much?' or 'what do I buy?' (sts.chapter13.b0171, sts.chapter13.b0141, sts.chapter13.b0139, sts.chapter13.b0160, sts.chapter13.b0144) Quote (Caption, editorial voice): "the chapter’s budget is fictional."
- **major**: The chapter argues against its own title. b0156 says any housing will do, b0157 commands 'Build a shouse', b0159 calls it 'one attempt', and the closing assignment suggests building nothing. A reader comes out of 'The Shouse Protocol' unsure whether the book recommends a shouse. (sts.chapter13.b0156, sts.chapter13.b0157, sts.chapter13.b0159, sts.chapter13.b0143, sts.chapter13.b0137) Quote (CT, author voice): "It might lead to a shared bench and no construction at all."
- **major**: The shouse is defined three times in two chapters, with conflicting proportions. Chapter 12 b0123 and Chapter 13 b0157 say half and half (as does the Appendix F glossary), and then CT's own original note in b0164, two blocks later, says seventy-thirty. (sts.chapter13.b0157, sts.chapter13.b0164, sts.chapter12.b0123) Quote (CT, author voice (quoting his own earliest note)): "Seventy percent production, thirty percent survival."
- **major**: The Vienna section is a source-criticism seminar, not a story. Across 368 words it never says what Karl-Marx-Hof was, how many people it housed, or what rent cost, and it never connects back to the shouse or Elijah. Most of its sentences say what the sources can't prove. Naming 'Karl-Marx-Hof' without context also hands a hostile reviewer a free headline. (sts.chapter13.b0162, sts.chapter13.b0163, sts.chapter13.b0165, sts.chapter13.b0166, sts.chapter13.b0167, sts.chapter13.b0183) Quote (CT, author voice): "They don't establish that every applicant obtained a home, every tenant was satisfied, or another city could simply copy the arrangement."
- **major**: Fourier's law in LaTeX, with Q-dot notation, in a book meant to bring total newcomers up to speed. It is a skim-killer that adds nothing the plain sentence after it doesn't already say, and it risks rendering badly in plain-text and EPUB editions. (sts.chapter13.b0129, sts.chapter13.b0173, sts.chapter13.b0130) Quote (CT, author voice): "Everything depends on the insulation layer."
- **major**: Safety-compliance patches sit inside the scene and at its emotional peak. The phone call that answers the failed call in Chapter 3, the book's longest-running setup, has a permit report at its center. The stove paragraph lists inspector, installer and listed chimney before the payoff, and even the socks joke carries a clearance warning. (sts.chapter13.b0019, sts.chapter13.b0152, sts.chapter13.b0016) Quote (Elijah, fictional character): "Inside. Insulated chimney through the roof. Installer picked the system; inspector signed it Tuesday."
- **major**: The 'In this chapter' bullets promise content the chapter doesn't deliver. The heat bullet promises a takeaway about harvested heat, but the Foundations have no section on wood heat, firewood, flue safety or heating cost, only an insulation equation. The bullet's punchline also depends on Chapter 5. (sts.chapter13.b0146) Quote (CT, author voice (chapter bullets)): "Heat you harvest beats heat you subscribe to. The oldest technology in this book has a flue now."
- **minor**: The scene explains itself at both of its turns, against the Voice Guide's rule that the vise already said it. The narrator steps in to insist Elijah's lane was real, and the final block spells out what the photo meant after the mother's line already said it. (sts.chapter13.b0150, sts.chapter13.b0153) Quote (Narrator, third-person scene voice): "here is the part he would want said plainly: his lane turned out to be real work, and he was good at it."
- **minor**: Figure placement and caption spoil the best beat. The vise image and its caption sit between the snap (b0011) and Marta's speech (b0149), and the caption uses her line before she says it, giving a character's words to the editorial voice. (sts.chapter13.b0115, sts.chapter13.b0116, sts.chapter13.b0149) Quote (Caption, editorial voice, borrowing Marta's line): "The vise. The metal does the truth. Bright and crystalline."
