- `b0185` "Algae can contribute to some diets; that doesn't show that this prototype replaces balanced feed, its mineral inputs, or the work of maintaining water quality." : Peer-review hedging. Nothing in the 2024 draft sounds like a methods section. CT would make the point in one line or not at all.
- `b0123` "But identifying, containing, and disposing of dangerous material is a job for trained people with the right equipment." : Public-service-announcement register, a third triplet in a row. The CT-sounding line is the next one, 'Unknown material is never a neighborhood experiment.'

### Where a newcomer gets lost

- `b0134`: 'flashed' (firmware flashing) is used as a verb before anything explains it.
- `b0136`: 'the LoRa lattice' arrives before LoRa is explained (b0118, and in Chapter 14). In a cut without Chapter 14 it's opaque.
- `b0011`: 'the checklist law' points at a Chapter 11 rule that the text never calls a checklist.
- `b0138`: 'buck converter', 'service cable' and 'startup log' are unexplained. 'The vise, the shovel' are callbacks to Chapters 13 and 15 that mean nothing if those scenes are cut.
- `b0108`: 'the known-good image and configuration, hashes checked' is sysadmin shorthand a newcomer can't parse.
- `b0143`: 'college' is Marta's nickname for Elijah, set up in Chapter 6. Cold, it reads as a typo.
- `b0152`: The field-kit figure shows a 'cyberdeck' that the chapter never mentions. Only Appendix E explains it.
- `b0127`: 'hand-winding axial-flux alternators' is unexplained.
- `b0163`: 'inverter pair' is unexplained. The reader doesn't know why there would be two.
- `b0118`: 'Chapter 14 made the case for a neighborhood mesh' assumes Chapter 14 survives the cut.
- `b0148`: 'Begin with Chapter 9's chain' doesn't work on its own; the section's whole content is a pointer to another chapter.
- `b0167`: 'CNC plasma table' is unexplained, and 'trained like Marta' sets a fictional character as the training standard in the nonfiction.
- `b0099`: Curtis appears with no introduction. A reader of a cut that drops Chapters 7, 9 and 11 has never met him.

### Skim problems

- The briefing bullet 'Debugging in the dark is a solved problem: halve it' isn't backed up anywhere in the Foundations. A skimmer who skips the fiction never gets the method.
- The body-metaphor headings (nervous system, muscle, skeleton) set up a structure, then the chapter abandons it for 'Food', 'Cleanup' and 'Help that reaches a body'. The skimmer loses the thread.
- A skimmer who reads only pictures and captions mostly gets disclaimers: b0155, b0157, b0163, b0165, b0166 and b0184 all say what something is not.
- The same workbench photo appears twice (b0002 and the AI-adapted collage b0180), so the chapter looks padded.
- Two homework lists close the chapter back to back (b0170 and b0176), and they overlap.
- The longest paragraphs sit in the Precedent (b0172 at 130 words, b0173 at 144 words), where the skimmer is least invested and the history least load-bearing.
- An HTML comment (b0182) sits in the block stream between a list and a figure.

### Best blocks

- `b0098`: Four short sentences that turn a technical outage into personal guilt: 'The hole was only in the map. But the hole was his.' Narrator. This is the Narrative register at full strength.
- `b0099`: Curtis lacing his boots 'amounted to a verdict', and Marta's 'Talk before you touch.' Character shown through habit, no exposition.
- `b0100`: The most physical writing in Part III: madrone trunks, the multimeter kept warm inside the coat, Marta running the net 'the way she'd once run a floor'.
- `b0137`: Marta's 'Power or brain? Halve it.' is the only place the chapter actually teaches the debugging method its bullets promise.
- `b0138`: The diagnosis done step by step with real readings (12.9, 5.1) and an honest root cause: he 'accepted one successful transmission as a test'. Keep it, though the last sentence needs CT's knife.
- `b0108`: The payoff: the known-good image in the dry bag, Priya's 'North houses just came back. Hello, ridge.', and the constellation image. Earned, not explained.
- `b0143`: Marta's 'Gods with cold fingers and a multimeter, that's the job.' is the chapter's thesis in the right mouth and the right register. Trim the speech around it, not the line.
- `b0144`: 'They came down. There was soup.' This lands the scene, and the Conclusion (sts.conclusion.b0126) pays off 'the flash done solo'. Load-bearing for the ending.
- `b0114`: The chapter's actual definition, and a CT-sounding hammer: 'it's less a tool than a leash.'
- `b0127`: A real builder (EasyGrow) with a real quote: 'I'm not in business to get rich. I'm in business to grow food and help people grow food.' It does more than SECTOR 07 with fewer hedges.
- `b0150`: The one non-obvious, safety-critical fact in the mesh section: 'Meshtastic's default shared channel key is public.' A newcomer would get burned without it.
- `b0146`: Two sentences that make the case for local fabrication better than the three paragraphs and two figures after them: 'you're still waiting on permission.'
- `b0170`: The chapter's real Monday list (two boards, a DC corner, solder). Items 1 and 2 are exactly what the VOICE-GUIDE asks of Part III.
- `b0174`: 'A catalog of tools is secretly a map of possible selves.' The best line in the precedent, and the precedent's whole argument.


## Chapter 18: The Roadmap and the Premortem Pivot

**Verdict.** The premortem meeting is the emotional peak of the co-op arc. Marta's shoulder is the best planted payoff in the book (the PT Tuesdays from Chapter 11), and the Devendra arrival closes the broom loop cleanly. Everything around those scenes sags. A grant-committee subplot is written as fiction, with a diagram in the middle of the scene. A meta line spends the Conclusion's last word early. The Foundations isn't a roadmap, answers none of the hard objections it raises, and reruns food-service governance material from Chapters 9, 11 and 19. Its own precedent admits the analogy doesn't hold.

**Publishable as is?** No. The scene from b0130 through b0071 and the Devendra arrival (b0073 to b0154) are publishable and load-bearing for the Conclusion. The kitchen-funding subplot (b0136 to b0143, b0186, b0187) and b0145 are not. The Foundations isn't publishable: there's no roadmap, the objections it raises go unanswered, it duplicates Chapters 9, 11 and 19, its landlord pitch contradicts Chapter 17, and its precedent defeats itself. For the core cut, about 1,135 words of opener and scene survive, plus about 920 words of Foundations: b0156, b0098, b0103 to b0105, b0100, b0111, b0115, b0128, b0125, and the P-22 story, mechanism, rule and practice (b0178, b0181 to b0184).

### Failure modes

- **critical**: The title promises 'The Roadmap and the Premortem Pivot'. There's no roadmap (no phases, no sequence, no timeline), and the word 'pivot' never appears in the body. Late in the chapter it says 'This roadmap is written for...' about a roadmap it never drew. What the reader actually gets is a premortem worksheet for a meal-delivery route. (b0001, b0003, b0101, b0117, b0163) Quote (CT author voice): "This roadmap is written for two audiences, and they need the same thing."
- **critical**: It raises the reader's four strongest objections (supply chains, guns, people without work, the powerful crushing alternatives), tells the reader to name a risk and build against it, and then answers none of them. 'What could stall it' swaps in a different list (war, chips, quartz, backlash) that is about AI progress, not about whether the co-op survives. A skeptic notices the dodge immediately. (b0156, b0098, b0164, b0102, b0165) Quote (CT author voice (voicing the reader's objection)): "Somebody with more guns takes the greenhouse."
- **major**: It duplicates three other chapters. 'Build the machine and the promise together', 'A proposal people can vote on' and 'Grow the promise' rerun Chapter 11's 'Free to eat, paid for somewhere' (sts.chapter11.b0164: 'Donations can start a project. They shouldn't quietly carry a permanent promise nobody's actually funded.') and Chapter 19's rungs. 'What could stall it' duplicates Chapter 19's 'What would stop it'. By Chapter 18 the reader has heard the donations point three times. (b0159, b0103, b0104, b0105, b0106, b0160, b0107, b0108, b0109, b0110, b0112, b0121, b0119, b0122, b0123, b0124, b0170, b0102) Quote (CT author voice): "Donations can add to it, but they don't carry the core promise, because donations come and go."
- **major**: The kitchen-funding subplot turns the book's best scene into grant minutes. The register switches mid-meeting from Marta's shoulder to an unnamed coordinator's invoice math. Reuben's column reads like a policy brief, and a figure captioned as an illustration of the fiction interrupts the scene. Irony: the only unnamed person in the room is in a scene whose moral (b0144) is that every risk needs a name. (b0136, b0137, b0138, b0139, b0140, b0141, b0142, b0143, b0186, b0187) Quote (narrator (Elijah scene)): "None of that guaranteed a vote or supplied an unpaid meal. It gave them a request with costs, responsibilities, and people behind it."
- **major**: b0145 spends the Conclusion's bookend. The book ends on 'You're holding it.' (sts.conclusion.b0019), and two sections earlier the narrator has already told the reader the notebook became 'the book you are nearly done holding'. The surprise is gone before the final line arrives. The same block also uses a 'not X, but Y' construction to explain what the notebook 'was trying' to be. (b0145) Quote (narrator (Elijah scene)): "He started, that week, turning the notebook into the book you are nearly done holding."
- **major**: 'Who this is for' shows up in the second-to-last chapter. It names two audiences, neither of which is CT's stated three (newcomers, singularity regulars, readers wanting a different perspective). It skips the laid-off 45-year-old the book is built around (Denny, the line cook). It also pitches property owners on machines because human labor 'has gotten too expensive', which is the same labor-cost pitch Chapter 17 b0128 sneers at. (b0174, b0101, b0175, b0176, b0177) Quote (CT author voice): "the same machines can help maintain and upgrade housing, land, and infrastructure that human labor alone has gotten too expensive to keep up."
- **major**: The book's measuring standard is a capitalized coinage built on the word 'Adequate'. Against the subtitle 'We Don't Have to Live Like This', the banner is bureaucratic and deflating, and it shows up for the first time in Chapter 18 of 19. Its figure text slides into abstract nouns. (b0067, b0166, b0168, b0172, b0173) Quote (CT author voice): "So here's the standard I want to measure a community by."
- **major**: The premortem goes uncredited. It's Gary Klein's technique, and he's cited nowhere in the book, Appendix B included. An informed critic will flag the chapter's central tool as presented without its source. The only definition is a metaphor that hinges on 'falsify', which a lay reader takes to mean 'fake'. (b0067, b0117) Quote (CT author voice (briefing bullet)): "A premortem is an obituary you write early enough to falsify."
- **major**: The precedent defeats itself. After three paragraphs selling Y2K as anticipatory engineering, the author concedes it doesn't transfer. The middle paragraph is 128 words of agency names (HUD, Oak Ridge, SSA) with page-range citations. (b0179, b0180, b0181) Quote (CT author voice): "Y2K had a known deadline and a bounded class of bug. This transition has neither."
- **minor**: The worksheet table and the worksheet figure show the same six questions, then a caption disclaims the figure. (b0079, b0163, b0188, b0189) Quote (CT author voice (caption)): "An illustrative worksheet from this chapter's premortem record, not the measured performance of a service."
- **minor**: The briefing bullets spoil the scene ('the shoulder' means nothing before the meeting), and bullet 4 restates the Eisenhower epigraph directly above it as the author's own words. (b0067, b0129) Quote (CT author voice (briefing bullet)): "Plans are worthless. Planning is everything. The people doing the planning are the plan."
- **minor**: The narrator explains callbacks and character, against the VOICE-GUIDE: the broom hand-off is labeled liturgy, Elijah's competence is summarized for the reader, and the names column is given its moral. (b0131, b0144, b0148) Quote (narrator (Elijah scene)): "the way liturgy works, the way they had been said to him on the exact spot he was standing"
- **minor**: Fact-check and compliance residue has leaked into list items, fiction and dialogue. The reader is told the world hasn't run out of sand, a claim they never heard. Elijah's privacy hygiene is narrated mid-epiphany. Marta, whose register is 'Things die of people, college', speaks in liability language. (b0102, b0144, b0118, b0070) Quote (CT author voice): "A disruption matters at a specific link, not because the world has run out of ordinary sand."
- **minor**: Disclaimers duplicated from Chapter 17. Chapter 17 b0141 has already said that food first doesn't mean everything else waits, and the 'tool doesn't pick' line is the fourth instance in the book. (b0116, b0106) Quote (CT author voice): "The tool doesn't pick. We do."

### What readers would complain about

- *newcomer*: "The chapter title promised a roadmap. I got a worksheet for running a food bank's delivery route."
- *skeptic*: "He writes 'Somebody with more guns takes the greenhouse' and then just never answers it. Fear wearing the costume of caution? That's an author wearing the costume of an answer."
- *singularity regular*: "The only singularity content in the roadmap chapter is a five-item list of what could slow AI down, and item five is 'things nobody's thought of'."
- *reader under 25*: "It tells me my anger is justified, then turns around and tells landlords robots are great because human labor got too expensive."
- *laid-off worker in his forties*: "'Who this is for': people under twenty-five and property owners. I'm the guy in every one of his examples and he forgot to list me."
- *critic*: "The premortem is Gary Klein's. The whole chapter is built on it, and he doesn't get a single citation."
- *hostile podcast host*: "After 400 pages the big new standard is the 'Adequate Level of Care'. Adequate. That's the revolution?"
- *fiction reader*: "Best scene in the book, Marta's shoulder, the box left open, and then it turns into the minutes of a funding committee, with a diagram."
- *reader who finishes the book*: "Chapter 18 already told me the notebook became the book I'm holding, so 'You're holding it.' landed like a rerun."
- *skeptic*: "His own precedent says Y2K doesn't apply. Then why is it the precedent?"

### Weak writing

- `b0124` "There will be fights about money and ownership, arguments about methods, and failures nobody saw coming. None of that means the way things are now is the only way they can be." : A list of three for rhythm, then a limp abstract hammer. CT author voice.
- `b0127` "Wide lens, narrow focus." : Slogan cliche, also used in Appendix C. CT author voice.
- `b0177` "The old arrangement is breaking under us. It's time to start building the next one." : A generic closer that could end any activist pamphlet. CT author voice.
- `b0168` "because it's urgent, concrete, and measurable" : A list of three for rhythm. The next sentence repeats the move ('Each needs its own expertise and evidence'). CT author voice.
- `b0110` "Each choice puts different people in charge, so everyone should see the options side by side." : Committee voice ('everyone should see the options'). CT author voice.
- `b0172` "Adequate provision is a commitment to verify, not an achieved effect of robots or a closed loop." : Abstract nouns stacked, plus a 'not X' construction, in figure alt text. CT author voice (alt text).
- `b0173` "Tools help; remaining work, supplies and reliable delivery still need provision." : Bureaucratese ('still need provision'). CT author voice (caption).
- `b0102` "There's always a column for the unknown." : Filler, a list item that says nothing. CT author voice.
- `b0165` "If progress stalls, people still need to eat, and everything in this book still helps." : Overclaim ('everything in this book'). CT author voice.
- `b0175` "and that's a structural failure, not a failure of your ambition." : A 'not X' construction that flatters the reader. Pandering. CT author voice.
- `b0182` "Prevention is invisible by construction." : Math jargon ('by construction') in a sentence meant for everyone. CT author voice.
- `b0184` "the share of income and hours a week you put into skills, soil, savings, and community" : An alliterative list for sound, with no number, in a practice that claims to set a budget. CT author voice.
- `b0136` "She couldn't turn a promise of another grant into a paid invoice." : Flat accounting prose in a scene. The coordinator has no name, no body and no voice. Narrator.
- `b0145` "not notes toward surviving something, but a manual for it, with the open boxes left open" : A 'not X, but Y' construction that explains the book's own design to the reader. Narrator.
- `b0158` "a valley full of successful founders would still leave most of the world outside the fence." : Confusing. 'Founders' of what? It undercuts the co-op in a muddle instead of a clear limit. CT author voice.
- `b0107` "It's an example, not a program that's been funded." : A disclaimer as the section's second sentence. It kills momentum before the proposal starts. CT author voice.
- `b0131` "with the quiet competence of a man who had finally found the exact right size for his gift" : The narrator grades the character instead of showing him work. Narrator.
- `b0144` "because a risk without a name attached isn't a risk, it's a wish with error bars" : A good line used to explain the scene's moral. An 'isn't X, it's Y' construction. Narrator.

### Not in your voice

- `b0142` "Together they could take a defined proposal to the public meeting where the next food-service allocation would be discussed." : County-memo prose inside fiction. The 2024 draft and the ELIJAH-PROTOCOL's gritty, sensory register both point the other way. Nothing here can be seen, smelled or touched.
- `b0168` "Each needs its own expertise and evidence, and each can be organized around the same question: do people actually receive it?" : Grant-application voice. The CT of 2024 asks the reader 'What's your role when the machines wake up?', not 'can each be organized around the same question'.
- `b0176` "Property owners, local institutions, community stewards: the same machines can help maintain and upgrade housing, land, and infrastructure" : The 2024 draft says 'Capitalism fights to the last breath and takes a lot of people with it.' This is a sales pitch to the people the book elsewhere says hold the leash.
- `b0118` "Keep people's personal details out of public records unless they're needed and the people agree." : Data-protection boilerplate. Necessary in a policy, but it's nobody's voice, least of all CT's.
- `b0070` "We need someone qualified to cover the gap. Starting an apprentice doesn't make one finished." : Spoken by Marta, and not Marta. Two paragraphs earlier she says 'Things die of people, college.' This line sounds like a liability reviewer wrote it into her mouth.
- `b0110` "The county could run it, or pay several groups to run it." : An options memo. No second person, no heat, no image. The CT-sounding move in the same section is 'You shouldn't have to be liked to be fed.' (b0111).

### Where a newcomer gets lost

- `b0130`: Five unexplained references in the opening paragraph: 'the shouse', 'the trust's scheduled payment to Irene Calder', 'the library's download counter', 'Frank's first full year at the tool library', 'Tyler's name added to the roster'.
- `b0131`: 'the Crucible' (Chapter 11), 'The Memphis platform buying their steel distributor the way it bought Frank's' (Chapter 14), 'every stage' (the nine stages, Part I), and 'epochs' (ML jargon, in b0132).
- `b0145`: 'MADRONE ≠ DRONE', '1:40 in the morning at a kitchen table', and 'the repair he had misunderstood and the meal they had delivered wrong' all depend on Chapter 0, Chapter 9 and Chapter 7 surviving the cut.
- `b0073`: Devendra and 'a Claypot all-hands' need Chapters 1 and 14. Without them the arrival is a stranger in a car.
- `b0153`: 'Swarf' is never defined here. The callback only works if Chapter 6's broom scene is kept.
- `b0140`: Rosa's wrist and her warehouse job come from Chapter 7. Cold, she's a name on a note.
- `b0107`: 'the five-household trial' assumes Chapter 9's Foundations.
- `b0116`: 'The fired cook's dinner' assumes the Chapter 7 and Introduction line cook.
- `b0067`: 'premortem' is defined only as a metaphor, and 'falsify' reads to a lay reader as 'fake'.
- `b0102`: 'fabs' is unexplained, and the quartz-crucible item answers a sand claim the reader never heard.
- `b0012`: 'the lever' (Denny's channel as the co-op's leverage, Chapter 16) is used as if established.

### Skim problems

- The Foundations has eleven H2 headings, several nearly identical ('Build the machine and the promise together', 'A proposal people can vote on', 'Grow the promise at the speed you can keep it', 'Keeping people whole while it's built'). A skimmer can't tell them apart or find the one thing to do.
- The worksheet table (b0163) and the worksheet figure (b0188) present the same six questions back to back.
- Briefing bullet 4 repeats the Eisenhower epigraph that sits directly above it, and bullet 2 refers to 'the shoulder' before the reader knows whose.
- The scene runs about 1,450 words, and the funding subplot in its middle (b0136 to b0143) has no hook a skimmer can grab.
- 'Who this is for' arrives in Chapter 18. A skimmer deciding whether the book is for them never reaches it.
- The Precedent's densest paragraph (b0180, 128 words of HUD, Oak Ridge, SSA and GAO page ranges) sits where the skimmer wants the punchline.

### Best blocks

- `b0130`: Elijah's 'It's two years from now, and the co-op is dead. I'd like to spend two hours on how it died.' is the best chapter-opening line in Part III and teaches the premortem in one breath.
- `b0131`: 'modeled, modeled, modeled, modeled' is rhythm doing argument. It sets up the fall without the narrator saying so.
- `b0010`: Marta's 'Your machine's got no column for the stuff that actually kills these things' is the turn of the whole chapter, delivered in her voice.
- `b0132`: Marta's 'the spreadsheet was the coroner, not the cause. Things die of people, college.' is the chapter's thesis said by the person who earned it.
- `b0012`: Burnout named, and Denny 'started to argue and then didn't'. Shown, not told.
- `b0133`: 'nineteen households is eighteen marriages and change' (narrator, in Reuben's free indirect voice). Funny and true, and it advances the plot.
- `b0134`: Frank's founder-dependence point and 'wearing all four of their faces': the room as the risk.
- `b0135`: Marta's 'Shoulder's done' pays off the Chapter 11 PT Tuesdays and gives Tyler a future. The best planted payoff in the book.
- `b0071`: 'INTERIM WELDING CAPACITY: UNRESOLVED, and left the box open.' This is honesty as craft, and it's the book's ethic in one image.
- `b0140`: Rosa's note, 'And can you tell us before the week it stops?', is the one block of the funding subplot that works as fiction. It's the human cost in a single question.
- `b0153`: Elijah's 'Swarf goes in the red bin.' closes the Chapter 6 broom loop, and the Conclusion relies on Devendra arriving 'with the children'.
- `b0152`: Curtis's 'Dinner's dinner.' answers Devendra's shame in two words. That is the book's whole food argument without a lecture.
- `b0156`: The most CT-sounding Foundations block: it turns the reader's skepticism into the reader's own premortem.
- `b0103`: The clearest statement of the book's practical program: 'Do only the first and you get impressive machines behind a price somebody can't pay.'
- `b0111`: 'You shouldn't have to be liked to be fed.' A tattoo line that earns its place.
- `b0115`: 'Good.' followed by 'Using hunger to fill jobs is exactly the problem this book is trying to end.' Heat aimed at a system, not at the reader.
- `b0182`: 'because the catastrophe it prevented never shows up to testify.' The precedent's only line a reader will remember.
- `b0184`: Practice item 1, the household Y2K, is concrete, timed, and sized for one weekend: the Field Manual working as designed.


## Chapter 19: The Ladder: How Money Becomes Optional

**Verdict.** The best public-stakes scene in the back half of the book is bolted to a Foundations section that keeps apologizing for itself. The chapter is titled 'How Money Becomes Optional', and rung after rung its own text admits the proofs prove no such thing. The ladder isn't a ladder either: each rung 'stands on its own', Rung 8 repeats Rung 1, and Rung 9 is a reassurance, not a step. This is also the chapter How to Use sends short-route newcomers to straight from the Introduction, and it opens on six co-op names they have never met, then reads like a county grant application with three f-bombs in front of it.

**Publishable as is?** No. The scene (b0074 to b0094) is nearly there, except b0093, which explains the scene and calls a lesson 'for the first time' that Chapters 12 and 18 already taught. The Foundations need blocks dropped before a cold reader sees them: the methods caveats (b0018, b0019, b0053), the triple-stated 'seven precedents, two holders' disclaimer, Get involved's restatement of the scene (b0106 to b0109), and the unexplained Tucson case (b0110, b0111). Several defects live inside otherwise essential blocks and can't be cut out whole, so they go back to CT: b0016 and b0027 mix action and disclaimer, b0093 mixes a good image with the moral, the 'Day Care' heading is ambiguous, Project Blue is never defined, and the short-route cold open needs a fix.

### Failure modes

- **critical**: How to Use sends every short-route reader from the Introduction straight here (sts.how-to-use.b0004: 'The Introduction, Chapter 19, and Appendix C'), and three of the five 'Start with' rows include Chapter 19. The chapter opens with roughly 900 words from the deepest-continuity scene in the book: a roll call of co-op names, a route funded 'through June', 'Walt's dad', Devendra's children. The most-read chapter is the one least able to stand alone, and the plan the reader came for doesn't begin until after the scene and a rant. (b0074, b0075, b0080, b0086, b0090) Quote (Narrator (third-person Elijah scene)): "Curtis in his route jacket, Reuben with an accordion folder under one arm, the kitchen coordinator with a banker's box of receipts, Devendra with a child asleep against his shoulder and the other one counting ceiling tiles."
- **critical**: Every 'Proof it's possible' label is undercut by its own paragraph. Rung 2's proof says neither example did the rung. Rung 6's proof is an income-tested program (full lawyers only at or below 200 percent of the poverty guidelines), offered for a rung whose What line reads 'Take money tests out of essentials.' Rung 7's proof is an unenacted UK costing. Rung 8's proof says the records don't yield the count. A hostile reviewer only has to quote the book against itself. (b0006, b0013, b0022, b0042, b0043, b0048, b0053) Quote (CT, author voice): "This is a precedent for public legal help, not proof that income tests have already disappeared."
- **critical**: Overpromise and clock mismatch. The title and b0098 promise a literal plan to make money optional. What arrives is food-waste counts, school lunches, land trusts, and tenant lawyers. The book's own forecast is AGI by Thanksgiving 2027 (sts.introduction.b0088), but the chapter's precedents run on decades: rural electrification took 'twenty to twenty-five years', the NHS ran 1942 to 1948, and the scene's win is a staff study 'for the next budget cycle'. A singularity reader will ask what the ladder does if the forecast is right, and the chapter never says. (b0001, b0098, b0037, b0066, b0088) Quote (CT, author voice): "a literal, step-by-step plan to convert society over to the way it's supposed to be"
- **major**: The ladder metaphor collapses under its own text. Rungs don't depend on each other ('Each rung stands on its own'). Rung 7 is the entire destination (food, shelter, care, transport, information). Rung 8 re-runs Rung 1 and says so. Rung 9 is a promise about markets, not an action anyone takes. The images also tangle: rungs, a floor that rises, a market 'above the floor', a ticket, a door, a scoreboard. (b0062, b0010, b0047, b0050, b0052, b0053, b0056) Quote (CT, author voice): "Each rung stands on its own."
- **major**: The scene announces its lesson, which breaks the voice guide's narrative rule ('If a narrative passage ends by explaining what it meant, cut the explanation'). It also claims a first. Elijah learned exactly this in Chapter 12 (sts.chapter12.b0157: 'The dashboard had been right about the timeline and the pattern, and none of it closed the deal.') and again one chapter earlier in Chapter 18's names column (sts.chapter18.b0144). The Rosa caption does the same thing to her testimony. (b0093, b0102) Quote (Narrator (third-person Elijah scene)): "understood for the first time that the model had never been the argument. It was the receipt you handed over after someone else had made it."
- **major**: Register whiplash with no landing. The reader goes from CT's rage (b0096, b0097) to a methods section within about 300 words. The Field Manual register is supposed to hand the reader a wrench. Here it hands them a data-quality footnote written for a fact-checker. (b0096, b0097, b0016, b0018, b0019) Quote (CT, author voice): "A monthly update can report progress without pretending an annual estimate was measured again."
- **major**: Get involved re-teaches the scene as a pamphlet. The scene already showed the three-minute limit and the red light, Reuben's one costed page filed with the clerk, the study, and the due date written on the tab. The how-to then restates each step. Show then tell is explaining the joke, and in a 50,000-word cut it's dead weight. (b0107, b0108, b0109, b0076, b0078, b0094) Quote (CT, author voice): "Find the next meeting agenda. Check the public-comment rules and speaking limit. Write one request that fits the time: what you want, who it serves, and what the next step would cost."
- **major**: The Tucson example is undefined, off-topic, and a partial loss. 'Project Blue' is never identified, so a newcomer can't tell what was fought or why. It has nothing to do with food, and the follow-up block concedes the project went ahead outside city limits. The motivating example in an action section is a story about the limits of action. (b0110, b0111, b0112) Quote (CT, author voice): "On August 6, 2025, its mayor and council ended negotiations over Project Blue's proposed city annexation and development agreement."
- **major**: The chapter ends four times. b0062 lands ('A town that only ever gets to Rung 3 has still fed its people.'), then Get involved ends on Douglass and b0114, then a month-list, then P-23's practice tells the reader to go to a meeting and speak on the record, which is the third version of that instruction. (b0062, b0114, b0064, b0073) Quote (CT, author voice): "Make one. Then follow it through."
- **major**: Repetition. The 'seven have precedents, two hold it together' disclaimer appears in b0006, b0013, the b0002 alt text, and the b0003 caption. The Bevan sentence is the epigraph (b0004) and is paraphrased again in b0069. The 'decide on the record, then build the institution that makes the decision real' formula appears in b0013 and b0070, and 'on the record' appears again in b0073. Rural electrification is told twice, by Irene (b0086) and by Rung 5 (b0037). (b0006, b0013, b0002, b0003, b0004, b0069, b0070, b0073, b0037, b0086) Quote (CT, author voice paraphrasing Aneurin Bevan (real person)): "Four years later Bevan put the principle in one sentence: no society can legitimately call itself civilized if a sick person is denied medical aid because of lack of means."
- **minor**: Profanity budget blown in one chapter: 'fucking' three times (b0096, b0097, b0105) against the voice guide's 'a handful of times in 85,000 words'. b0105 was added in v0.11.0 and sits between two bland civics paragraphs, so the third one reads as bolted on rather than earned. (b0096, b0097, b0105) Quote (CT, author voice): "Take fucking action."
- **minor**: Nine stages versus nine rungs. In a chapter built on nine numbered rungs, a reference to 'the nine stages' and 'Stage 5' (from Chapter 2) is a guaranteed mix-up for anyone who skipped Part I. (b0061) Quote (CT, author voice): "The resistance described in Stage 5 can be real even if the nine stages never unfold in order."
- **minor**: Strawman. The county analyst's realistic objection (no ongoing obligations) is capped with an absolute nobody in local government would say on the record, so Irene can knock it down. Skeptical readers can smell a rigged exchange. (b0084) Quote (County analyst (fictional character)): ""We simply can't afford to make food free," he said. "Nobody can.""
- **minor**: The anchor cast is missing. Marta (the protocol's 'anchor'), Priya, and Denny aren't at the one public-stakes scene of Part III, and nothing explains why. The kitchen coordinator, who carries the food program across Chapters 7, 18, and 19, still has no name. (b0074, b0089)
- **minor**: Stale-fact risk. The SB 1383 target is 'by 2025' in a book dated 2026, with no word on whether it was met. 'Counts food insecurity every year' needs checking against USDA's September 2025 announcement that it was ending the annual Household Food Security report. A hostile fact-checker will look at both. (b0017) Quote (CT, author voice): "Nationally, the Department of Agriculture counts food insecurity every year"
- **minor**: Rung 4's 'proof' cites a land trust that lost its land, which hands the critic the counterexample. (b0032) Quote (CT, author voice): "recounts New Communities in Georgia, which lost its original land and later bought a new property."

### What readers would complain about

- *newcomer on the short route*: "The book told me to read the Introduction and then Chapter 19. Chapter 19 dropped me into a county meeting with Curtis, Reuben, 'the kitchen coordinator', Devendra, Irene Calder, Rosa, and somebody's dad named Walt. I didn't know who any of them were, what 'the route' was, or why I should care that it was funded through June."
- *singularity regular*: "He says AGI by Thanksgiving 2027. His plan for that is to count food waste in a spreadsheet and win a county 'study' for the next budget cycle. Rural electrification took twenty-five years and the NHS took six. The machines will be finished before Rung 3."
- *skeptic economist*: "Every section headed 'Proof it's possible' ends by telling me it isn't proof. Rung 6's 'proof' is a means-tested program in a rung about getting rid of means tests. And 'who pays?' gets exactly one bullet: the fights will be real."
- *conservative or libertarian reader*: "'State-sanctioned violence', 'Greed is a sickness', a socialist health minister in the epigraph, 'from charity to entitlement', and then he swears this isn't a revolution."
- *fiction reader*: "Elijah overbuilds something, a working person corrects him, he learns. Renders, dashboard, sensor map, premortem model, and now fourteen slides. Then the narrator tells me he understood it 'for the first time'."
- *community organizer*: "The scene showed me how public comment works better than the how-to that follows it. And the real-world example it picks is a fight Tucson residents mostly lost, over a 'Project Blue' the book never explains."
- *hostile podcast host*: "He's 'so fucking pissed off', and two pages later he's explaining that you can't subtract households from pounds of food. Pick a register, man."
- *skimmer*: "I saw 'The Day Care Stopped Costing Money' and assumed this part was about childcare. It's about the NHS."
- *healthcare skeptic*: "He holds up the NHS as the model for making needs free and says nothing about what it costs or how it's doing now. Selective precedent."

### Weak writing

- `b0097` "People go hungry and suffer because they *have* to, not because there isn't enough." : It's ambiguous and reads as if hunger were their obligation, because the 'the rules make them' meaning never reaches the page. It's also a not-X-but-Y construction.
- `b0097` "Greed is a sickness, and too many of the decisions that shape our lives serve private property first, enforced by state-sanctioned violence." : It denounces where it should diagnose: a cliché diagnosis plus activist jargon ('state-sanctioned violence'), which the voice guide warns against ('Diagnosis beats denunciation'). The very next sentence, about the badge and the gun, makes the same point concretely and better.
- `b0098` "a literal, step-by-step plan to convert society over to the way it's supposed to be" : It overclaims, 'convert ... over to' is padding, and 'supposed to be' assumes the conclusion. Three paragraphs later the chapter admits none of it is proven.
- `b0013` "This isn't a revolution, and it doesn't wait for one. It doesn't need a single leader, a new constitution, or the permission of the people who profit from the way things are now." : It opens with a denial, then runs a list of three for rhythm, inside a 115-word block that also restates the disclaimer.
- `b0013` "It needs people who live somewhere to decide, out loud and on the record, that everyone who lives there gets what they need to survive, and then to build the institutions that make the decision real." : 'People who live somewhere' is vague filler, and the sentence reappears almost word for word in b0070.
- `b0010` "Make survival a public floor instead of a private purchase, one rung at a time, starting with food, and let automation keep raising the floor." : The metaphors collide: a ladder whose rungs raise a floor. It's also built on 'instead of'. This is the thesis line, and it can't hold a single image.
- `b0016` "Keep their units and reporting periods visible. You can't subtract households from pounds of food; you can investigate how much suitable food could reach those households, when, and at what cost." : Academic drift. It's a methods note answering a critic the reader never heard, and it deflates the most punchable rung, 'count what's wasted and who's hungry'.
- `b0022` "Neither example established this rung's universal free meal. They show parts of the arrangement already working." : A stack of abstract nouns ('arrangement', 'parts') and a hedge that empties the bold 'Proof' label above it.
- `b0027` "A public kitchen open to everyone uses an established capability. Extending that promise still takes public authority, a funded operating plan, suitable facilities, and people to run it." : The strongest real precedent in the chapter ends on a four-item list of obstacles written in grant-application nouns.
- `b0048` "That was a UK proposal using its period's assumptions, not the price of meeting every need everywhere." : It's a disclaimer posing as prose, and a 'not X' construction whose only job is to shrink the claim.
- `b0026` "Move the meal from charity to entitlement" : 'Entitlement' is a partisan trigger word in US usage, and it loses half the room in one noun.
- `b0056` "Restaurants, gadgets, art sold for a fortune, a billion dollars if someone wants to build one." : Garbled. 'Build' a billion dollars? The list loses its grammar at the punchline.
- `b0058` "Count maintenance and the remaining human work, then decide how the gain reaches people." : An accounting hedge wedged between the payoff ('Not abolished. Optional.') and the closing line about the machines.
- `b0060` "Honesty about the obstacles is part of the plan." : Throat-clearing that congratulates itself.
- `b0104` "Talk to your government. Form a coalition with your peers. Get together with local business owners and petition your city council. Write a book." : A generic civics list, and 'Write a book' lands as an accidental joke in a book.
- `b0106` "Posting can recruit neighbors, share records, and get a proposal into people's hands." : A defensive aside in committee voice, built on a list of three. It reads like EDITION-NOTES' 'recognizes useful online organizing' turned into a sentence.
- `b0108` "A speech starts a conversation; the follow-up keeps the request alive." : A fortune-cookie balance line.
- `b0111` "Residents could influence the council's decision; they couldn't settle every decision governing the project." : Abstract, bureaucratic, and conceding. 'Decision' appears twice.
- `b0112` "Both require people to make decisions together." : Empty. It says nothing the reader couldn't have assumed.
- `b0069` "Some people had insurance or public provision before it; access hadn't been universal. The change was who could claim care and how the work was organized and paid for." : Academic hedging buried inside the chapter's best historical line ('No new medical discovery arrived that morning to make the principle possible. A new institution did.').
- `b0093` "understood for the first time that the model had never been the argument. It was the receipt you handed over after someone else had made it." : Explains the scene, and 'for the first time' is false within the book's own arc.
- `b0102` "A funded route means households can plan beyond the next delivery." : The caption restates the lesson Rosa just delivered in her own words, which is explaining the joke.
- `b0086` ""My father-in-law ran that same line," she said. "Walt's dad. Nineteen thirty-nine." : Irene's line (fictional character) is ambiguous on first read. Did he say 'that same line', or run into it, or string a power line? The strongest speech in the chapter stumbles on its first clause.
- `b0002` "France’s precedent concerns surplus recovery rules; land trusts use long-term leases." : The figure alt text carries fact-check notes, so screen-reader users get a disclaimer instead of a description.

### Not in your voice

- `b0016` "Keep their units and reporting periods visible. You can't subtract households from pounds of food; you can investigate how much suitable food could reach those households, when, and at what cost." : CT's 2024 draft swings big and never qualifies ('we’re a LOT closer than you think'). This is a data-methods appendix voice he never writes in.
- `b0019` "A monthly update can report progress without pretending an annual estimate was measured again." : Written for a statistician reviewer, not for a newcomer. Nothing in the draft sounds remotely like it.
- `b0013` "Those precedents have limits; none proves the whole plan has worked." : CT's draft uses not-X-but-Y to expand a claim ('We're not just building a tool; we're potentially crafting a new form of life.'). Here the negation only shrinks the claim, and that defensive pattern runs all through the Foundations.
- `b0022` "Neither example established this rung's universal free meal." : Legalistic register ('established', 'this rung's universal free meal'). The draft talks the way a person talks across a table.
- `b0043` "This is a precedent for public legal help, not proof that income tests have already disappeared." : Fact-check scar tissue. The draft's anger ('Capitalism fights to the last breath and takes a lot of people with it') doesn't stop to file disclaimers.
- `b0106` "Posting can recruit neighbors, share records, and get a proposal into people's hands." : Committee prose added in v0.11.0. CT's draft would have said it in one blunt line or not at all.
- `b0110` "On August 6, 2025, its mayor and council ended negotiations over Project Blue's proposed city annexation and development agreement." : A recitation from the civic record, with no image, joke, or stake. CT's draft always grounds an abstraction in a person ('Your mom doesn't call you for tech help anymore; she's got Einstein on speed dial.').
- `b0069` "Some people had insurance or public provision before it; access hadn't been universal. The change was who could claim care and how the work was organized and paid for." : A historian's hedge. The draft's register is confident and conversational, not peer-reviewed.
- `b0108` "A speech starts a conversation; the follow-up keeps the request alive." : A tidy civics-pamphlet aphorism. CT's own hammer lines are rougher and funnier ('Kidding. Mostly.').

### Where a newcomer gets lost

- `b0074`: Six co-op people named in one sentence (plus Elijah), with no reintroduction and no role tags beyond props.
- `b0075`: 'The route', what it delivers, and to whom are never stated in this chapter. The scene assumes Chapters 7 and 18.
- `b0086`: 'Walt's dad': Walt Calder is from Chapter 12. A newcomer can't place Irene or why her land matters.
- `b0014`: 'Adequate Level of Care' is a bold coined term borrowed from Chapter 18. It gets a one-line gloss, but the chapter keeps using it as a metric (b0050, b0052) as if it were defined.
- `b0061`: 'Stage 5' and 'the nine stages' assume Chapter 2, and they collide with the nine rungs.
- `b0061`: 'Capture' is used in its regulatory-capture sense without explanation.
- `b0110`: Project Blue is never identified. The reader can't tell what it was or why residents opposed it.
- `b0022`: 'the British Restaurants of Chapter 11' and the Chapter 14 French law are pointers, not explanations.
- `b0032`: 'Chapter 12 shows one being born', 'Chapter 15 shows the machinery', and New Communities are all deferred to other chapters.
- `b0053`: 'Chapter 18's "Put the failed meal on the board"' is a cross-reference a skipper can't resolve.
- `b0064`: 'pick one machine from Chapter 17' sends the short-route reader to a chapter they were told to skip.

### Skim problems

- '## The Foundations' (b0008) sits directly on top of '## Why I wrote this chapter' (b0095), so it's an empty section heading.
- 'The plan, in one sentence' (b0009) is followed by 325 words over five blocks, and the one sentence is buried under the caveats that follow it.
- In every rung that has a Proof paragraph, Proof is the longest block (b0017 102w, b0027 99w, b0037 103w, b0043 91w, b0048 85w), and each ends in a disclaimer. A skimmer gets citations and caveats and misses the action.
- The What/Proof/Who/How template breaks at the top of the ladder: Rung 8 has no Who, and Rung 9 has no Proof, Who, or How.
- 'Get involved' runs 417 words with no labels, bullets, or bold. It's the longest Foundations section and the least scannable.
- Three action lists stack at the end: Get involved, 'What you can do this month' (b0064), and the P-23 practice (b0073).
- The heading 'Precedent P-23: The Day Care Stopped Costing Money' scans as childcare.
- The ladder figure (b0002), the one thing a skimmer wants, arrives about 1,450 words in, after the scene and the rant.
- The chapter title stacks colons: 'Chapter 19: The Ladder: How Money Becomes Optional'.

### Best blocks

- `b0082`: Rosa's testimony (fictional character). It's specific, unsentimental, and carries the chapter's thesis in her own mouth: 'You can't plan a week around maybe.'
- `b0086`: Irene's speech (fictional character) is history used as a weapon mid-fight, exactly as the protocol wants. 'I'd like him to say who it can't be afforded *by*' is the best line in the chapter. It makes Rung 5's REA paragraph partly redundant.
- `b0088`: An honest non-win: 'how a county said *maybe* in writing.' It keeps the book from fantasy.
- `b0092`: Rosa changes '*more*' to '*the rest*', a one-word edit that says everything about who owns the promise.
- `b0080`: Elijah's failure at the mic: the red light, the wrong slide, the popped 's'. Friction, not lecture.
- `b0074`: 'The county chambers smelled like wet coats and floor wax.' A strong sensory open, though the roll call that follows needs CT's fix.
- `b0096`: The closest thing in the chapter to CT's 2024 voice ('What the fuck is going on?'): anger aimed at the arrangement, not the reader.
- `b0097`: 'somebody with a badge and a gun has permission to come and put you out' names the enforcement mechanism concretely.
- `b0021`: 'Every unnecessary form is a door some hungry person won't walk through.' A tattoo line, in the voice guide's own terms.
- `b0027`: California universal school meals is the one precedent that actually does the rung, at state scale, for everyone who asks.
- `b0038`: 'When the market won't serve a need because the need doesn't pay, people can own the infrastructure themselves, with public credit behind them.' This is the book's thesis in one sentence.
- `b0057`: 'Not abolished. Optional.' CT's signature hammer, planted in the Introduction (sts.introduction.b0094, b0147).
- `b0062`: 'A town that only ever gets to Rung 3 has still fed its people.' The right ending for the plan.
- `b0071`: The rule answers the analyst from the scene directly: who decided, who paid, who kept the service running.
- `b0113`: Douglass (real person), 'Power concedes nothing without a demand,' with b0114 'Make one. Then follow it through.' as CT's close.
- `b0064`: Concrete, per-reader actions sized for one month. It's real Field Manual.


## Conclusion: What If It All Goes Right?

**Verdict.** A chapter titled 'What If It All Goes Right?' spends most of its first 1,100 words in the voice of a procurement memo and introduces a brand-new term (solarpunk) via a 2008 blog post. Then it hits its climax, deflates it with a 632-word Clifford Stoll precedent, and only then plays the Bear Flag coda: three endings, in the wrong order. The argument half also undercuts the book's thesis. After nineteen chapters saying the rules are the fence, it tells the reader the 'last fence' is their own mindset, and it warns against regulation one chapter after asking counties to regulate. The coda is the best prose in the file, and it stops three times to explain its own lesson.

**Publishable as is?** No. The coda (b0008 to b0019) is close, but its weakest sentences live inside essential blocks (b0125's 'That was the other thing three years had taught him', b0126's recap list and moral, b0085's last two sentences), so those fixes are CT's. For the core cut, the policy-brief blocks (b0090, b0091, b0095, b0097, b0098, b0100 to b0102) and the solarpunk detour (b0130 to b0135) can go whole. P-24 should leave the skeleton or go back to CT to move, because nothing should stand between 'So wake up' and the Bear Flag.

### Failure modes

- **critical**: Three endings, in the wrong order. The moral mandate climaxes ('So wake up. All the way up.' then 'Food first. Then keep the promise growing.'), then a horizontal rule hands the reader a 632-word history lesson about a 1995 Newsweek column and CERN licensing, with its own closer ('You'll be building.'), and only then comes the narrative bookend. Each ending drains the one before it. (b0079, b0109, b0120, b0124, b0019) Quote (CT, author voice): "The Ledger closes with a receipt. Frame it."
- **critical**: The final argument contradicts the book's thesis. The Introduction says the rules that make survival cost money 'are choices, enforced, and choices can change' (sts.introduction.b0153), and Chapter 19 says 'The obstacle is the rule' (sts.chapter19.b0070). The last pep talk relocates the barrier inside the reader. That's the self-help industry's move, and it hands every critic of the book's politics a gift. (b0115) Quote (CT, author voice): "That voice is the last fence, and it's the only one nobody else can tear down for you. Look this thing straight in the face."
- **critical**: The title promises a vision and gets a policy memo. 'What If It All Goes Right?' is answered by five modest sentences (b0090), an immediate hedge (b0091), and then 'Choose what success means' in procurement language. The one chapter that should make a newcomer want the future reads like a grant rubric. (b0090, b0091, b0095, b0097, b0098, b0100, b0101, b0102) Quote (CT, author voice): "That work belongs in public decisions, workplaces, research programs, procurement agreements, and community projects."
- **major**: It contradicts Chapter 19. Chapter 19's plan is public rules: 'Limits on using credit reports and private risk scores', no shutoffs, right to counsel, county funding (sts.chapter19.b0042). The conclusion then warns that companies will 'regulate everyone else out'. A skeptic hears an AI-industry anti-regulation talking point and asks which side the book is on. (b0112) Quote (CT, author voice): "if we let them regulate everyone else out of the one opportunity that could change their lives"
- **major**: It contradicts itself one section apart. b0108 says to let the frightened worker be frightened. Three blocks later, b0111 puts the worker's fear in scare quotes and calls building the future 'the only intelligent thing to do', which makes everyone who disagrees unintelligent. (b0108, b0111) Quote (CT, author voice): "We aren't "destroying jobs"; we're automating the toil and the risk, and insisting the benefit reach the people who did them."
- **major**: Forecast whiplash. 'My AGI forecast may be wrong' (b0104) and 'My bet is 2027... You'll be building' (b0124) sit in the same chapter. The forecast-may-be-wrong hedge is already stated in sts.introduction.b0153, sts.introduction.b0088, sts.chapter1.b0174, and sts.appendix-c.b0041. By the conclusion it has stopped reading as candor and started reading as a tic. (b0104, b0124) Quote (CT, author voice): "My AGI forecast may be wrong."
- **major**: A new concept arrives on the last pages, carried by trivia. Solarpunk is introduced with a blog's date and a cargo ship, glossed 'for this book', and then backed by a Le Guin paraphrase that waters down the most quotable speech it could have cited. The section added in v0.11.0 to deliver hope delivers a footnote. (b0130, b0135, b0131, b0132, b0133, b0134) Quote (CT, author voice): "On April 30, 2008, a blogger writing as John-Robert proposed the word for a literary genre, taking inspiration from a cargo ship using wind to supplement its engines."
- **major**: The coda explains itself. The voice guide's rule is that 'the scene never announces its lesson', yet b0125 states what three years taught him, b0126 states the moral outright, and b0085 explains what the toast meant. The image of the future having 'addresses' (b0084) already did all of it. (b0125, b0126, b0085, b0055) Quote (Narrator (third-person Elijah scene)): "that the answer to the machine was never going to be a smarter machine."
- **major**: The coda runs on callbacks the 'wider view' route never plants. That route is Preface, Chapter 6, Chapter 12, Conclusion (sts.how-to-use.b0004), and it skips Chapter 0, where the Bear Flag, the four-dollar beer, the napkin, the telescope, the toast, and 'You're holding it.' are all set up. b0126's highlight reel (renders, optimized script, commented-out line, crust, flash) points to Chapters 9, 10, 11, 15, and 17, none of which are on that route. (b0114, b0060, b0126, b0055, b0056, b0008) Quote (Narrator (third-person Elijah scene)): "the renders, the optimized script, the commented-out line, the crust called a planet, the flash done solo"
- **major**: The Stoll gambit is the most worn move in tech boosterism. b0122's fairness to Stoll helps, but 'You know how this rhymes' plus a 1995 'Internet? Bah!' clipping is the slide every crypto and AI deck uses, and skeptics will tune out at it. Stoll's self-deprecating quote is also sourced to Wikipedia in a chapter about receipts. (b0121, b0122, b0124) Quote (CT, author voice describing Clifford Stoll (real person)): "Clifford Stoll, an astronomer who had been online for two decades, published "Why the Web Won't Be Nirvana," better remembered by its print title, "The Internet? Bah!""
- **major**: The anchor cast is missing from the ending. The three-years roll call names Devendra, Frank, Rosa, Curtis, and Reuben. Marta (the protocol's anchor), Priya, and Denny (whose arc is getting 'a stake in something that is his') get nothing in the final scene. (b0082, b0126) Quote (Narrator (third-person Elijah scene)): "Long enough for Devendra to arrive with the children, for Frank to put his name on a different bench"
- **minor**: The 'In this chapter' bullets give away the closing slogan word for word, so the moral mandate reads as a repeat when it arrives. (b0128, b0111, b0109) Quote (CT, author voice): "Success is more than labor removed from a payroll: count time returned, danger avoided, access secured, and who holds the power."
- **minor**: The narrative frame breaks. The narrative zone names a chapter number and refers to 'this book' as a document inside the scene, which jolts anyone reading the coda as fiction. (b0055, b0126) Quote (Narrator (third-person Elijah scene)): "the drive was the same drive from Chapter 6 run in reverse"
- **minor**: A 'three' tic: three years and some weeks, three realtors, three people, three careers, three years taught him, 'Three years.', the first time in three years, three men, three years early. It reads as a motif hammered past the point of effect. (b0055, b0114, b0125, b0082, b0085, b0060)

### What readers would complain about

- *bored newcomer*: "It ends three times. 'So wake up' felt like the end. Then came a history lesson about a 1995 Newsweek column and CERN software licenses. Then a short story. I skimmed to 'You're holding it.'"
- *skeptic*: "The 'Internet? Bah!' clipping. Every crypto pitch deck I've ever sat through had that exact slide."
- *singularity regular*: "He says his AGI forecast 'may be wrong', and a few pages later it's 'My bet is 2027. You'll be building.' Pick one."
- *left-wing critic*: "Nineteen chapters telling me the rules are rigged, then the big finish says the 'last fence' is my mindset. A Tony Robbins ending on a political-economy book."
- *policy reader*: "Chapter 19 asks my county to regulate credit scores and shutoffs. The conclusion warns me about companies that 'regulate everyone else out'. Which is it?"
- *newcomer on the wider-view route*: "The last scene is all callbacks: the renders, the optimized script, the commented-out line, the crust called a planet, the flash done solo, Manny, the pressure switch, the Fisher. I had no idea what any of it meant."
- *literary reader*: "The bar scene is lovely until the narrator stops to tell me what it meant. Three times."
- *reader who loved Marta*: "Marta carried the whole story, and she isn't in the ending. Neither is Denny."
- *general reader*: "'What If It All Goes Right?' And the answer is procurement agreements."
- *conservative reader*: "Opens on an epigraph from 'The Soul of Man under Socialism'. Thanks for the warning."

### Weak writing

- `b0087` "The new era doesn't ask permission, and it still has work to do." : Vague personification: an era that 'has work to do'. A caption that says nothing about the image.
- `b0090` "Useful work continues. More of its burden is carried by machines, and more of its benefit reaches people." : Passive voice and abstract nouns, the flattest possible answer to the chapter's own title question.
- `b0091` "The world remains complicated. We remain capable of selfishness, error, and cruelty. There's still maintenance to do." : A pre-emptive hedge in a list of three, delivered one paragraph into the vision.
- `b0095` "Training can be useful. So can a new business or a different occupation. None is an adequate answer on its own to the question of where a person stands while the ground moves." : A concession, a concession, then a hedge. Lecture tone.
- `b0097` "Providers are supported. Recipients can obtain what they need without employment or payment being the entrance test." : Bureaucratic passive ('Providers are supported') and policy nouns ('Recipients', 'entrance test').
- `b0098` "A finished wall, a laboratory result, or a bag of collected waste can be part of an answer." : An abstract list of three that is supposed to be concrete and isn't.
- `b0100` "Ask about time returned, danger avoided, access secured, and power over the service." : Stacked nominal phrases, and it repeats the In-this-chapter bullet.
- `b0101` "That work belongs in public decisions, workplaces, research programs, procurement agreements, and community projects." : A five-item padding list. 'Procurement agreements' in the last chapter of a book meant to change a newcomer's life.
- `b0102` "We can insist that commitments be funded and decisions challengeable." : Academic drift. 'Challengeable' is a word no one says aloud.
- `b0103` "Wanting everyone to have enough isn't a technical prediction. It's the standard we bring to the technology." : A not-X-it's-Y construction, used for symmetry rather than force.
- `b0107` "Ask for an answer that names provision and responsibility. A promise without either is still waiting to become a promise someone can use." : Abstract nouns ending in a near-tautology.
- `b0111` "We're building a better tomorrow because it's the only intelligent thing to do." : A cliché ('better tomorrow') plus contempt for anyone who disagrees.
- `b0111` "We aren't weird for using tools to solve our survival; we're human." : It answers an accusation nobody in the book made, and leaves the reader wondering who called them weird.
- `b0111` "The choice is ours: keep arguing over the scraps of a dying arrangement, or build the machinery of our own freedom." : A false binary and a stock rhetorical frame.
- `b0115` "Here's the truth the fear keeps trying to bury. Things are changing, and you can do this." : Throat-clearing into a generic pep talk.
- `b0115` "That voice is the last fence, and it's the only one nobody else can tear down for you. Look this thing straight in the face." : Self-help framing and a cliché, and it contradicts the thesis.
- `b0112` "Every capable person who wakes up is one more on the side of the light" : A Manichean cliché, and 'wakes up' is red-pill-coded for exactly the skeptics the book wants to reach.
- `b0081` "Stop waiting. Show up. Start building." : A list of three for rhythm. A poster, not a sentence.
- `b0109` "Food first. Then keep the promise growing." : A mixed metaphor (promises don't grow), and the last line of the argument goes soft at the moment it needs a hammer.
- `b0132` "For this book, the useful idea is a future where renewable energy, repairable tools, and shared decisions make daily life more secure." : An academic frame ('For this book, the useful idea is') and a triple.
- `b0133` "Ursula K. Le Guin argued that writers can help people imagine alternatives to the way they live, and that arrangements of human power can be changed." : A paraphrase that flattens a famous speech into mush. 'Arrangements of human power' is the least quotable way to cite one of the most quotable writers alive in 2014.
- `b0116` "Access depended on decisions about ownership and permission, made by identifiable people and an institution." : Academic drift, with an awkward hedge ('identifiable people and an institution').
- `b0117` "That didn't give everyone a computer or a connection. It removed a particular barrier to building on particular software." : The qualification eats the point, and 'particular' appears twice.
- `b0124` "You're living through this decade's version of Stoll's essay, published weekly, about AI, by smart people, containing accurate complaints." : A comma pile-up of modifiers with no rhythm.
- `b0080` "because that discomfort is a compass pointing at your beachfront." : A mixed metaphor (a compass pointing at beachfront), built on jargon from Chapter 14.
- `b0114` "because history doesn't repeat but it does like a good seat" : A riff on the Twain cliché the book already uses in sts.introduction.b0129 and echoes in b0124. Too cute by half.
- `b0125` "For how people were living through it, he had learned to ask someone, and wait for an answer." : Inverted, clunky syntax, and it explains the lesson.
- `b0085` "There was no forecast in it this time. Just relief at the ordinary, unfinished work waiting for him at home." : Explains the toast the reader just watched land.
- `b0085` "to the whole grinding hopeful improbable room" : Stacked adjectives with no commas, a workshop flourish.
- `b0055` "He didn't stop. You don't need to stop for the things you're carrying with you." : An aphorism that explains the image of the madrones instead of trusting it.
- `b0056` "His mother called at 9:40 Sunday, four days before, right on schedule" : Unclear time anchor: 'four days before' what? The reader has to back-solve from 'Thursday' in the previous block.
- `b0050` "*The proofs. Ten copies, perfect-bound. The book, finally.*" : The caption repeats b0008's 'ten copies, perfect-bound' word for word.

### Not in your voice

- `b0097` "Providers are supported. Recipients can obtain what they need without employment or payment being the entrance test." : CT's draft says 'If humanity could get its collective head out of its collective ass, we could be living in the Star Trek future or the Jetsons.' This is a white paper.
- `b0101` "That work belongs in public decisions, workplaces, research programs, procurement agreements, and community projects." : Institutional list-making. The draft never names a process when it can name a person.
- `b0102` "We can insist that commitments be funded and decisions challengeable." : Uncontracted, nominal, lecture register. This is the 'academic drift' the voice guide names as the actual defect.
- `b0131` "On April 30, 2008, a blogger writing as John-Robert proposed the word for a literary genre, taking inspiration from a cargo ship using wind to supplement its engines." : An encyclopedia entry. CT's draft introduces ideas by imagining them ('Imagine a world where Einstein is your entry-level tech support'), not by dating their coinage.
- `b0116` "Access depended on decisions about ownership and permission, made by identifiable people and an institution." : Licensing-history prose with no heat. Nothing in the 2024 draft resembles it.
- `b0081` "Stop waiting. Show up. Start building." : EDITION-NOTES records that this moral call replaced CT's dictated passage in v0.11.0 ('The original dictated passage remains available in the earlier worktree's editorial note'). The replacement is a generic three-beat poster line, so this block is the clearest place where CT's own words were swapped out.
- `b0115` "That voice is the last fence, and it's the only one nobody else can tear down for you." : Life-coach register. CT's draft treats fear as intelligence, not as an inner fence: 'that fear you feel is good. It means you're paying attention.'
- `b0104` "My AGI forecast may be wrong." : CT's draft is proudly unhedged ('we’re a LOT closer than you think'). Here the hedge is repeated until it reads as nervousness.
- `b0085` "to the whole grinding hopeful improbable room" : The narrative register is allowed its own voice, but this adjective pile is MFA polish. CT's draft gets its warmth from jokes and plain talk ('grab your favorite drink and your favorite tinfoil hat'), not from lyric stacks.

### Where a newcomer gets lost

- `b0120`: 'The Ledger' (without 'Precedent') is never defined in this chapter.
- `b0124`: 'My bet is 2027' doesn't say a bet on what. 'twenty-four entries' assumes a linear read of every chapter.
- `b0080`: 'beachfront' is Chapter 14 jargon (the beachfront audit), used here with no gloss.
- `b0126`: Five callbacks in one clause (renders, optimized script, commented-out line, crust, flash), each from a different chapter.
- `b0055`: 'Curtis had fixed it for two dollars', 'Chapter 6', and the grandmother's madrones all assume Chapters 0, 6, and 9.
- `b0056`: Manny and the pressure switch are a Chapter 3 setup.
- `b0008`: 'the Fisher's replacement firebrick' depends on the grandmother's stove from Chapters 5 and 13.
- `b0114`: The corner table, the realtors, the CSS tutorial, and the 40 percent battery are all Chapter 0, which the wider-view route skips.
- `b0082`: Devendra, Frank's 'different bench', and Rosa's '*prepared, less spicy*' are unexplained for anyone who skipped Chapters 7, 14, 16, and 18.
- `b0018`: 'check the cover of this one' only works if the reader knows the working title from Chapter 0 and b0008. Otherwise it's a puzzle saved only by the next line.

### Skim problems

- The title 'CONCLUSION: What If It All Goes Right?' is in all caps, inconsistent with 'Chapter 19:' styling.
- The section headings are abstract imperatives ('Choose what success means', 'Take the next decision seriously') that tell a skimmer nothing about their content.
- The 'In this chapter' bullets (b0128) give away the closing slogan, so the moral mandate reads as a repeat.
- The payoff section, 'The moral mandate' (427 words), sits in the middle. A skimmer jumping to the end lands on P-24's 632 words of Stoll and CERN before reaching the scene.
- The P-24 practice list (b0080) is 159 words in three dense items, and the last asks the reader to 'go back through the practices behind you', which a skimmer never did.
- The proofs caption (b0050) duplicates b0008, so the image adds no information.

### Best blocks

- `b0084`: 'Now it had addresses.' The book's whole arc in five words, with no explanation needed.
- `b0056`: The mother's call (fictional character): 'For three years, honey. It reads better than it sounded.' plus the pressure-switch payoff. Warm, specific, earned.
- `b0008`: The proofs mailed flat to his mother with '*This is what I was trying to say on the phone.*' pays off the failed Chapter 3 call.
- `b0060`: 'tipped like a man who used to get tipped' and 'three years early and one page short' are the best sentences in the coda.
- `b0114`: 'The Bear Flag hadn't changed, which was the eeriest thing it could have done.' The five-dollar beer quietly pays off Chapter 0's four-dollar one.
- `b0085`: 'Congratulations' reclaimed. It pays off Chapter 0's hollow toast and, whether intended or not, CT's own 2024 opening line ('So the robots have taken over. Congratulations! You've lost.'). It must survive, even though its last two sentences should go.
- `b0082`: 'Nobody had mistaken a stack of forms for dinner.' It honestly says the route still needs money.
- `b0083`: The route is covered, and he turns the screen down. Understatement that does the work b0125 and b0126 try to do with explanation.
- `b0018`: Sets up the bookend. Without it, b0019 has nothing to stand on.
- `b0019`: 'You're holding it.' The verbatim echo of Chapter 0's close. The book's single best structural payoff.
- `b0078`: The closest thing in the chapter to CT's raw voice: 'by "number go up," by a quarterly line on somebody else's chart.' The best argument-register paragraph here.
- `b0079`: 'So wake up. All the way up.' A short hammer after a long run, the move the voice guide names as the register's defining one.
- `b0094`: 'don't answer only with a demonstration of the machine' quietly indicts Elijah's Bear Flag demo without saying so.
- `b0108`: 'They don't owe the future enthusiasm. We owe one another a future that gives us a reason for it.' The humane line the moral mandate then undercuts.
- `b0122`: Stoll treated fairly: 'He was describing the worst version of the technology and mistaking it for the final one.' If any of P-24 survives, it's this.


## Appendix A: Questions for a Food Baseline

**Verdict.** A council packet bolted onto a book that otherwise talks to one person. 458 words of questions, caveats and a legal disclaimer, with no number, no example, no voice and nothing a reader can do this week. It restates the food proposal that Appendix C, the Glossary and Chapter 19 already state. Its only structural reason to exist is that sts.chapter19.b0028 points at it.

**Publishable as is?** No. Not one sentence is in CT's voice. It fails the book's own Field Manual rule (VOICE-GUIDE: a thing to do this week, a number, or a named part). Its closing disclaimers make the author sound afraid of his own proposal. For the core cut, keep at most b0056 and b0054, and only if sts.chapter19.b0028 survives. Otherwise drop the whole appendix and that pointer block with it.

### Failure modes

- **critical**: Wrong register for the entire appendix. No first person, no 'you', no image, no joke, no number, no named tool. It reads as a grant form or a committee handout, not as the Argument or the Field Manual. (sts.appendix-a.b0051, sts.appendix-a.b0053, sts.appendix-a.b0058, sts.appendix-a.b0060, sts.appendix-a.b0061) Quote (CT author voice): "The purpose of the brief is to make the next conversation concrete enough to improve the proposal."
- **major**: All questions and no answers. Nine numbered prompts hide about twenty-two sub-questions, and the appendix never shows one answered, one filled-in trial page, or one town that did it. The reader gets homework. (sts.appendix-a.b0056, sts.appendix-a.b0058) Quote (CT author voice): "Describe a small trial in one page: the need, partners, task, recipient route, available resources, duration, comparison, and review date."
- **major**: The liability disclaimers make the author sound afraid of his own proposal, and they hand a hostile reader an easy clip. (sts.appendix-a.b0051, sts.appendix-a.b0062) Quote (CT author voice): "It grants no tax exemption, power to terminate a contract, suspension of other rules, or immunity from a claim."
- **major**: Redundant. The proposal in b0053 and b0054 appears again in Appendix C (b0027, b0029), the Glossary (appendix-f b0018, b0019) and Chapter 19. A full-book reader meets the same paragraph four times. (sts.appendix-a.b0052, sts.appendix-a.b0053, sts.appendix-a.b0054) Quote (CT author voice): "Make adequate food available regardless of employment or ability to pay."
- **minor**: Cut dependency. sts.chapter19.b0028 ends by sending the reader here. If A is dropped and that block is kept, the pointer dangles, and a block-level cut can't patch the sentence. (sts.appendix-a.b0050) Quote (CT author voice): "Appendix A has the questions to bring."

### What readers would complain about

- *community organizer / food pantry volunteer*: "I'm exactly who this page is for. It gave me nine questions my pantry has been asking for twenty years, and not one answer, one example, or one town that pulled it off."
- *hostile podcast host*: "The appendix on feeding everyone ends by promising it 'grants no tax exemption, power to terminate a contract, suspension of other rules, or immunity from a claim.' Even the author's food plan has lawyered up."
- *bored newcomer*: "Reads like a city council packet. I read the bold words and closed the book."
- *skeptic*: "'Use automation where it demonstrably helps.' Okay, where? The appendix never names one place it did."

### Weak writing

- `sts.appendix-a.b0053` "Keep the person receiving food at the center of the account." : Abstract. 'Account' is ambiguous (a ledger or a story?), and the sentence gives the reader nothing to do.
- `sts.appendix-a.b0053` "Use automation where it demonstrably helps supply that food, while preserving effective existing methods and services." : Hedge words ('demonstrably', 'effective') do the job an example should do. Nothing here is demonstrated.
- `sts.appendix-a.b0056` "Who gains or loses income, time, discretion, or bargaining power?" : Stacked abstract nouns. The list is there for coverage, not meaning.
- `sts.appendix-a.b0056` "Who covers equipment, operating costs, maintenance, remaining labor, storage, and distribution? Which commitments are real, and how long do they last?" : A six-item noun pile followed by a vague follow-up. This is grant-form diction.
- `sts.appendix-a.b0058` "State what has not yet been arranged." : Bureaucratic imperative with uncontracted 'has not'. The VOICE-GUIDE treats uncontracted prose as academic drift.
- `sts.appendix-a.b0060` "It doesn't eliminate the need for judgment, public participation, and decisions people can challenge." : A list of three for rhythm, in a lecturing tone, and it says nothing a reader didn't assume.
- `sts.appendix-a.b0051` "This is a discussion brief, not an ordinance or a claim of legal authority." : A 'not X' disclaimer as the opening line. Throat-clearing before any content.
- `sts.appendix-a.b0061` "The purpose of the brief is to make the next conversation concrete enough to improve the proposal." : Padding that ends the appendix on a whimper. It describes the document instead of doing anything.

### Not in your voice

- `sts.appendix-a.b0051` "Use it with affected residents, workers, food providers, relevant specialists, and public institutions." : CT's 2024 draft opens with a punch: 'So the robots have taken over. Congratulations! You've lost.' This opens with a stakeholder list. CT never writes 'relevant specialists'.
- `sts.appendix-a.b0062` "Don't present this page as a universal legal template." : The draft never hedges. It says we're 'a LOT closer than you think'. This is a lawyer's note, the opposite of CT's confident, slightly reckless voice.
- `sts.appendix-a.b0054` "The proposal does not require abolishing restaurants, paid employment, or food sold above the baseline." : Uncontracted, third-person 'the proposal'. CT's draft talks in contractions and second person ('You're afraid. I get it.').
- `sts.appendix-a.b0056` "What constitutes suitable and sufficient provision across dietary needs, seasons, and living circumstances?" : 'Constitutes', 'provision' and 'living circumstances' are policy-paper diction. The draft's diction is spoken ('If humanity could get its collective head out of its collective ass').

### Where a newcomer gets lost

- `sts.appendix-a.b0053`: It never says why food, why now, or what AI has to do with it. The only technology words are 'automation' and 'machine'. A reader who arrives here from Chapter 19 or How to Use has no frame.
- `sts.appendix-a.b0058`: 'Recipient route' and 'comparison' are grant-writing jargon, used without explanation.
- `sts.appendix-a.b0056`: The Authority question assumes the reader knows which 'existing permissions, responsibilities, and public processes' apply to a food service. A newcomer doesn't, and nothing points them anywhere.

### Skim problems

- The 209-word list (b0056) is nine bolded single nouns (Need, Food, Work, Access...), each hiding two or three sub-questions. A skimmer collects nine labels and no content.
- There is no headline answer or number anywhere, so a skimmer has nothing to carry away.
- The last three blocks (b0062, b0060, b0061) are all caveats, so the last thing a skimmer sees is a disclaimer.

### Best blocks

- `sts.appendix-a.b0056`: The Access question ('How does someone request or receive food without money, a smartphone, transport, or proof of employment?') is the sharpest test of the book's whole proposal. Continuity ('What happens to the meal when a machine, crop, vehicle, funding source, or organization fails?') is the second sharpest. If anything in A survives, it is this block.
- `sts.appendix-a.b0054`: It scopes the proposal: restaurants and paid work stay, and the baseline has to reach people who can't prepare or store ingredients. That second clause is the non-obvious point a newcomer would miss.


## Appendix C: Executive Reference Guide

**Verdict.** This is the 'short route' (sts.how-to-use.b0004 sends newcomers to the Introduction, Chapter 19 and Appendix C), so it is the page most people will judge the book by. It opens on a policy sentence with no story, no stakes and no voice. The definition and the objections list are the best compression in the back matter.
